# Decisão da Comissão, de 22/07/2026 relativa à compatibilidade com o mercado comum de uma operação de concentração (Processo no COMP/M.12278 - PARAMOUNT SKYDANCE / WARNER BROS DISCOVERY) com base no Regulamento (CE) n. 139/2004 do Conselho (Apenas faz fé o texto em língua inglesa)

Subject:   Case M.12278 – PARAMOUNT SKYDANCE / WARNER BROS
           DISCOVERY
           Commission decision pursuant to Article 6(1)(b) of Council Regulation
           No 139/2004 (1) and Article 57 of the Agreement on the European Economic
           Area (2)



Dear Sir or Madam,


(1)    On 2 June 2026, the European Commission received notification of a proposed
       concentration pursuant to Article 4 of the Merger Regulation by which Paramount
       Skydance Corporation (‘Paramount’ or the ‘Notifying Party’, United States) intends to
       acquire sole control of Warner Bros. Discovery (‘Warner’, United States) (the
       ‘Transaction’). (3) Paramount and Warner are designated hereinafter as the ‘Parties’.




(1)   OJ L 24, 29.1.2004, p. 1 (the ‘Merger Regulation’). With effect from 1 December 2009, the Treaty on the
      Functioning of the European Union (‘TFEU’) has introduced certain changes, such as the replacement of
      ‘Community’ by ‘Union’ and ‘common market’ by ‘internal market’. The terminology of the TFEU will be
      used throughout this decision.
(2)   OJ L 1, 3.1.1994, p. 3 (the ‘EEA Agreement’).
(3)   OJ C 3264, 17.6.2026.
1.    THE PARTIES
(2)   Paramount is a US publicly listed global media, streaming and entertainment company
      that creates content and experiences for audiences worldwide, including films, television
      series, and animated content. Paramount’s portfolio of brands and production assets
      include, among others, Paramount Pictures, Paramount Television, CBS, CBS News,
      CBS Sports, Nickelodeon, MTV, BET, Comedy Central, Showtime, Paramount+, Pluto
      TV and Skydance Media, LLC animation, interactive/games and sports divisions. In
      2025, Paramount generated worldwide turnover of EUR 25.6 billion, of which EUR
      [details of the Parties’ revenues] billion in the EEA.
(3)   Warner is a US publicly listed global media, streaming and entertainment company that
      creates and distributes a diverse portfolio of content and products across television, film,
      streaming and interactive gaming. Warner’s portfolio of brands and production assets
      includes, among others, Discovery Channel, CNN, DC Studios, New Line Cinema, TNT
      Sports, Eurosport, Max, HBO, Warner Bros. Motion Picture Group, Warner Bros.
      Television Group, and Warner Bros. Games. In 2025, Warner generated worldwide
      turnover of EUR 33 billion, of which EUR [details of the Parties’ revenues] billion in the
      EEA.


2.    THE OPERATION AND CONCENTRATION
(4)   On 27 February 2026, Paramount and Warner executed a merger agreement pursuant to
      which Paramount will acquire all the shares in Warner for a cash consideration of
      approximately USD 110 billion (at a price of USD 31 per share). If the Transaction has
      not closed by 30 September 2026, Warner’s shareholders will receive a USD 0.25 per
      share ‘ticking fee’ for each quarter (measured daily) until closing.
(5)   As a result of the Transaction, Paramount will acquire sole control over Warner. The
      Transaction constitutes a concentration within the meaning of Article 3(1)(b) Merger
      Regulation.


3.    UNION DIMENSION
(6)   The undertakings concerned have a combined aggregate worldwide turnover of more
      than EUR 5 000 million (Paramount: EUR 25.6 billion; Warner: EUR 33 billion). Each
      of them has an EU-wide turnover in excess of EUR 250 million (Paramount: EUR [details
      of the Parties’ revenues] billion; Warner: EUR [details of the Parties’ revenues] billion),
      and neither of them achieves more than two-thirds of its aggregate EU-wide turnover
      within the same Member State. The notified operation therefore has a Union dimension.


4.    INDUSTRY OVERVIEW
(7)   The audiovisual (‘AV’) industry has traditionally been split into: (i) the theatrical value
      chain, centred on feature films and cinema exhibition, and (ii) the AV value chain,
      focused on television and home entertainment. Content is distributed in each of the
      theatrical and AV value chains, along three different levels: the upstream, intermediate
      and downstream. Many operators are vertically integrated and active at more than one
      level.
(8)   Theatrical value chain. The upstream level encompasses the production of films
      intended for theatrical release and the licensing of those films for exploitation in cinemas,
      regardless of whether the producer distributes the film itself or licenses the distribution
      rights to a third-party distributor. The intermediate level covers the wholesale distribution
                                                  2
        of films to cinemas, carried out either by independent third-party distributors, or through
        self-distribution by film producers. The downstream level includes the theatrical
        exhibition of films by cinemas.
(9)     AV value chain. The upstream level encompasses the production of content (e.g. series,
        TV shows) and the licensing-out of such content to companies active in the intermediate
        or downstream levels. The intermediate level covers the wholesale supply of TV channels
        to retail TV distributors. The downstream level includes the retail provision of AV
        services to end-customers primarily via (i) linear TV channels, and (ii) subscription
        video-on-demand platforms (‘SVOD’).
(10)    The market for AV content constitutes the upstream level of the AV value chain,
        encompassing both the production of AV content and the licensing of distribution rights
        to wholesale suppliers of TV channels, as well as retail providers of AV services. At this
        level of the AV value chain, production studios either produce new AV content
        commissioned and tailored to the needs of other market participants, or license-out pre-
        produced AV content which is available off-the-shelf. (4)
(11)    The wholesale supply of TV channels constitutes the intermediate level between the
        upstream creation and licensing of AV content and the downstream retail distribution of
        AV content to end customers. At this level, wholesalers package AV content and
        associated broadcasting rights into linear and non-linear offerings for retail TV
        distributors. (5)
(12)    The retail provision of AV services to end users constitutes the downstream level of the
        AV value chain which follows the upstream level for the production and licensing of AV
        content and the intermediate level for wholesale supply of TV channels. Retail suppliers
        of AV content include (i) TV distributors, including Multichannel Video Programming
        Distributors (‘MVPDs’), (ii) Over The Top (‘OTT’) providers delivering AV content
        online, and (iii) to a limited extent, in physical formats. (6)
(13)    The AV value chain has been undergoing a significant structural change over the last
        years, as linear viewership is shifting towards non-linear services, especially SVOD
        services. The changing media consumption patterns have resulted in a decline in linear
        TV viewership and a corresponding increase in non-linear consumption of AV
        content. (7)
(14)    The Parties are active on all levels of the theatrical and AV value chains except for
        theatrical exhibition (as they do not operate their own cinemas).

5.      RELEVANT MARKETS

5.1.    Introduction
(15)    The Parties’ activities result in horizontal overlaps that give rise to affected markets in
        (i) the production and licensing of films for theatrical release, (ii) the distribution of films
        for theatrical release, (iii) the production and licensing of AV content, (iv) the wholesale
        supply of TV channels, and (v) the retail supply of AV services. (8) (9)
(16)    The Transaction also results in vertical relationships between the Parties’ business
        activities in some levels of the theatrical value chain and in some levels of the AV value
        chain. However, these vertical links do not give rise to affected markets in any EEA
        country. (10)
(17)    Further, the Transaction results in conglomerate links in light of the Parties’ activities in
        neighbouring markets in the AV value chain where the Parties’ market share is 30% or
        more.

5.2.    Production and licensing of films for theatrical release

5.2.1.    Product market definition

5.2.1.1. Commission precedents
(21)      In previous decisions, the Commission has considered the question whether the
          production and licensing of films for theatrical release and the distribution of films for
          theatrical release constitute a single or two separate product markets. While in some cases
          the definition of the market was ultimately left open (15), in others the Commission found
          that the production/licensing and the distribution of films for theatrical release constitute
          two separate markets. (16)

(22)    In particular, in Time Warner/CME, the Commission considered that the
        production/licensing of films for theatrical distribution and the distribution of these films
        for theatrical release constitute two separate product markets. (17)
(23)    More recently, in The Walt Disney Company/Twenty-First Century Fox and
        Amazon/MGM, the Commission defined two separate product markets: one for the
        production and licensing of distribution rights to third-party distributors, and one for the
        distribution of films to cinemas. (18) The Commission noted that simply producing a film
        cannot constitute a market before a market-based exchange, such as the distribution of a
        film, takes place. (19)
(24)    The Commission has also considered – but left open – a further segmentation of the
        market between films produced by US film studios (hereafter also referred to as ‘US
        films’) and those produced by non-US film studios (hereafter also referred to as ‘non-US
        films’). (20)

5.2.1.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(25)    The Notifying Party does not express a view in the Form CO as regards the existence of
        a market for the production and licensing of films that would differ from the production
        and licensing of films to third-party distributors, as discussed in Amazon/MGM and The
        Walt Disney Company/Twenty-First Century Fox. (21)

5.2.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(26)    The Commission found that while there are fully integrated film studios that produce and
        distribute their own content, not all content producers are distributors. Similarly, there
        are independent distributors that do not produce content, but operate solely at the
        distribution level, engaging directly with cinemas where the content producers are either
        unable or unwilling to do so. (22) Having regard to the above, the Commission concludes
        that production and licensing on the one hand, and distribution of films for theatrical
        release, on the other hand, belong to separate relevant markets.
(27)    As regards a possible further sub-segmentation between US and non-US films, from a
        demand side most cinema operators consider that films by non-US film studios are never
        alternatives to films by US film studios. (23) This is because, as a general matter, films
        from non-US studios do not generate the same level of admissions, box-office revenue
        or footfall as films from US studios, with the latter typically accounting for a vast
        majority of admissions. (24) Further, it appears from the market investigation that films
        from US studios generally benefit from greater public recognition and substantially larger
        marketing budgets. (25) Finally, it follows from the market investigation that US studios
        obtain better commercial terms in their negotiations with cinema operators than non-US

          studios, which reflects the two points made immediately above. (26) That asymmetry is
          borne out by consumption data for the Union as a whole: films of US origin account for
          around two thirds of cinema admissions in the Union, whereas European films account
          for less than a third, and films originating in a European country other than that of release
          for a small fraction of admissions. (27)
(28)      Some market participants suggested that, within the market for the production and
          licensing of films for theatrical release, a narrower segment for high-budget or
          ‘blockbuster’ films should be distinguished. The Commission considers this is an
          element of differentiation that will be considered in the competitive assessment
          (paragraphs (201) and (210) and Table 2).

5.2.1.4. Conclusion
(29)      In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
          the production and licensing of films for theatrical release and the distribution of films
          for theatrical release constitute two separate product markets.
(30)      The question whether the market for the production and licensing of films for theatrical
          release should be further segmented based on whether the films were produced by US or
          non-US studios can be left open, since the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as
          to its compatibility with the internal market under any plausible market definition.
(31)      The Commission will assess the impact of the Transaction on the narrower market
          segment, i.e., the market for the production and licensing of US films for theatrical
          release, since the Parties are not active in the production and licensing of non-US films.

5.2.2.    Geographic market definition

5.2.2.1. Commission precedents
(32)      In past decisions, the Commission found the geographic market for the production and
          licensing of films for theatrical release to likely be at least EEA-wide and possibly
          worldwide. (28)

5.2.2.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(33)      As explained at paragraph (25) above, the Notifying Party does not express a view in the
          Form CO as regards the existence of a market for the production and licensing of films,
          nor, therefore, on its geographic scope. (29)

                                                               7
5.2.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(34)    Most responding film studios, and all responding US film studios, consider that they
        compete with one another on a worldwide level, including in the EEA. (30) As also
        explained by several respondents to the market investigation that expressed a view
        (‘respondents’), studios compete globally for financing, screenplays and talent, as well
        as for audiences in cinemas. (31) No responding studio considered that US studios and
        non-US studios do not compete with one another. (32)
(35)    The Commission acknowledges that studios frequently license the theatrical rights to
        their films to third-party distributors on a territory-by-territory basis, and that respondents
        pointed in this regard to the need for marketing and versioning to reflect local linguistic
        and cultural specificities. (33) However, respondents explained that sub-distributors are
        engaged where the studio does not have a local presence capable of serving a fragmented
        national exhibition market. (34) The territorial scope of the licences granted therefore
        reflects the organisation of the downstream distribution of films to exhibitors, which is
        national in scope (Section 5.3), rather than the conditions of competition prevailing at the
        upstream level. The choice of the territories for which rights are granted is itself a
        commercial parameter determined by the studio, and the studios from which distributors
        in any given Member State source films are the same across the EEA. The Commission
        therefore considers that territorial licensing does not evidence national conditions of
        competition for the production and licensing of films for theatrical release from US
        studios.
(36)    Some respondents indicated that competition also takes place at national or regional
        level. Several non-US studios explained that, in the territories in which they are active,
        they compete with local producers as well as with US studios and with producers active
        in several countries. (35) One US studio likewise explained that, alongside worldwide
        competition between major studios, local and regional producers compete for local-
        language and culturally specific content. (36) These responses concern the production of
        local and local-language films. They are consistent with the segmentation retained in
        Section 5.2.1, and explain why the geographic scope of this market was left open in past
        decisions, which concerned an undifferentiated market encompassing both films from
        US studios and locally produced films. That consideration bears on the market for films
        from non-US studios and not on the market for films from US studios.

5.2.2.4. Conclusion
(37)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
        the geographic market for the production and licensing of US films for theatrical release
        is at least EEA-wide. It will therefore carry out the assessment of this market on an EEA-
        wide basis.

5.3.1.    Product market definition

5.3.1.1. Commission precedents
(45)      In previous decisions, the Commission has considered the question of whether the
          production and licensing and distribution of films for theatrical release constitute a single
          or two separate product markets. While in some cases the definition of the market was
          ultimately left open (40), in others the Commission found that the production and
          licensing, on the one hand, and the distribution of films for theatrical release, on the other
          hand, constitute two separate markets. (41)

5.3.1.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(46)      For the reasons set out below, the Notifying Party submits that a distinction between self-
          distribution of films for theatrical release and the licensing thereof to third-party
          distributors is not warranted.
(47)      From a supply side, studios routinely choose between self-distribution and licensing to
          third-party distributors on a territory-by-territory basis. In practice, a studio may directly
          distribute in some countries while appointing a third-party distributor in other countries
          or may change distribution partners from time to time. Also, distribution agreements
          typically involve revenue-sharing arrangements (e.g., distribution fees as a share of total
          box office), aligning incentives across the value chain, recognizing the inherent risky

(38)     Form CO, paragraph 272.
(39)     Form CO, paragraphs 386-392.
(40)     Commission decision of 20 December 2012 in case M.6791 – The Walt Disney Company/Lucasfilm,
         paragraph 53; and Commission decision of 13 July 2010 in case M.5779 – Comcast/NBC Universal,
         paragraph 55.
(41)     Commission decision of 14 June 2013 in case M.6866 – Time Warner/CME, paragraph 152; Commission
         decision of 6 November 2018 in case M.8785 – The Walt Disney Company/Twenty-First Century Fox,
         paragraphs 30-32.

        nature of film production and distribution, and reflecting economic integration rather than
        separation.
(48)    From a demand side, first, cinemas seek timely access to titles that will draw audiences.
        Their decisions turn on expected title performance, not on whether a title is distributed
        directly by a studio or via a licensed distributor. Second, cinemas apply similar
        negotiating criteria, commitments and revenue-splits whether dealing with a studio
        directly or with a licensed distributor, which typically handles the studio’s entire slate in
        a given territory. Third, booking and programming decisions by cinemas respond to the
        same commercial considerations whether they contract directly with a studio or with a
        licensed distributor. (42)
(49)    The Notifying Party also considers a potential segmentation between films produced by
        US studios and those produced by non-US studios unwarranted, for the following
        reasons:
(50)    From the supply-side perspective, US and non-US studios compete in similar ways, for
        example, releasing films theatrically and using marketing to raise awareness of a new
        release across the EEA. Further, co-productions and financing partnerships between US
        and non-US studios are common and further blur any line between US and non-US films.
(51)    From the demand-side perspective, first, the film industry is inherently global. US and
        non-US films generally compete for the same audience and screen time, with cinemas
        curating diverse slates and distributors (including local ones) handling films from both
        US and non-US studios. Second, audience demand is primarily driven by story, genre,
        cast, rather than the geographic location of the film studio that produced the film. Third,
        ticket prices at cinemas do not depend on whether a film is produced by a US or non-US
        studio, demonstrating that consumers view these films as interchangeable. Fourth, box
        office depends on factors such as quality and marketing spend rather than whether the
        film originates from a US vs. non-US studio. Fifth, reviews and critical acclaim – which
        influence consumer choice – are evaluated without regard to production origin. (43)
(52)    In any event, the Notifying Party submits that the precise market definition can be left
        open, as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
        internal market under any plausible market definition. (44)

5.3.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(53)    Most studios and distributors that responded to the Commission’s market investigation
        consider that there is a separate market for the distribution of films to cinemas. (45).
        Further, the Commission considers that it would be artificial to segment this market per
        distribution route, that is distribution either directly by film studios or through licenses
        granted to third-party distributors. In this respect the Commission also notes that in both
        cases it is the studio that determines the commercial strategy of its films and retains final
        approval over key parameters, and the distributor implements that strategy at the studio’s
        direction. (46) Similarly, from a demand side perspective, it does not matter for a cinema
        whether it licenses the film from a distributor or the film studio itself.
(54)    As regards a segmentation of the market for the distribution of films to cinemas between
        films from US studios and films from non-US studios, most studios that responded to the
        market investigation have indicated that they compete with all film studios, wherever


(42)   Form CO, paragraphs 444-446.
(43)   Form CO, paragraphs 447-449.
(44)   Form CO, paragraphs 456-457.
(45)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1a Theatrical distribution value chain’, question C.A.1.
(46)   Response to RFI 24, Questions 8(a) and 9(b).

          they are based. (47) Similarly, most distributors that responded to the market investigation
          consider films produced by US studios to be alternatives to films produced by non-US
          studios at the distribution level. (48) However, as explained above at paragraph (27), from
          the perspective of cinemas, non-US films are not alternatives to US films because, as a
          general matter, non-US films do not generate the same level of admissions, box-office
          revenue or footfall as US films.

5.3.1.4. Conclusion
(55)      In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
          the distribution of films to cinemas constitutes a separate market and that this market
          should be further segmented between US films and non-US films. This decision does not
          take a position on whether films from non-US studios constitute a single market or
          several since the Parties are not active on any such market or segment.

5.3.2.    Geographic market definition

5.3.2.1. Commission precedents
(56)      The Commission has previously considered the market for the distribution of films to
          cinemas as national, and, in some cases, regional, based on considerations such as
          language differences or the fact that distribution is often carried out on a national
          basis. (49)

5.3.2.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(57)      The Notifying Party agrees with the Commission’s view. In any event, the Notifying
          Party submits that the geographic scope of the market can be left open, as the Transaction
          does not raise serious doubts under any plausible market definition. (50)

5.3.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(58)      Most studios and distributors that responded to the Commission’s market investigation
          have indicated that film distribution takes place on a national basis. (51) Similarly, most
          cinemas that responded to the market investigation have indicated that they acquire the
          right to screen films in their cinemas on a national basis. (52)

5.3.2.4. Conclusion
(59)      In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
          the market for the distribution of both US films and non-US films to cinemas is national
          in scope.

5.4.      Production and licensing of AV content
(60)      Both Paramount and Warner are active in the production and licensing of AV content.
(61)      Paramount is active in the production and licensing of AV content primarily through its
          TV Media and Studios segments. Paramount supplies films (following theatrical release)
          and television programming, including scripted and unscripted content. In the EEA,

          Paramount also licenses news content on a limited basis and has secured certain sports
          rights. As a vertically integrated player, Paramount produces AV content for captive use
          through its own wholesale and retail distribution services, while also licensing pre-
          produced AV content and producing commissioned content for third parties. Its main
          production activities are carried out through CBS Studios, Paramount Television Studios
          and Paramount Pictures. Paramount is also active, to a lesser extent, on the demand side
          through the acquisition of distribution rights to third-party AV content for use on its TV
          channels and VOD services. (53)
(62)      Warner is active in the production and licensing of AV content primarily through its
          studios business segment. Warner produces AV content primarily for captive use on its
          own networks and SVOD services, while also licensing pre-produced AV content to and
          producing commissioned content for third-party broadcasters and streaming platforms.
          Its main production activities are carried out through Warner Bros. Television, Warner
          Bros. Unscripted Television, Warner Bros. Animation, Cartoon Network Studios, HBO
          and Warner Bros. International Television Production. Warner is also active, to a lesser
          extent, on the demand side through the licensing and commissioning of third-party AV
          content for its own services. (54)

5.4.1.    Product market definition

5.4.1.1. Commission precedents
(63)      The Commission has previously concluded that there are separate markets for: (i) the
          production of commissioned AV content (also referred to as ad hoc or new content); and
          (ii) the licensing of pre-produced AV content (available off-the-shelf) (55).
(64)      With regard to the market for production of commissioned AV content, the Commission
          found the product market to be limited to non-captive AV production, thereby excluding
          captive AV production (i.e., AV content produced by broadcasters for use on their own
          TV channels), as this AV content is not offered on the market (56).
(65)      For both the market for pre-produced AV content and the market for commissioned AV
          content, the Commission has considered but ultimately left open further segmentations:
          (i) by content type (i.e., films, sports, and other AV content), and (ii) by exhibition
          window (free-to-air “FTA”, pay TV/SVOD). (57)
(66)      The Commission previously also considered a potential distinction between the
          acquisition of content to be incorporated into linear TV channels and non-linear AV

        services, but the Commission found that such a potential segmentation between linear
        and non-linear AV content would not be appropriate. (58)
(67)    In what concerns films, the Commission considered distinguishing between (i) US and
        non-US films, (ii) premium and non-premium content and (iii) by exhibition window ((i)
        pay TV/SVOD – first exhibition window; (ii) pay TV/SVOD – second exhibition
        window; (iii) TVOD; (iv) Pay-per-view “PPV”; and (v) FTA). (59)

5.4.1.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(68)    The Notifying Party submits that the production and supply of commissioned AV content
        and the licensing of pre-produced AV content belong to the same product market. (60)
(69)    From a supply-side perspective, the same studios deploy the same infrastructure and
        creative talent across both activities and can readily switch between the two models
        without incurring significant additional costs. (61) From a demand-side perspective,
        acquirers of AV content operate a unified content budget and continuously arbitrate
        between in-house production, co-production, commissioning and licensing based on slate
        needs, editorial strategy, timing, exclusivity and expected return on investment, with
        audience demand driven by whether content is new to the viewer rather than by its mode
        of acquisition. (62) The Notifying Party further notes that the industry has shifted towards
        greater content ownership, further blurring the boundary between in-house production,
        commissioning and acquisition of pre-produced content. (63)
(70)    Considering a hypothetical market for pre-produced AV content (separate from
        commissioned content), the Notifying Party considers that the market does not need to
        be further segmented according to the exhibition window since first and second
        exhibition windows distinction is increasingly outdated and arrangements vary across
        studios, countries and over time. (64)
(71)    Further, the Notifying Party considers that the market does not need to be sub-segmented
        according to the type of content. The Notifying Party claims that a segmentation between
        films and TV content is not warranted as both formats increasingly rely on the same
        talent, production resources and commercial considerations. (65)
(72)    Further, the Notifying Party does not consider it appropriate to segment further between
        US and non-US films, given the convergence of distribution channels and production
        models, and the fact that audience demand is driven primarily by content rather than
        geographic origin. (66)
(73)    Finally, as regards a segmentation between premium and non-premium pre-produced AV
        content, the Notifying Party submits that such a segmentation is not warranted. The
        Notifying Party considers that the distinction is inherently subjective and that both
        categories compete for the same customers, are supplied by the same studios and are

        consumed alongside one another, with audience demand driven primarily by content
        characteristics rather than any premium classification. (67)
(74)    In any event, the Notifying Party considers that it is not necessary for the Commission to
        reach a conclusion on the exact market definition as the Transaction does not raise serious
        doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any hypothetical market
        definition.

5.4.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(75)    Given that affected markets arise only on narrow markets for the licensing of pre-
        produced US films to FTA on the one hand, and to pay TV/SVOD on the other hand, the
        Commission’s market investigation focused on whether the licensing of pre-produced
        AV content constitutes a separate market from the production of commissioned AV
        content and to what extent these segmentations are relevant.
(76)    The Commission’s market investigation confirmed that the product market for
        production and licensing of AV content should be further segmented, having confirmed
        the possible market segmentations in the Commission precedents (as per Section 5.4.1.1.)
        as explained below.
(77)    First, the majority of respondents who expressed a view considered there are separate
        markets for commissioned AV content and pre-produced AV content. (68) Respondents
        noted that both markets are characterised by different pricing mechanisms and
        competitive conditions. One respondent explained that ‘these two types of content differ
        in terms of pricing, negotiation processes, exclusivity, and competitive conditions’ and
        that ‘treating them as separate product markets is appropriate’. (69) Nevertheless, some
        respondents noted that the emergence of vertically integrated streaming services and the
        increasing importance of exclusive rights have significantly blurred the boundary
        between the two categories. (70) The involvement of an acquirer in the creation of a work
        also occurs along a spectrum, from a limited pre-sales contribution to full financing with
        ownership of the intellectual property, so that the characterisation of a work under either
        heading is not always clear-cut. (71)
(78)    Second, the majority of respondents considers that the market for the licensing of pre-
        produced AV content should be limited to non-captive content. (72) As one respondent
        explained, ‘a significant and increasing share of pre-produced content is now held back
        for exclusive, in-house exploitation by vertically integrated players’.
(79)    Third, the majority of respondents to the market investigation indicated that the market
        for the licensing of pre-produced AV content should be further segmented by content
        type: (i) films, (ii) sports, and (iii) other AV content (including series, reality shows, kids
        shows, etc.) (73) as well as between (i) FTA, and (ii) pay TV, including SVOD. (74)
        Respondents noted that FTA broadcasters and pay TV/SVOD operators continue to rely
        on different business models, acquisition strategies and monetisation mechanisms. At the
        same time, some respondents observed an increasing convergence between certain

        SVOD services and traditional FTA offerings. Several respondents observed that hybrid
        business models combining advertising-funded and subscription-based revenues have
        become increasingly common and that content is frequently exploited across both
        segments through windowing strategies. (75)
(80)    Fourth, as regards the licensing of pre-produced films, the majority of respondents
        consider both US and non-US films are part of the same market. The majority of
        respondents explained that US and non-US films are broadly substitutable, noting that
        they are often acquired through similar licensing processes and evaluated according to
        audience appeal, editorial fit, window availability and price rather than origin alone.
        Nevertheless, some respondents also submitted that US films and non-US films fulfil
        different strategic functions for broadcasters and SVOD providers. In particular, they
        argued that major US studio productions often benefit from substantial production
        budgets, extensive international marketing campaigns and proven commercial success,
        making them especially valuable for audience attraction and subscription acquisition.
        One respondent submitted that US films and non-US films serve ‘entirely different
        strategic purposes for broadcasters and SVOD platforms’ and that the absence of
        premium US films ‘cannot readily be offset by acquiring more local or European films’.
        Other respondents highlighted differences in international appeal, pricing, audience
        expectations and regulatory requirements applicable to European works. However, some
        of those respondents also indicated that the distinction has become increasingly
        blurred. (76)
(81)    Fifth, as regards the licensing of pre-produced films, the majority of respondents
        considered that a segmentation based on exhibition windows ((i) pay TV/SVOD – first
        exhibition window; (ii) pay TV/SVOD – second exhibition window; (iii) TVOD; (iv)
        PPV; and (v) FTA) remains either entirely or at least partially accurate. For those
        respondents that considered the windows were only partially accurate, the results of the
        market investigation remain inconclusive as regards what alternative windows would be
        more suitable. (77)
(82)    Respondents generally considered that different exhibition windows continue to have
        distinct economic value, marketing characteristics and exclusivity conditions. For
        instance, one respondent indicated ‘The distinction remains broadly accurate, as
        different windows (first vs second pay-TV/SVoD, TVoD, PPV, FTA) still reflect distinct
        licensing strategies, pricing models, and degrees of exclusivity’. (78)
(83)    At the same time, respondents pointed to important changes in content distribution
        strategies. In particular, respondents highlighted that the growing role of (vertically
        integrated) SVOD platforms, the shortening of theatrical exclusivity periods (in favour
        of other windows in the AV value chain) and the increasing (flexibility of the)
        exploitation of content across multiple distribution channels, have significantly blurred
        the boundaries between these traditional sequential windows as well as lead to some
        overlaps between windows. As one respondent noted, ‘major content owners
        increasingly license and exploit content across multiple distribution channels and
        windows throughout the content lifecycle’. (79) For instance, a content provider may
        choose to align a film release on TVOD with availability on its own streaming platform.
        Another respondent indicated, ‘[…] the rise of vertically integrated direct-to-consumer
        ("D2C") SVoD services has significantly eroded the traditional windowing sequence:
        films are increasingly released directly (or very shortly) on the rights owner's own SVoD

          compressing the classic windows. The distinction between a "first" and a "second" pay-
          TV/SVoD exhibition window in particular has become increasingly blurred. A
          segmentation built on a rigid sequence of exhibition windows therefore no longer fully
          reflects current market reality’. (80)
(84)      Several respondents confirmed that SVOD and pay TV would be part of the same
          exhibition window. (81)

5.4.1.4. Conclusion
(85)      In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision, it
          is appropriate to consider a potential segmentation between the licensing of pre-produced
          AV content and the production of commissioned AV content.
(86)      Concerning all other potential segmentations, the Commission considers that the precise
          product market definition for the production and licensing of AV content may be left
          open, as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the
          internal market under any potential product market definition, as the below assessment
          considering each of the potential market segmentations presented above shows.

5.4.2.    Geographic market definition

5.4.2.1. Commission precedents
(87)      The Commission has previously considered the geographic market for the licensing of
          AV content (including potential segmentations) as either national or regional, based on
          linguistically homogeneous areas. (82)

5.4.2.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(88)      The Notifying Party submits that the precise scope of the geographic market for the
          production and licensing of AV content can be left open as the Transaction does not raise
          serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any plausible market
          definition. (83)

5.4.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(89)      The majority of respondents to the market investigation that expressed a view consider
          that competition in the production and licensing of AV content market takes place at the
          national level. (84)

5.4.2.4. Conclusion
(90)      In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
          the geographic scope of the market for the production and licensing of AV content can
          be left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with
          the internal market under any potential geographic market definition, whether national

5.5.    Wholesale supply of TV channels
(91)    Both Paramount and Warner are active in the wholesale supply of TV channels.
(92)    Paramount’s TV channels feature a mix of entertainment, films, and children’s
        programming. In the EEA, Paramount’s TV channels include, among others,
        Nickelodeon, Nick Jr., Nicktoons, Comedy Central, MTV, Super!, and SkyShowtime (a
        joint venture between Paramount and Comcast). (86)
(93)    Warner offers a number of TV channels, including films, sports, children’s, lifestyle, and
        factual programming. In the EEA, Warner’s main channels include HBO, Cinemax, CNN
        International, Discovery Channel, Discovery History, Discovery Life, Animal Planet,
        Travel Channel, Food Network, HGTV, TLC, Cartoon Network, Cartoonito,
        Boomerang, Eurosport, and TNT Sports. (87)

5.5.1.1. Commission precedents
(94)    In previous decisions, the Commission identified a separate product market for the
        wholesale supply of TV channels, (88) consisting in the packaging of AV content
        (produced or acquired) into TV channels, which are supplied by wholesale suppliers of
        TV channels to retail TV distributors/MVPDs.
(95)    Within this market, the Commission has considered a distinction between FTA TV
        channels and pay TV channels. (89) In addition, the Commission considered that the
        segment for pay TV channels may be sub-segmented into premium pay TV channels and
        basic pay TV channels. (90) In certain cases, the Commission considered that FTA
        channels might form part of the same market as basic pay TV channels. (91)
(96)    Furthermore, the Commission also examined, but ultimately left open, whether the
        following additional segmentations would be appropriate: (i) by genre/thematic content




(85)   At EEA-wide level, the Parties’ combined shares do not give rise to any affected markets in any possible
       sub-segmentations of the market for the production of AV content.

        (films, sports, news, children and general entertainment), (92) and (ii) between linear and
        non-linear services. (93) (94)

5.5.1.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(97)    The Notifying Party submits that there is, at least, a single overall market for the
        wholesale supply of TV channels, and that no segmentation should be made between
        (i) FTA and pay TV channels; (ii) basic and premium pay TV channels; (iii) genre; or
        (iv) linear and non-linear services. (95)
(98)    Furthermore, the Notifying Party submits that, even the overall market for the wholesale
        supply of TV channels does not accurately capture the competitive dynamics in the
        industry. This is because the popularity of linear TV is in sustained decline as linear
        consumption is shifting at scale to non-linear viewing alternatives, mainly SVOD/VOD
        services. SVOD/VOD services are now routinely promoted and offered by retail TV
        distributors/MVPDs alongside linear TV channel portfolios. Equally, retail TV
        distributors/MVPDs increasingly integrate, and weigh SVOD/VOD offers in wholesale
        carriage negotiations. As a result, the delineation of a relevant product market including
        only linear TV channels substantially understates the competitive constraints by
        SVOD/VOD services under which wholesale suppliers of linear TV channel operate. (96)
(99)    As regards the potential segmentation of the overall market for the wholesale supply TV
        channels into pay TV channels and FTA TV channels, the Notifying Party submits that
        such a segmentation is not warranted. From a supply side perspective: first, pay TV and
        FTA TV channels compete for and show similar content, such that FTA TV wholesale
        operators frequently acquire AV content rights in competition with pay TV wholesale
        operators; second, often the same channel brand (with substantially the same
        programming) can be broadcasted in either FTA TV or pay TV form; third, often the
        same wholesale supplier of TV channels offers both FTA TV and pay TV channels within
        the same EEA country. From a demand side perspective: first, negotiations for the
        acquisition of TV channels often cover both FTA TV and pay TV channel portfolios;
        second, pay TV subscribers generally have access to FTA TV channels, which constrains
        the willingness of retail TV distributors/MVPDs to pay for pay TV channels when strong
        FTA TV channels address the same audience needs. (97)

                                                              18
(100)    Further, the Notifying Party submits that a distinction of pay TV channels between
         ‘premium’ and ‘basic’ would not be appropriate. There is no industry-wide definition as
         to what constitutes ‘premium’ and ‘basic’. The distinction is further blurred due to the
         rise of bundled packages. From a supply side perspective, channel programming sits on
         a continuum of quality. The same TV channel may be re-tiered or repositioned across
         retail TV distributors/MVPDs, further undermining any split. From a demand side
         perspective, retail TV distributors/MVPDs build channel lineups to meet audience
         preferences for variety, quality, and convenience. They substitute among channels based
         on price, audience appeal and terms. For viewers, the value is the overall bundle and
         experience, including the ability to access diverse content via a single subscription. (98)
(101)    In addition, the Notifying Party submits that a distinction by genre is not warranted. From
         a supply side perspective, wholesale suppliers of TV channels typically package channels
         of different genres together and negotiate them with retail TV distributors/MVPDs using
         the same commercial levers (e.g., carriage fees, minimum guarantees, marketing
         commitments). Wholesale negotiations are typically conducted on a portfolio basis
         (including multiple TV channels and VOD content) rather than on channel-by-channel
         basis or genre‑by‑genre basis, and often a single fee is charged across all channels in the
         package. From a demand side perspective, retail TV distributors/MVPDs routinely swap
         TV channels within and across genres. The retailers’ decision to acquire a particular
         channel does not turn on whether a channel could be identified as belonging to a
         particular ‘genre’. Retailers build diverse bundles of channels across different genres to
         offer consumers a wide selection of content in one subscription. Therefore, they buy
         portfolios, not isolated genre silos, and carriage negotiations occur on ratings and brand
         equity rather than genre. (99)
(102)    Last, the Notifying Party notes that a segmentation between linear and non-linear services
         does not reflect market dynamics as much of the same content is available in both linear
         and non-linear form. Most retail suppliers of AV services acquire both linear and non-
         linear rights together (e.g., linear rights plus non-linear rights such as TV Everywhere
         and catch-up services), rather than purchasing them separately. Similarly, the majority of
         wholesale suppliers supply both linear and non-linear rights to retailers of AV services
         (with most wholesale TV channel licencing agreements including non-linear catch-up
         and catalogue rights as standard). (100)

5.5.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(103)    The results of the market investigation confirmed the Commission’s precedent of
         identifying a separate market for the wholesale supply of TV channels. (101) Within such
         market, the majority of respondents to the market investigation consider that the
         distinction between the supply of FTA channels and pay TV channels remains
         appropriate. (102)
(104)    However, the results of the market investigation yielded mixed results on whether a
         distinction should be made between ‘basic’ and ‘premium’ pay TV channels. While the
         majority of respondents indicated that a distinction between ‘basic’ and ‘premium’ pay
         TV channels would be relevant (103), most respondents submitted that there does not exist
         a consistent definition of ‘basic’ and ‘premium’ pay TV channels. (104) Furthermore, the

         majority of respondents indicated that there are TV programmes distributed to different
         types of channels that market themselves both as ‘basic’ and ‘premium’ (for example,
         the same TV programme would be accessible through both ‘basic’ and ‘premium’ TV
         channels). (105) In addition, the majority of respondents noted that there are TV channels
         being carried as part of a basic-tier package of one retail TV distributor/MVPD and
         offered within a premium-tier package of another retail TV distributor/MVPD. (106)
(105)    As regards the potential distinction between the wholesale supply of linear and non-linear
         services (e.g. TV everywhere, catch-up services, etc.), the majority of respondents to the
         market investigation consider that linear and non-linear services belong to the same
         product market. In this regard, the majority of respondents confirmed that non-linear
         services have gradually been associated to TV channels in order to complement the TV
         offering and enhance the viewer experience of traditional linear TV channels without
         such non-linear services constituting a separate offering. (107)
(106)    In relation to a potential segmentation of the market for the wholesale supply of TV
         channels by genre, the majority of respondents to the market investigation indicated that
         generally a distinction by genre would be relevant. (108) The majority of respondents also
         stated that if a retail TV distributor/MVPD were to drop a channel from its retail offering,
         it would typically replace such channel with another channel of the same genre. (109)
(107)    However, at the same time the results of the market investigation point towards a broader
         market definition potentially encompassing TV channels of different genres. In
         particular:
(108)    From a demand side perspective, the majority of respondents to the market investigation
         submitted that the decision of retail TV distributors/MVPDs to acquire one or several TV
         channels is not typically based on the channel’s genre. Rather their acquisition decision
         is based on a multi-genre approach, namely acquiring a diverse blend of TV channels
         irrespective of their genre. (110) In addition, the majority of respondents indicated that
         retail TV distributors/MVPDs do not typically have a fixed investment budget allocated
         to particular genres. (111)
(109)    Specifically, respondents explained that ‘[t]he viewer is a household that needs
         diversity’. Therefore, ‘[r]etail TV distributors typically aim to build a balanced and
         diversified portfolio of channels in order to meet the expectations of a broad audience’.
         Generally, acquisitions of TV channels ‘may serve multiple objectives, including
         audience diversification, strengthening of attractive or reputed content offerings,
         achieving economies of scale, or targeting specific viewer segments. In this context, the
         selection of TV channels is guided not only by genre but also by their complementarity
         within the existing portfolio and their contribution to broader objectives such as growth,
         profitability, and market positioning’. Another respondent stated that ‘[a]s a cable
         operator, we aim to offer a balanced and attractive channel portfolio that addresses the
         diverse preferences of our subscribers. While genre remains an important consideration
         for content selection and packaging, our acquisition decisions are made with a view to
         maintaining a balanced overall portfolio rather than following a single genre-based

                                                          20
         strategy. We therefore adopt a multi-genre approach that reflects subscriber demand,
         commercial conditions and the overall attractiveness of our retail offering’. (112)
(110)    Against this background, respondents noted that retail TV distributors/MVPDs do not
         typically ‘allocate fixed budgets to specific genres and instead focus on the overall value
         of content portfolios and distribution agreements’. In practice, ‘investment decisions are
         made holistically, based on the need to build an attractive and competitive overall
         channel portfolio. Budgets are therefore flexible and can be reallocated depending on
         strategic priorities, market conditions, and content availability. In the same vein, another
         respondent indicated that retail TV distributors/MVPDs ‘try to allocate investment
         (acquisition) budgets to a mix of genres, optimising the financials with consumer value
         across genres and wholesale suppliers’. (113)
(111)    From a supply side perspective, an overwhelming majority of respondents indicated that,
         negotiations between wholesale suppliers of TV channels and retail TV
         distributors/MVPDs are typically conducted on a portfolio basis, including multiple TV
         channels of different genres (as opposed to a genre-by-genre basis, or channel-by-channel
         basis). (114) The licence/carriage fee structure for the portfolio varies, whereby sometimes
         a single fee across the package is charged, and other times separate fees are allocated per
         channel. (115)
(112)    In this respect a respondent explained that portfolio-negotiations ‘reflect common
         commercial practice as content providers seek to distribute their channel portfolios as a
         package. While commercial terms may be influenced by the value or popularity of
         specific channels, negotiations are generally carried out for the portfolio as a whole
         rather than on a genre-by-genre or channel-by-channel basis’. Another respondent noted
         that ‘wholesale negotiations are most commonly conducted on a portfolio basis,
         particularly where media groups supply multiple channels across different genres. In
         such cases, commercial discussions often cover a bundle of channels, which may include
         a mix of genres (e.g. entertainment, news, children’s and thematic channels), together
         with associated commercial terms’. (116) A further respondent noted that ‘[w]hile
         negotiations are often conducted on a portfolio basis, the underlying pricing structure is
         not uniform across the market. Some channel suppliers operate under traditional
         carriage fee arrangements, whereby fees are allocated to individual channels or channel
         groups. Other agreements may be based on broader portfolio-level commercial
         arrangements. [...]. As a result, both portfolio-based and channel-specific pricing
         structures can coexist within the same retail distribution platform. Accordingly, neither
         a package-level fee nor a channel-specific fee can be regarded as universally
         representative of current market practice’. (117)
(113)    In light of the above considerations, a market definition potentially encompassing TV
         channels of different genres cannot be excluded.
(114)    Concerning children’s TV channels in particular, when asked about the
         importance/relevance of children’s TV channels in wholesale portfolio-negotiations, the
         majority of respondents indicated, either that children’s TV channels are an important
         but not decisive factor in wholesale negotiations, or that they have no or minimal
         relevance in driving wholesale negotiations (including the price of the bundle). (118)

(115)    In light of the above market feedback, the Commission considers that there are
         indications that competition in the market for the wholesale supply of TV channels does
         not take place on a genre-by-genre basis and, therefore, that the delineation of narrow
         genre-specific product markets may not be warranted. In any event, for completeness, in
         this decision the Commission carried out its competitive assessment on the basis of the
         narrowest genre-specific product market segmentations (namely: children, films, sports,
         news, and general entertainment).
(116)    With regard to a potential narrowly delineated product market for the wholesale supply
         of children’s TV channels, the results of the market investigation demonstrate that SVOD
         services, to the extent they offer children’s content, may form part of the same relevant
         product market as dedicated children’s TV channels.
(117)    In this respect the majority of respondents to the market investigation consider that
         SVOD services offering children’s content (such as Disney+, Netflix, and Amazon Prime
         Video) constitute alternatives to dedicated children’s TV channels. (119) (120) In
         particular, a respondent indicated that ‘SVOD is a different consumption method of the
         same content, which is highly popular and can replace linear viewing of the same
         content’. Similarly, another respondent noted that ‘[v]iewership trends and customer
         behaviour clearly show that these services are becoming highly relevant substitutes for
         dedicated children’s TV channels’. A further respondent stated that ‘from the perspective
         of viewers and retail distributors, both types of services compete to satisfy demand for
         children’s entertainment and educational content. The growth of services such as
         Disney+, Netflix, Amazon Prime Video and Apple TV+ has increased the availability of
         children’s content through on-demand consumption models, which are particularly well
         suited to younger audiences. As a result, these services can exert competitive pressure
         on traditional linear children’s channels. That said, the degree of substitutability may
         vary depending on the specific channel, content offering, brand strength and consumer
         preferences. While SVOD services and dedicated children’s channels are not necessarily
         identical products, they are increasingly viewed as alternative ways of accessing
         children’s content. Therefore, while dedicated children’s TV channels still have a role in
         pay-TV packages, their importance is decreasing due to the growing popularity and
         availability of children’s content on SVOD and OTT platforms’. (121)
(118)    Furthermore, the majority of respondents indicated that commercial/pricing negotiations
         between wholesale suppliers of TV channels and retail TV distributors/MVPDs (very)
         often cover access to both linear TV channels and non-linear SVOD services. (122) In this
         respect, respondents noted that to the extent wholesale suppliers offer both TV channels
         and SVOD services, then ‘usually the negotiations cover […] both services’. Equally,
         retail TV distributors/MVPDs ‘typically seek a range of ways of delivering content to
         their customers, which includes linear and on-demand services (whether catch up or a
         separate SVOD environment)’. (123)
(119)    In addition, when asked about switching possibilities in case the price of a children’s TV
         channel is increased or offered on less favourable terms, almost an equal number of
         market participants responded that switching could happen to either another children’s
         TV channel or to SVOD services. (124) Respondents indicated that the main factors that

          may prevent viewers of a children’s TV channel from switching to viewing children’s
          content on a SVOD platform include subscription costs for SVOD platforms, viewer
          preferences and the lack of curated viewing environment. (125)
(120)     Based on the market feedback set out above, the Commission considers that a broader
          relevant product market for children’s programming encompassing both dedicated
          children’s TV channels and SVOD services offering children’s content is plausible. In
          any event, on a conservative basis, in this decision the Commission carried out its
          competitive assessment on the basis of the narrowest product market including only
          dedicated children’s TV channels.

5.5.1.4. Conclusion
(121)     In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision, it
          is not appropriate to consider a potential segmentation in the market for the wholesale
          supply of TV channels between linear and non-linear services. In what concerns all other
          potential segmentations, the Commission considers that the precise product market
          definition for the wholesale supply of TV channels may be left open, as the Transaction
          does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any
          potential product market definition. For the purposes of this decision, the Commission
          conducted a competitive assessment considering each of the potential market
          segmentations presented above (i.e., distinction between FTA and pay TV channels,
          between premium and basic pay TV channels, and by genre).

5.5.2.    Geographic market definition

5.5.2.1. Commission precedents
(122)     The Commission previously considered that the market for the wholesale supply of TV
          channels is either national in scope or comprises a linguistically homogeneous area
          (which can be either broader or narrower than a national market). (126)

5.5.2.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(123)     The Notifying Party submits that the precise scope of the geographic market for the
          wholesale supply of TV channels can be left open as the Transaction does not raise
          serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market under any plausible market
          definition. (127)

5.5.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(124)     The majority of respondents to the market investigation confirmed that competition in
          the market for the wholesale supply of TV channels takes place at national level. (128)

5.6.      Retail supply of AV services
(126)     Both Paramount and Warner are active in the retail supply of AV services.
(127)     Paramount is active in the retail supply of AV services through its direct-to-consumer
          (‘DTC’) activities. In the EEA, Paramount offers non-linear AV services through the
          SVOD services Paramount+ and SkyShowtime, as well as the FAST service Pluto TV,
          which are available in several EEA countries. (129)
(128)     Warner is active in the retail supply of AV services through its DTC activities. In the
          EEA, Warner offers non-linear AV services primarily through the SVOD services HBO
          Max and Discovery+, as well as the Polish streaming service Player.pl. (130)

5.6.1.    Product market definition

5.6.1.1. Commission precedents
(129)     In its past decisional practice, the Commission considered a split of the market for the
          retail supply of AV services into (i) FTA retail services and (ii) pay TV retail
          services. (131) The Commission has also examined whether the market for the retail
          supply of AV services could be segmented further (i) between premium and basic pay
          TV services; (132) (ii) between linear and non-linear services (pay per view, video on
          demand); (133) and (iii) between distribution technologies (cable, satellite, terrestrial or
          IPTV). (134)

(130)    In Amazon/MGM, the Commission assessed SVOD services separately. (135)

5.6.1.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(131)    The Notifying Party submits that the market definition can be left open, as the
         Transaction does not raise any competitive concerns on any potential plausible market.
         However, the Parties note that linear services exert a declining competitive pressure on
         non-linear services, particularly on SVOD services. (136)
(132)    In particular, the Notifying Party submits that SVOD services are not substitutable with
         linear television and other non-linear services, such as advertising-funded video-on-
         demand (‘AVOD’), transactional video-on-demand (‘TVOD’), PPV and free ad-
         supported streaming television (‘FAST’). (137)
(133)    First, the Notifying Party submit that, from a supply perspective, the SVOD services
         business model differs fundamentally from linear television, which offers scheduled
         programming, and from non-linear retail services like AVOD, TVOD, and PPV services,
         which rely on different monetization models, offer a different consumer value
         proposition, and exhibit different consumption patterns. These differences translate into
         distinct pricing structures, content strategies and investment incentives. (138)
(134)    Second, from a demand perspective, SVOD services provide on-demand access to
         content libraries across devices and are primarily based on subscription revenues,
         whereas linear television is based on scheduled programming and is often oriented
         towards live content, including news and sports. (139)
(135)    Third, SVOD services are not constrained by AVOD and FAST services, given the
         absence of a subscription fee and the presence of ads. SVOD users may complement their
         viewing experiences with AVOD/FAST services but would not generally replace their
         SVOD subscriptions with ad-funded free alternatives. (140)
(136)    Fourth, SVOD services also differ from TVOD and PPV services, which are based on
         one-off transactions for individual titles or specific events (often live) rather than
         recurring subscriptions. TVOD/PPV viewers will not typically expand one-off purchases
         into recurring subscription fees absent a broader change in viewing needs. (141)
(137)    Finally, the Notifying Party submits that video sharing platforms and user generated
         platforms like YouTube, TikTok or Instagram, are not substitutes for SVOD services.
         Most content on those services is user generated, not commissioned or overseen. That is
         a stark difference from the highly produced, scripted, longform programming that drives
         SVOD subscriptions. (142)

5.6.1.3. Commission’s assessment
(138)    Given that affected markets arise only in the narrowest possible market for the retail
         supply of SVOD services, the Commission’s market investigation focused on whether

                                                      25
          SVOD constitutes a separate product market to other non-linear services, such as AVOD,
          FAST, TVOD and PPV. (143)
(139)     The majority of respondents to the Commission’s market investigation that expressed a
          view consider the retail supply of SVOD services to be a separate market from other
          types of offerings, e.g., AVOD, FAST, TVOD or PPV. (144) Several respondents pointed
          to differences across the relevant commercial and consumer-facing aspects of the
          services. In particular, they noted that SVOD services are characterised by recurring
          subscription payments and continuous access to a catalogue of content, whereas ‘AVOD
          and FAST are primarily advertising-funded and free-to-access, while TVOD and PPV
          are based on individual transactions for specific content’. Respondents further
          considered that these differences affect ‘pricing, monetisation, content offering,
          consumer expectations and the way in which users access and consume the services’.
          Similarly, another respondent noted that ‘SVOD, AVOD, FAST, TVOD and PPV continue
          to differ significantly in terms of business model, consumer behaviour, pricing structure
          and content consumption patterns’. (145)
(140)     At the same time, some respondents argued that the distinction between SVOD and other
          non-linear audiovisual services has become less clear-cut. These respondents highlighted
          the increasing convergence of business models across AV content services, noting in
          particular that ‘the traditional distinction between SVOD and AVOD has become
          increasingly blurred’ as ‘[m]any major SVOD providers now offer advertising-supported
          subscription tiers, including services such as Netflix and Disney+. As a result, consumers
          are increasingly accessing content through hybrid business models that combine
          subscription revenues with advertising. From a consumer perspective, content
          availability, price, user experience and brand recognition are often more important than
          the underlying monetisation model’.
(141)     Some respondents also pointed to technological developments, noting that the
          widespread adoption of connected devices enables consumers to access different types
          of audiovisual services through the same interface and on the same devices. (146)

5.6.1.4. Conclusion
(142)     In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
          the question whether there should be an overall market for the retail supply of AV content
          or whether there should be a separate product market for the retail supply of SVOD
          services can be left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
          compatibility with the internal market under any plausible product market definition (i.e.,
          an overall market for the retail supply of AV content or a separate product market for the
          retail supply of SVOD services).

5.6.2.    Geographic market definition

5.6.2.1. Commission precedents
(143)     The Commission has previously considered the market for the retail supply of AV
          services to be national in scope since suppliers compete on a nation-wide basis to supply

         different distribution technologies.

5.6.2.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(144)    The Notifying Party submits that the precise scope of the geographic market for the retail
         supply of AV services can be left open as the Transaction does not raise serious doubts
         as to its compatibility with the internal market under any plausible market definition. (150)

5.6.2.3. Commission’s assessment
(145)    The majority of respondents to the market investigation indicated that the relevant market
         definition for AV markets, including retail supply of AV services is national. (151)

5.6.2.4. Conclusion
(146)    In light of the above, the Commission considers that, for the purposes of this decision,
         the relevant geographic market for the retail supply of AV services, including all its
         possible sub-segments (including SVOD), is national in scope.


6.       COMPETITIVE ASSESSMENT

6.1.     Introduction
(147)    Under Article 2(2) and (3) of the Merger Regulation, the Commission must assess
         whether a proposed concentration would significantly impede effective competition in
         the internal market or in a substantial part of it, in particular through the creation or
         strengthening of a dominant position.
(148)    In this respect, a merger may entail horizontal and/or non-horizontal (namely, vertical or
         conglomerate) effects. Horizontal effects are those deriving from a concentration where
         the undertakings concerned are actual or potential competitors of each other in one or
         more of the relevant markets concerned. Vertical effects are those deriving from a
         concentration where the undertakings concerned are active on different levels of the
         supply chain. Conglomerate effects are those deriving from a concentration where the
         undertakings concerned are in a relationship which is neither horizontal nor vertical. A
         concentration may involve all three types of effects. In such a case, the Commission will
         appraise horizontal and non-horizontal effects in accordance with the guidance set out in

        paragraphs 49-52; Commission decision of 15 March 2022 in case M.10349 – Amazon/MGM,

         the relevant notices, that is to say the Horizontal Merger Guidelines (152) and the Non-
         Horizontal Merger Guidelines. (153)

6.2.     Rationale of the Transaction
(149)    The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will enable the merged entity to reach
         more consumers, increase output and compete more effectively across streaming, filmed
         entertainment, and television media.
(150)    In streaming, the merged entity will provide a broader platform offering across
         Paramount+, HBO Max, Discovery+, and Pluto TV (Free Ad-Supported Streaming
         Television, ‘FAST’), which has the required scale to effectively compete with Netflix,
         the dominant SVOD incumbent. Paramount intends on creating a scaled streaming
         platform by integrating Paramount+ and HBO Max and plans to increase content spend.
(151)    In filmed entertainment, the merged entity will maintain the studios as distinct creative
         organisations, growing the scaled supply of high-quality content for their combined
         services and third-party distribution. Post-Transaction, Paramount expects studios’
         revenues to grow as the merged entity’s combined film slate (154) increases to [>30] films
         with larger average worldwide box office estimates [details of the Parties’ strategy and
         business activities] and growth in the TV licensing to third parties [details of the Parties’
         strategy and business activities]. In television media, the Transaction brings together
         Paramount’s and Warner’s complementary content across linear broadcasting and will
         offer a more compelling proposition to advertisers.
(152)    The Notifying Party further submits that the merged entity will operate on a more than
         USD 6 billion cost synergy opportunity, in addition to the more than USD 3 billion in
         standalone cost efficiencies that Paramount expects to achieve in its current internal
         transformational plans. These efficiencies will help the merged entity to compete against
         major industry players like Netflix and Disney. These cost savings opportunities will be
         generated in (i) labour, via the elimination of overlapping roles ([details of anticipated
         synergies]), (ii) non-labour savings, from overlapping non-labour spend in areas like
         [details of anticipated synergies] and (iii) [details of anticipated synergies]. (155)

6.3.     Market shares
(153)    According to the Horizontal Merger Guidelines and the Non-Horizontal Merger
         Guidelines, (156) in the assessment of the effects of a merger, market shares constitute a
         useful first indication of the structure of the markets at stake and of the competitive
         importance of the relevant players.
(154)    In this Section, the Commission presents the Parties’ market shares in all the relevant
         markets described in Section 5 above where the Parties have horizontally overlapping
         activities that give rise to affected markets, as well as the Parties’ market shares of 30%

(153)   Guidelines on the assessment of non-horizontal mergers under the Council Regulation on the control of
        concentrations between undertakings (‘Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines’), OJ C 265, 190.2008.
(154)   A film slate refers to a collection of films that a film studio plans to produce or distribute within a specific
        period. It is essentially a lineup of upcoming films.
(155)   Form CO, paragraphs 1603 and 1604.
(156)   Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 14; Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 24.
