6.4.       Horizontal effects

6.4.1.     Legal framework
(167)      The Horizontal Merger Guidelines describe two main ways in which horizontal mergers
           may significantly impede effective competition. In particular, the proposed concentration
           might be creating or strengthening a dominant position: (i) by eliminating important
           competitive constraints on one or more firms, which consequently would have increased
           market power, without resorting to coordinated behaviour (non-coordinated effects); and
           (ii) by changing the nature of competition in such a way that firms that previously were
           not coordinating their behaviour, are significantly more likely to coordinate and raise
           prices or otherwise harm effective competition (coordinated effects). (167)

6.4.1.1. Non-coordinated effects
(168)      A merger giving rise to horizontal non-coordinated effects might significantly impede
           effective competition by creating or strengthening the dominant position of a single firm,
           one which, typically, would have an appreciably larger market share than the next
           competitor post-merger. Moreover, also mergers that do not lead to the creation or the
           strengthening of a single firm’s dominant position may create competition concerns
           under the substantive test set out in Article 2(2) and Article 2(3) of the Merger
           Regulation. Regarding mergers in oligopolistic markets, the Merger Regulation clarifies
           that ‘under certain circumstances, concentrations involving the elimination of important
           competitive constraints that the merging parties exerted upon each other, as well as a

(167)     Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 22.
                                                                 40
         reduction of competitive pressure on the remaining competitors, may, even in the absence
         of a likelihood of coordination between the members of the oligopoly, result in a
         significant impediment to effective competition’. (168)
(169)    The Horizontal Merger Guidelines list a number of factors which may influence whether
         or not significant horizontal non-coordinated effects are likely to result from a merger,
         such as the large market shares of the merging firms, the fact that the merging firms are
         close competitors, the limited possibilities for customers to switch suppliers, or the fact
         that the merger would eliminate an important competitive force. Not all those factors
         need to be present to make significant non-coordinated effects likely and it is not an
         exhaustive list. (169)
(170)    The Horizontal Merger Guidelines acknowledge that a merger that creates or strengthens
         the market power of a buyer may also significantly impede effective competition, in
         particular by creating or strengthening a dominant position. In certain circumstances,
         competition in the downstream markets could be adversely affected if, in particular, the
         merged entity was likely to use its buyer power vis-à-vis its suppliers to foreclose its
         rivals. (170)
(171)    To assess whether a merger that creates or strengthens buyer power would significantly
         impede effective competition, the Commission must analyse the competitive conditions
         in the upstream markets and to evaluate the possible positive and negative effects of the
         merger. (171)
(172)    In this context, paragraphs 24-36 of the Horizontal Merger Guidelines apply mutatis
         mutandis to the buyer side of the market, to investigate whether the Transaction would
         result in an increase in buyer power, as a result of the elimination of important
         competitive constraints that the merging Parties exerted upon each other prior to the
         Transaction.
(173)    These principles apply to all upstream purchasing markets, including markets for the
         purchase of labour. The Market Definition Notice applies to purchasing markets, with
         the substitution assessment focusing on the alternatives available to suppliers i.e., the
         workers. (172) Effects impacting workers but unrelated to the loss of competition resulting
         from the merger — such as corporate restructuring — fall outside the scope of this
         assessment under Article 2 of the Merger Regulation.

6.4.1.2. Coordinated effects
(174)    A merger in a concentrated market may also significantly impede effective competition
         due to horizontal coordinated effects where, through the creation or the strengthening of
         a dominant position, it increases the likelihood that firms are able to coordinate their
         behaviour and raise prices, even without entering into an agreement or resorting to a
         concerted practice within the meaning of Article 101 TFEU. A merger may also make
         coordination easier, more stable or more effective for firms that were already
         coordinating before the merger, either by making the coordination more robust or by
         permitting to coordinate on even higher prices. (173)



(168)   Merger Regulation, recital 25. Similar wording is also found in paragraph 25 of the Horizontal Merger
        Guidelines.
(169)   Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraphs 26-36.
(170)   Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 61.
(171)   Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 63.
(172)   Commission Notice on the definition of the relevant market for the purposes of Union competition law,
        C/2023/6789, OJ C, C/2024/1645, 22.2.2024 (‘Market Definition Notice’), paragraph 7.
(173)   Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 39.
                                                        41
(175)     To assess whether a merger gives rise to horizontal coordinated effects, the Commission
          should examine, firstly, whether it would be possible to reach terms of coordination and,
          secondly, whether the coordination would likely be sustainable. These criteria are
          elaborated further below.
(176)     As regards the possibility of reaching terms of coordination, coordination is more likely
          to emerge in markets where it is relatively simple to reach a common understanding on
          the terms of coordination. (174) Coordination may take various forms, including keeping
          prices above the competitive level, limiting output or capacity, limiting product features,
          delaying innovation, or dividing the market, for instance by geographic area, by customer
          characteristics or by allocating contracts in bidding markets. Firms may also coordinate
          by following the market leader in its commercial strategy, such as a price increase or a
          change to other commercial or supply terms. (175)
(177)     Moreover, in examining the possibility and sustainability of coordination, the
          Commission should specifically consider the changes that the Transaction brings about,
          focusing on whether those changes are likely to create or strengthen an environment
          conducive to coordination, rather than on establishing that coordination will actually
          occur. The reduction in the number of firms in a market may in itself be a factor that
          facilitates coordination. A merger may also increase the risk of coordinated effects in
          other ways, for example by eliminating a firm with a disruptive presence on the market,
          by increasing similarities between the remaining firms, or by creating structural or
          commercial links between competitors, including through joint ventures.
(178)     As regards the sustainability of coordination, coordination is sustainable where three
          further conditions are met, in addition to reaching a shared understanding on the terms of
          coordination. (176) Firstly, the coordinating undertakings must be able to monitor to a
          sufficient degree whether the terms of the coordination are being adhered to; there must
          be sufficient market transparency for each undertaking concerned to be aware,
          sufficiently precisely and quickly, of the way in which the market conduct of each of the
          other participants in the coordination is evolving. Secondly, there must be some form of
          credible deterrent mechanism that can come into play if deviation from the terms of
          coordination is detected. Thirdly, the reactions of outsiders, such as current or future
          competitors, and also the reactions of customers, should not be such as to jeopardise the
          results expected from the coordination. (177) In applying these conditions, it is necessary
          to avoid a mechanical approach involving the separate verification of each of them in
          isolation, without taking account of the overall economic mechanism of a hypothetical
          tacit coordination. (178)

6.4.2.    Overview of horizontally affected markets
(179)     Based on the market shares provided in Section 6.3, the Transaction results in several
          horizontally affected markets and potential market segments at different levels of the
          theatrical and AV value chains, considering all potential product market segmentations
          and their geographic scope.

6.4.3.      The theatrical distribution value chain - Assessment of unilateral effects
(183)       As set out in Sections 5.2 and 5.3 above, the Commission has previously found that
            separate markets exist for (i) the production and licensing of films for theatrical release,
            and (ii) the distribution of films to cinemas.
(184)       The impact of the Transaction on the market for the production and licensing of US films
            for theatrical release will be assessed in Section 6.4.3.1. The impact of the Transaction
            on the market for the distribution of US films to cinemas will be assessed in
            Section 6.4.3.2. (183)

6.4.3.1. Production and licensing of US films for theatrical release

6.4.3.1.1         The Notifying Party’s views
(185)       The Notifying Party submits that the horizontal overlap between Paramount and Warner
            as regards the production and licensing of films for theatrical release will not lead to any
            competition concerns for the following reasons.
(186)       First, the Notifying Party submits that the rise of subscription streaming services and the
            entry of large technology companies into content production and distribution have
            expanded competition. As a result of this, the concepts of a ‘big five’ / ‘legacy’ studio
            would be outdated.
(187)       Second, the Notifying Party submits that the model by which studios distributed most of
            their films through cinemas, before turning to rental and other windows, has changed by
            the shift in audience consumption habits. According to the Notifying Party, streaming
            has surpassed cinemas in 2022 and many consumers now view new feature films on a
            small screen (e.g. TV, tablet) via streaming services.
(188)       Third, the Notifying Party submits that the dynamics in the market mean that no film
            studio can accumulate or sustain market power over time. Film performance is inherently
            uncertain, and all films would be substitutable. According to the Notifying Party,
            competition takes place film-by-film and consumers do not base purchasing and viewing
            decisions based on the name of the studio. This is reflected in box office revenues of film
            studios, which are inherently volatile. (184)
(189)       In relation to complaints that the Transaction may result in a reduction of theatrical output
            by the Parties post-merger, the Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will not lead
            to any competition concerns for the following reasons.
(190)       Firstly, the Notifying Party submits that a hypothesis of a reduction in theatrical output
            does not accurately reflect the economics of the film industry. The Notifying Party argues
            that the idea that a studio could withhold a film and recapture its audience through other
            releases presumes close substitutability and predictable performance, which does not
            reflect the nature of the film industry. The Parties’ profit maximization strategy involves

(182)    Commission Notice on a simplified treatment for certain concentrations under Council Regulation (EC)
         No 139/2004 on the control of concentrations between undertakings 2023/C 160/01, OJ C 160, 5.5.2023,
         p. 1 (‘Notice on Simplified Treatment’).
(183)    As set out at paragraph 180 above, on an overall market for the distribution of films to cinemas, the only
         affected market is Iceland. Iceland will hereafter be covered when discussing the narrower market for the
         distribution of US studios’ films to cinemas, where the Parties’ shares are higher. An absence of concern on
         such market logically extends to the wider market for the distribution of films to cinemas.
(184)    Reply to RFI 20, paragraph 31; reply to RFI 24, paragraph 4.
                                                             44
            having a portfolio of theatrical releases across a broad range of genres, as (i) the
            performance and profitability of a film are ex ante uncertain; (ii) consumer preferences
            are themselves highly heterogeneous and differentiated; and (iii) releasing films
            theatrically is a key milestone or signal of a film’s revenue-generating prospects. (185)
(191)       Secondly, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties have no incentive to decrease
            theatrical output to benefit their own SVOD offerings. The Parties do not treat these two
            distribution channels as substitutes; they form part of an integrated value chain in which
            each window reinforces the others. The decision to move forward with a film is based on
            the anticipated revenue from each window. Therefore, releasing a film straight to
            streaming fundamentally undermines the lifetime profitability prospects of a title. A
            theatrical release functions as a critical promotional mechanism and a signal of quality
            that supports other windows. (186)
(192)       Thirdly, the Notifying Party submits that the theatrical production market is and will
            remain vibrantly competitive post-merger, which reinforces investment incentives. There
            is a broad range of rival studios who are capable of delivering in-demand theatrical
            content to both theatres and audiences, including major US studios, as well as other US
            and non-US film production firms. (187)
(193)       Fourthly, the Notifying Party submits that any comparison to the acquisition by The Walt
            Disney Company of sole control of parts of Twenty-First Century Fox, Inc. (M.8785,
            Disney/Fox), and the supposed reduction in film output resulting from this transaction is
            misplaced. (188)

6.4.3.1.2         Commission’s assessment
(194)       The Commission considers that, for the reasons set out below, the Transaction does not
            raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of
            horizontal unilateral effects on the market for the production and licensing of US films
            for theatrical release for the following reasons:
(195)       First, the Commission notes that the Parties’ combined market share in the EEA will
            remain just above [20-30]% and post-Transaction the merged entity’s market shares will
            be comparable to Disney [20-30]% and Universal [10-20]% (Table 1). The Commission
            considered average market shares for the years 2023-2025, given that market shares are
            inherently volatile as they reflect the box office success of film studios’ film slate in any
            given year. Taking into account an average market share for the years 2023-2025, the
            Transaction gives rise to an affected market in the EEA [20-30]% (Table 1) (189). On a
            market for the production and licensing of US films for theatrical release, the Transaction
            does not give rise to an affected EEA market in 2025. (190) Said market shares are based
            on annual box office revenue, which, according to the majority of film studios,

         distributors and cinemas, is the best metric to compare the competitive strength of a film
         studio (191).
(196)    The Commission further notes that the post-merger HHI amounts to [1800-2000] (192)
         and the HHI delta amounts to [200-400] on the affected market for the period 2023-2025.
         The Commission considers, in line with paragraphs 19-21 of the Horizontal Merger
         Guidelines, that this does not in itself indicate that the Transaction would not give rise to
         competition concerns. Therefore, a further analysis of the structural indicators of the
         Parties’ market power is required.
(197)    Second, the Commission considers that the Parties’ market power would not be different
         if one were to focus on the three parameters that are most important for a film studio to
         be successful. According to film studios and distributors, the three most important
         parameters are, respectively, (i) the access to valuable IP; (ii) the ability to attract talent;
         and (iii) the overall production budget for the slate of film releases. (193) For these
         parameters, the Commission considered the Parties’ market power on a worldwide basis.
         Market participants indicate that, on their own, neither Paramount nor Warner are
         considered to be the main competitor on any of these three parameters.
(198)    In the first place, as regards the films based on existing IP (franchises), which is important
         as it may lead to spin-offs and (further) development of franchises, film studios and
         distributors rank Warner and Paramount as respectively 3rd and 5th strongest players on
         the market. (194) When considering the top 100 highest-grossing films in the EEA
         between 2023 and 2025, Warner ranks 2nd in terms of the number of franchise films,
         while Paramount ranks 4th. However, 10 other studios also produced franchise films that
         featured among the top 100 highest-grossing films. Additionally, focusing only on
         franchise films released during the 2023-2025 period may overlook important franchise
         IP that was not exploited during that timeframe (e.g. the James Bond franchise). It should
         also be noted that the definition of a franchise, although widely used in the industry, is
         not clear-cut and may be applied differently across market participants. Furthermore,
         beyond film franchise IP (such as for example the Star Wars franchise), studios may rely
         on a range of other IP, including video games, comics and novels, as the basis for
         franchise films. (195)
(199)    In the second place, as regards the ability to attract talent (such as directors, actors,
         screenwriters etc.), film studios and distributors rank Warner as 3rd strongest competitor.
         In addition to Universal, Disney and Warner, film studios and distributors also rank A24
         and Apple ahead of Paramount. The results of the market investigation are unclear
         whether Sony or Paramount should be considered as the 6th strongest player when it
         comes to its ability to attract talent. (196)
(200)    In the third place, as regards the overall production budget for the slate of film releases,
         film studios and distributors rank Warner and Paramount as respectively 3rd and 4th
         strongest players on the market. (197) The Parties have provided worldwide market shares
         based on the overall theatrical production budget of film studios. These figures indicate
         that, on average between 2023 and 2025, Warner and Paramount are respectively the 2nd
         and 4th strongest film studio based on production budget. In any event, both in 2025 and

         over the period 2023-2025, the combined theatrical production budget of the Parties
         remains smaller than that of Disney. (198)
(201)    Third, the Commission assessed whether the Parties’ market power would be different if
         one were to focus on the production budget that film studios have available for the
         production of so-called blockbusters. Blockbusters are particularly important for
         cinemas. The International Union of Cinemas (‘UNIC’) explained that ‘for blockbuster
         titles, even the largest exhibitors cannot credibly refuse supply without incurring
         substantial and unrecoverable losses in attendance and revenue given the importance of
         every single blockbuster to cinema revenues’. (199) However, there is no universally
         accepted definition of what constitutes a blockbuster. UNIC indicated that, from a
         forward-looking perspective, it is general industry understanding that blockbusters are
         films with a production budget of USD 100 million and above. (200) The Parties provided
         worldwide market shares based on the total production budget for films with a production
         budget greater than or equal to USD 100 million. For the period 2023-2025, Warner and
         Paramount rank respectively as the 2nd and 4th strongest competitor based on films with
         a production budget greater than or equal to USD 100 million. Combined, the Parties’
         market share in 2025 and average market share for 2023-2025 would remain smaller than
         that of the strongest competitor, Disney. (201)
(202)    Fourth, the Commission considers that the Parties will continue to face competition from
         several well-established major US studios (Disney, Universal and Sony). Further, so-
         called mini-major studios (e.g. A24 and Lionsgate) as well as tech companies (Amazon
         and Apple) that have recently entered the market (in the case of Amazon, by acquiring
         film studio MGM), will also continue to exert a competitive constraint on the Parties.
(203)    In the first place, the Commission considers that several US film studios have been able
         to compete with the Parties in the last three years. The majority of film studios and
         distributors that responded to the market investigation indicated that Universal, Disney,
         Sony, local film studios and Amazon have competed with the Parties. In addition, a
         significant number of respondents indicate that Lionsgate and A24 also competed with
         the Parties in the last three years. (202) The majority of cinemas that responded to the
         market investigation also indicated that Universal, Disney and Sony competed with the
         Parties in the last three years. (203) In addition, a significant number of responding
         cinemas indicated that Amazon, Lionsgate, A24 and Apple also competed with the
         Parties. (204)
(204)    In the second place, the Commission considers that the same competitors will continue
         to compete with the Parties in the next three years. The majority of film studios and
         distributors that responded to the market investigation indicated that Universal, Disney,
         Sony and Amazon MGM will continue to compete with the Parties in the three years
         following the Transaction. A significant number of film studios and distributors also
         mention Lionsgate and A24 as players that will continue to exert a competitive constraint
         on the Parties. (205) The majority of cinemas that responded to the market investigation
         also indicated that Universal, Disney and Sony competed with the Parties in the next
         three years. In addition, a significant number of responding cinemas indicated that

(205)    In the third place, the Commission assessed for each of the competitors mentioned above
         what their individual market position is as well as their strength on the main competitive
         parameters. This confirms the results from the market investigation that these film studios
         exert competitive pressure on the Parties.
         (1)    The Walt Disney Company is a global entertainment company that produces and
                distributes films for theatrical release, and it owns its own streaming platform
                Disney+. Its film studios and brands include Walt Disney Pictures, 20th Century
                Studios, Searchlight Pictures, Marvel Studios, Lucasfilm, Walt Disney Animation
                Studios and Pixar.
                Considering the average 2023-2025 market share in the EEA, Disney is bigger than
                the Parties combined. Moreover, most film studios and distributors responding to
                the market investigation indicate that Disney is the strongest competitor on the
                most important parameter for a studio to be successful: films based on existing IP
                (franchises). (207) Disney’s franchises include, among others, the Marvel Cinematic
                Universe, Star Wars, Toy Story and Avatar. Notable film releases from the past
                three years include Inside Out 2, Zootopia 2, Deadpool & Wolverine, Moana 2,
                Lilo & Stitch, Guardians of the Galaxy vol. 3 and Avatar: Fire and Ash. The market
                investigation further reveals that film studios and distributors rank Disney and
                Universal as the strongest film studios in their ability to attract talent. Finally,
                Disney is considered to be the strongest competitor with regard to the size of its
                average production budget. (208) Indeed, Disney’s total theatrical production
                budget is the largest of any competitor in 2025 and on average for 2023-2025, and
                larger than that of the Parties combined (Table 2). (209) Also when looking at the
                production of blockbuster movies (see paragraph (201) above), Disney is the
                largest competitor in each of the last three years and will remain larger than the
                Parties combined. (210)
         (2)    NBC Universal is a global media and entertainment group that, among others,
                produces and distributes films for theatrical release. Its film studios include
                Universal Pictures, Focus Features, Working Title Films and the animation studios
                Illumination and DreamWorks Animation (hereafter together referred to as
                ‘Universal’).
                Following the Transaction, Universal will be the third largest player after Disney
                and the merged entity in the EEA for the period 2023-2025, based on market shares
                by box office revenues. The results of the market investigation show that film
                studios and distributors consider that Universal is the second strongest competitor,
                after Disney, when it relates to films based on IP (franchises). Universal’s
                franchises include Jurassic Park, Fast & Furious, Despicable Me (including
                Minions), and Mario. Notable film releases from the past three years, apart from
                films in the beforementioned franchises, include Oppenheimer, Wicked (and
                Wicked: For Good), Kung Fu Panda 4, Bridget Jones: Mad About the Boy,
                Downton Abbey: the Grand Finale and How to Train Your Dragon. Moreover, film
                studios and distributors consider that Universal is the strongest competitor, together
                with Disney, in its ability to attract talent. Finally, Universal is considered to be the

         (5)    A24 is an independent American entertainment company, founded in 2012, that

                While A24 has a small market share on average for the period 2023-2025 ([0-5]%

                highest number of titles after the ‘big five’ studios. (224) Third, A24 starts producing
                films with bigger budgets. In 2026, it was reported that A24 started the production

                release under the Amazon MGM Studios banner and through the revived Orion
                Pictures and United Artists-heritage labels (hereafter together referred to as
                ‘Amazon/MGM’).
                Amazon/MGM’s market share, based on box office revenues, for the period
                2023-2025 is currently small ([0-5]% in the EEA overall) (Table 1) (226). The
                Commission notes however that Amazon only closed the acquisition of MGM in
                2022 at the end of the Covid-19 pandemic and a year before the writers’ strike that
                impacted the American film industry in 2023. Film studios and distributors
                consider that Amazon/MGM ranks behind the ‘big five’ studios and Lionsgate
                when it relates to films based on IP (franchises). (227) Amazon/MGM’s franchises
                include in particular James Bond, as well as Rocky, Creed and Legally Blonde.
                Notable releases in the past three years include American Fiction, Challengers and
                After the Hunt. Most recently, Amazon/MGM released the film Project Hail Mary.
                As a non-sequel and non-franchise film, it currently ranks as the third highest-
                grossing film worldwide in 2026. (228) While Amazon/MGM only announced its
                first full theatrical slate after the acquisition of MGM in 2026, its ability to attract
                talent is demonstrated by the fact that Project Hail Mary is starring and produced
                by Ryan Gosling. In relation to the size of the average production budget, film

                studios and distributors rank Amazon/MGM to be approximately as strong as
                Paramount (behind Disney, Universal, Warner and Sony). (229) Amazon/MGM’s
                strength in relation to its production budget is also demonstrated through Project
                Hail Mary, which had a production budget of USD 200 million. (230) Finally,
                Amazon has announced that it aims to release 14 films in theatres in 2026 (231) and

         (7)    Apple produces original films under the Apple Original Films label. While it
                produces TV series and films primarily for Apple TV+, Apple also produces films
                for theatrical release.
                Apple has a small market share on average for the period 2023-2025 in the EEA
                overall ([0-5]%) (Table 1) (233). Film studios and distributors do not consider Apple
                to be a strong competitor in relation to films based on existing IP (franchises). (234)
                This is also clear from Apple’s notable releases in the past three years which are
                not based on existing IP: Killers of the flower moon, Napoleon, Argylle and F1:
                The Movie. Apple is ranked as the fifth strongest competitor (after Disney,
                Universal, Warner and A24) in its ability to attract talent. (235) This is reflected in
                the fact that Brad Pitt is the main actor in F1: The Movie, whereas Killers of the
                flower moon and Napoleon are directed by respectively Martin Scorsese and Ridley
                Scott. (236) Finally, film studios and distributors do not consider Apple to be a
                strong competitor in relation to the size of the average production budget. (237)
                However, Apple had the biggest theatrical production budget for the period 2023-
                2025 after the ‘big five’ studios and Lionsgate. In addition, when focussing on films
                with a theatrical production budget over USD 100 million (see paragraph (201)
                above), Apple was the largest competitor in the period 2023-2025 after the ‘big
                five’ studios. (238)
(206)    Based on the above, the Commission considers that the Parties will continue to face
         competition from several players on an EEA-wide market for the production and
         licensing of US films for theatrical release.
(207)    Fifth, the Commission considers that, while the Parties are close competitors, there is no
         indication that they would compete more closely with one another than with other major
         US studios, for the reasons set out below.
(208)    In the first place, and as set out in paragraph 199, above, film studios and distributors
         indicated in the market investigation that the access to valuable IP, the ability to attract
         talent and the overall production budget for the slate of film releases are the most

         important parameters for a film studio to be successful. (239) On each of these three
         parameters, film studios and distributors consider that Disney, Universal and Warner are
         the three strongest competitors. (240) In relation to films based on existing IP and the
         average production budget, Paramount and Sony rank closely as the fourth and fifth
         strongest competitor, whereas Paramount is considered to be a much weaker competitor
         than Disney, Universal, Warner and Sony in relation to its ability to attract talent. (241)
(209)    In the second place, at least each of the ‘big five’ film studios as well as Lionsgate and
         Amazon (can) rely on existing IP.
(210)    In the third place, in relation to the frequency of output, the Commission notes that all
         ‘big five’ studios released between 10 and 18 films in the EEA in each year between
         2023 and 2025 (with Universal releasing 19 films in 2023 and 23 films in 2025). In
         addition, A24 released 9 films in 2024 and 9 films in 2025. (242) Further, as set out in
         paragraph (205) above, Amazon MGM has announced that it aims to release 14 films in
         theatres in 2026 (243) and 15 films per year by 2027. (244) The Commission sees no reason
         why these films would not also be released in the EEA.
(211)    In the fourth place, while the Parties both invest in blockbusters, this applies to all
         competitors that are discussed in paragraph 205 above. Further, the production of
         blockbusters is not limited to said competitors. For example, Lightstorm Entertainment,
         which produces in particular the Avatar franchise, also produced several films in the last
         years that classify as blockbusters. (245)
(212)    Sixth, the Commission considers that, while the market for the production and supply of
         films for theatrical release is characterised by significant barriers to entry and expansion,
         the following evidence shows that these barriers are not insurmountable.
(213)    In the first place, while (branches of) the ‘big five’ film studios are all at least 100 years
         old, the founding and subsequent success of ‘mini-majors’ such as Lionsgate (founded
         in 1997) and A24 (founded in 2012) show that entry into the market for the production
         and supply of films for theatrical release is possible.
(214)    In the second place, several large tech companies that were active in adjacent markets
         have recently entered the market. Amazon acquired MGM in 2022. Apple founded Apple
         Studios in 2019 and has produced films for theatrical release since 2023.
(215)    In the third place, the Commission considers that while access to valuable IP (franchises)
         is the most important parameter to be successful, companies such as Apple and A24 show
         that successful entry without access to such IP is possible.
(216)    In the fourth place, the Commission tested in its market investigation whether vertical
         integration, that is, having one’s own distribution arm, is an important parameter to be
         successful. Film studios and distributors consider that vertical integration is a less
         important parameter for a film studio to be successful than the access to valuable IP, the
         ability to attract talent and the overall production budget. (246)

6.4.3.1.2.1.    Reduction of output
         During its investigation, the Commission received complaints from cinemas that the
         Transaction may result in a reduction of the number of films that the Parties would release
         in cinemas. For example, one cinema stated: (247) ‘Industry experience suggests that
         consolidation among major studios can lead to a reduction in overall theatrical output
         over time. For example, following the Disney-20th Century Fox transaction, the number
         of theatrical releases from the acquired studio significantly decreased compared to
         historical levels. A similar dynamic may occur in this case, although the extent and timing
         of any such effect remain uncertain’.
(217)    These concerns contradict the repeated public statements by Paramount to the opposite.
         During CinemaCon 2026, the largest annual industry gathering, its CEO stated in a
         presentation: ‘The second reason I came here today is I wanted to look every single one
         of you in the eye and give you my word: once we combine with Warner Bros., we are
         going to make a minimum of 30 films annually, across both studios. And every film –
         every film – will receive a full theatrical release, with a minimum 45-day window starting
         today’ (emphasis added). (248) In the first quarter Earnings Call for Paramount, its CEO
         similarly stated: ‘We are firmly committed to 30 theatrical films per year. If you look at
         the schedule, we have 15films [sic] on the calendar to release this year, up from 8 last
         year. So we’ve come close to basically doubling the output of our film studio, Paramount.
         If you look at the great job that the team at WBD has done, they have 15 films on the
         release calendar for this year. So the 2 companies are actually making 30 films to date,
         which Ithink [sic] is important to note and accelerating’ (emphasis added). (249)
(218)    Despite Paramount CEO’s public statements, and in light of the complaints it received,
         the Commission assesses whether the merged entity will have the incentive to reduce the
         number of films released as a result of the Transaction. (250)
(219)    While cinemas have expressed concerns that the Parties may reduce their combined film
         output following the proposed Transaction, the responses of film studios and distributors
         are more nuanced. While most of them indicated that the Parties have the incentive to do
         so, many were of the opposite view or considered that it would depend on the conditions
         of the merger. (251)
(220)    In view of the respondents’ replies, the Commission’s assessment focuses on the
         following elements: (i) Disney’s reduction of film output following its acquisition of
         Twenty-First Century Fox in 2019 (252); (ii) the fact that the merged entity would carry a
         significant amount of debt, which may force the Parties to reduce film output (253); and
         (iii) whether the combined current annual film output of Paramount and Warner would
         exceed what is economically optimal for the merged entity.

        accessed on 30 June 2026).

        on 30 June 2026).
(250)   Paragraph 32 of the Horizontal Merger Guidelines states in this regard: ‘When market conditions are such
        that the competitors of the merging parties are unlikely to increase their supply substantially if prices
        increase, the merging firms may have an incentive to reduce output below the combined pre-merger levels,
        thereby raising market prices. The merger increases the incentive to reduce output by giving the merged
        firm a larger base of sales on which to enjoy the higher margins resulting from an increase induced by the
        output reduction’.
(251)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1a Theatrical distribution value chain’, questions D.B.6 and D.B.6.1.
(252)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1a Theatrical distribution value chain’, question E.2.1.
(253)   UNIC, White Paper, 13 May 2026, paras. 153-155.
                                                           53
(221)    First, the Commission considers that Disney’s reduction of film output following its
         acquisition of Twenty-First Century Fox does not in itself constitute proof that the Parties
         would have the incentive to reduce film output following the Transaction.
(222)    In the first place, the Commission notes that Disney has a significantly different business
         model than the Parties. Disney is part of The Walt Disney Company. In addition to The
         Walt Disney Company’s entertainment business segment, which includes its activities in
         the theatrical distribution and AV value chains, a large part of The Walt Disney
         Company’s revenues is generated by the Parks, Experiences and Products business
         segment which includes its entertainment parks, cruise line and consumer products. (254)
         Hence, The Walt Disney Company has many more ways to monetize its IP than the
         Parties. As a result, the Parties are more dependent on film revenue than The Walt Disney
         Company.
(223)    In the second place, Disney’s acquisition of Twenty-First Century Fox had a different
         business rationale. As Bob Iger, Disney’s CEO at the time, explained, Disney evaluated
         the acquisition ‘through the lens of [its] future business’ with the objective of
         ‘accelerating [its] transformation into the DTC space on a global basis’. To achieve this,
         Disney intended to use Fox’s content library to ‘further enrich the content mix on [its]
         DTC platforms’ and refocus Fox’s studios on producing films for theatrical release as
         well as for Disney+ and Hulu. (255).
(224)    In the third place, the acquisition of Twenty-First Century Fox in 2019 was followed by
         the Covid-19 pandemic and later by the US writers’ strike in 2023. This has influenced
         the output of US films in general but also had an influence on the production and release
         strategies of said studios. These two exogeneous shocks make it hard to isolate any
         potential merger-driven reduction in theatrical output.
(225)    Firstly, the Covid-19 pandemic had a consequential negative impact on the industry for
         a significant period, as regards both film shootings and their release in cinemas, given
         the strict rules that applied in terms of masks, vaccination and social distancing. When
         studios were recovering from the adverse impacts of the pandemic, they were hit by the
         industry strikes in 2023. These have impacted all film studios, including Disney.
(226)    Secondly, the Commission notes that the Covid-19 pandemic also resulted in Disney
         deciding to release certain films directly on its streaming platform, which was launched
         in late 2019 and early 2020, depending on the market. In this regard, it is important to
         note that Disney has a significantly stronger position in the retail supply of SVOD
         services than the Parties, as set out in paras. (454) and (459) below. More importantly,
         Disney has reversed course after the Covid-19 pandemic. As noted by UNIC, ‘Disney is
         committed to theatrical releases, and while it released its films directly on its streaming
         service Disney+ during COVID-19, now the release of their new films starts with an
         exclusive theatrical window in cinemas’ (256).
(227)    Thirdly, the considerations set out above make it difficult to isolate and assess any
         potential merger-driven reduction in output, as any observed reduction could equally
         reflect a strategic business decision by Disney rather than an anticompetitive effect of the
         merger. Indeed, when considering the overall number of film releases (rather than just
         theatrical releases), Disney’s output increased by 10% in the post-merger period

        https://investors.thewaltdisneycompany.com/financials/annual-reports/default.aspx (last accessed on 26
        June 2026).
(255)   The Walt Disney Company, Q3 FY19 Earnings Conference Call Transcript, available at
        https://s206.q4cdn.com/979796730/files/doc events/2019/08/q3-fy19-earnings-transcript.pdf (last accessed
        on 7 July 2026).
(256)   Non-confidential version of the minutes of a call with UNIC, dated 15 January 2026, paragraph 8a.
                                                          54
         (2019-2025) compared to the pre-merger period (2012-2018). (257) Moreover, sensitivity
         analyses of Disney’s theatrical output before and after the merger highlight the difficulty
         of isolating any merger-specific effects, given the significant disruptions that affected the
         film industry during the relevant period and their impact on both production and release
         schedules. Given the long production cycles of theatrical films, the effects of these
         disruptions may persist for several years. (258)
(228)    Second, the Commission considers that the merged entity’s high level of indebtedness
         does not in itself mean that the merged entity will have the incentive to reduce its film
         output.
(229)    In the first place, the merged entity is dependent on the commercial success of its films
         for a large part of its revenues. In this respect, most film studios that responded to the
         market investigation indicated that a theatrical release is profitable in itself and will result
         in higher revenues from subsequent licensing than a direct-to-consumer release. (259)
         Therefore, a reduction in output of films for theatrical release appears unlikely as it would
         not make economic sense. Further, a review of the internal deal documents did not reveal
         any plans to reduce film production or theatrical releases either for Paramount or for
         Warner. (260)
(230)    In the second place, Warner was already a highly leveraged company prior to the merger.
         However, this did not prevent it from successfully producing new films and TV series.
         Further, the Commission understands that co-financing and co-production arrangements
         in the film and TV industries are typically structured on a project-by-project basis.
         Investment decisions are driven primarily by the commercial merits of the individual
         project, rather than by the debt leverage of the producing studio. As a result, investors
         are principally exposed to the risks associated with the specific project, rather than to the
         financial position of the producing studio. Accordingly, the merged entity’s level of
         indebtedness is not expected to materially affect its ability to finance new films. (261)
(231)    Third, the Commission considers that there is no reliable way to establish what the
         merged entity’s optimal annual film output would be and hence, whether a combination
         of Warner’s and Paramount’s film output would exceed it.
(232)    In the first place, the Commission notes that there is no generally accepted view of what
         a film studio’s optimal annual film output would be. Further, film studios do release
         several films at or around the same time and would even do so for films of the same
         genre.
(233)    Firstly, the market investigation confirms that, when setting a release date, film studios
         and/or distributors mainly factor in the genre and target audience of other films in
         cinemas. (262) However, several respondents indicated that more factors are considered
         when releasing a film. (263)
(234)    Secondly, most responding film studios and distributors indicated that a film studio could
         have overlapping release windows for its films, whereas most of them added the caveat
         that this was provided the target audience or genre is different. (264). However, the latter
         must be nuanced as half of film studios and distributors expressing an opinion indicated
         that they would release a film whose release window overlaps with that of another of

         their film of the same genre. (265) One respondent explained that ‘[i]t depends on the
         genre and mostly on the potential of such genre for the time of the year. There are niche
         films on which it would be risky to do that. But there are films that playing at the same
         time, will benefit both. The decision would not be different if both films are from our
         release’. (266)
(235)    In the second place, the Commission notes that it found no plans to reduce film
         production or theatrical releases either for Paramount or for Warner in the internal
         documents. (267) The Commission further notes that, prior to the Transaction, the overlap
         of the theatrical releases of Paramount and Warner was less than the overlap of each Party
         with the theatrical releases of the other ‘big five’ film studios. (268)
(236)    The Commission therefore considers that the evidence on file does not support the view
         that the Transaction would result in a reduction of output.

6.4.3.1.2.2.   Shortening of theatrical windows
(237)    During its investigation, the Commission received complaints from cinemas on the
         potential impact of the Transaction on the diversity of films available to European
         audiences. According to UNIC, the merged entity would be incentivised to reduce the
         length of the theatrical window. (269)
(238)    The Commission considers it unlikely that the merged entity would shorten the length of
         the theatrical window of its films, in essence for two reasons. First, the market
         investigation has indicated that the commercial success of a film in cinemas is crucial to
         its subsequent success in the audiovisual value chain. (270). A shortening of the theatrical
         window is therefore likely to negatively impact the commercial success of films in
         cinemas. Second, the strategy and/or statements of all other major US studios confirm
         that a shortening of the theatrical window does not make economic sense. Disney has
         explained that its films are often subject to a theatrical window of at least 45 days. (271)
         Further, public sources referred to by the Notifying Party indicate that Universal is in the
         process of extending the theatrical window for its films to approximately 45 days in 2027,
         and that Sony is a vocal proponent of longer windows. (272) The Notifying Party also
         explained that Universal experimented with shorter windows but concluded that this was
         unprofitable and reverted to longer windows. (273)

6.4.3.1.2.3.   Loss of diversity
(239)    During its investigation, the Commission received complaints from cinemas on the
         potential impact of the Transaction on the diversity of films available to European
         audiences. According to UNIC, the merged entity would be incentivised to reduce
         original and mid-budget productions. (274) UNIC noted that the financial incentives
         resulting from the Transaction, including the need to realise efficiencies, would likely
         reduce the merged entity’s appetite for creative risk-taking. (275)

(240)    UNIC further submitted that such a strategy could have adverse effects on local film
         production in the EEA. In particular, it argued that Warner currently plays an important
         role in the co-production and financing of local European films, amongst others in
         France, Germany and Poland. (276) According to UNIC, a reduction in original and mid-
         budget productions could therefore lead to lower investment in local productions and
         reduce the diversity of films available to European audiences, with a particular impact
         on independent cinemas, whose programming relies on a balanced pipeline of
         blockbuster, original and locally produced films. (277)
(241)    The Commission notes that the evidence on the file does not support the concern that the
         Transaction would result in a reduction of local film production in the EEA or a
         significant reduction in the production of original or mid-budget films. In particular, the
         Parties submitted evidence showing that both already maintain a significant pipeline of
         European productions extending beyond the implementation of the Transaction. Warner
         identified […] local EEA theatrical productions currently planned for release between
         2026 and 2029. (278) Likewise, Paramount submitted that its current theatrical slate
         extends through 2027 and includes a substantial pipeline of EEA productions and that
         this pipeline would continue beyond 2027. (279)
(242)    The Commission further notes that both Parties explained that investment decisions
         relating to European productions are made on a project-by-project basis, taking into
         account factors such as the quality of the script, its commercial potential and its strategic
         fit, rather than pursuant to geographically allocated budgets. (280) The Parties also
         explained that they currently rely on well-established local production, distribution and
         development teams across several Member States to identify, develop and co-produce
         European content and that these arrangements are expected to continue post-
         Transaction. (281)
(243)    The Commission has assessed not only whether the Parties have European productions
         in their current pipeline, but whether the merged entity would have the incentive to
         continue investing in such productions to the same extent as the Parties would have
         absent the Transaction. It considers that those incentives are unlikely to diminish, for two
         reasons. First, the Parties submitted that local and European content constitutes an
         important element of their competitive offering, particularly in light of the strong
         competition from other global streaming providers. (282) They further explained that the
         merged entity will continue to require a steady pipeline of new European content to
         attract and retain subscribers and to compete effectively across theatrical distribution and
         streaming services. (283) Second, the merged entity’s investment in European works is
         not solely a matter of commercial discretion. As set out at paragraphs (364) to (366)
         and (377), broadcasters and on-demand service providers in the Member States
         concerned are subject to European-works quotas and statutory investment obligations,
         which apply irrespective of the Transaction and constrain the extent to which the merged
         entity could reduce its investment in European productions below the level that would
         have prevailed absent the Transaction. (284)

(244)       The Commission therefore considers that the evidence on file does not support the view
            that the Transaction would be expected to reduce investment in European film production
            or materially diminish the diversity of films available to European audiences.

6.4.3.1.3         Conclusion
(245)       For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction does not raise
            serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal
            effects on the overall market for the production and licensing of US films for theatrical
            release in the EEA.

6.4.3.2. Distribution of US films for theatrical release to cinemas (285)
(246)       The Parties are active in the distribution of US films to cinemas throughout the EEA.
(247)       Paramount self-distributes its films in France, Spain and Ireland, and distributes via UIP,
            a jointly controlled JV between Paramount and Universal, in Denmark, Hungary,
            Norway, Poland and Sweden (these offices are referred to as ‘UIP OpCos’). In a number
            of EEA countries, UIP sub-licenses its distribution activities to a local distributor that
            distributes both of Paramount’s and Universal’s portfolios (‘UIP Shared Licensee’):
            Bulgaria, Cyprus, Czechia, Estonia, Finland, Latvia, Lithuania, Romania, Slovakia, and
            Slovenia. In Croatia, Greece, Iceland, and Portugal, UIP sub-licenses its distribution
            activities to local distributors as well. However, in these countries Paramount and
            Universal use different licensees (‘UIP Non-Shared Licensee’, together with UIP
            Shared Licensee: ‘UIP Licensees’). In the remaining countries, Paramount relies on
            distribution via third-party distributors or studios. (286)
(248)       Warner self-distributes in Belgium, Denmark, Finland, France, Germany, Ireland, Italy,
            Liechtenstein, Luxembourg, Netherlands, Norway, Poland, Spain, Sweden. In other EEA
            countries, Warner uses third-party distributors. (287)
(249)       Paramount mostly distributes its own films [details of the Parties’ distribution strategy]
            and UIP exclusively distributes Paramount and Universal films, except for Poland, where
            UIP acts as the theatrical distributor for [third-party studio]. (288) Warner may also
            distribute films for other studios, such as [third-party studio] as well as local EEA-based
            production companies. (289)

6.4.3.2.1         Combination of the Parties’ film portfolios

6.4.3.2.1.1.      The Notifying Party’s views
(250)       The Notifying Party submits that the horizontal overlap between Paramount and Warner
            on the market for the distribution of films to cinemas will not lead to any competition
            concerns for a number of reasons that, according to the Notifying Party, apply both at



        independent producers and over 50 broadcasters and streaming services in the Union on commissioning and
        acquisition practices; and ‘The European Media Industry Outlook 2025’, page 63.
(285)   The Transaction also gives rise to an affected market for distribution of all films in Iceland, however, given
        that Iceland is already affected in the market for distribution of US films, this Decision does not discuss the
        market for the “distribution of all films” separately. Given that the Commission can exclude concerns at the
        narrow market for distribution of US films by virtue of the combination of the Parties’ film portfolios, there
        are also no concerns on a market for distribution of all films, given that the Parties are not active in the
        market for non-US films.
(286)   Form CO, paragraphs 213-220.
(287)   Form CO, paragraph 387.
(288)   Form CO, footnote 180.
(289)   Form CO, paragraph 387 and Annex 1 to RFI 4.
                                                             58
         EEA and national level, as well as equally to the distribution of US and non-US films for
         theatrical release.
(251)    First, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties’ combined market shares are low to
         modest across EEA countries in a market for the distribution of all films, or moderate
         when looking at a market for the distribution of US films only. (290)
(252)    Second, the Notifying Party considers that the merged entity will continue to face
         competition from multiple rival international and national distributors in each of the
         affected markets, whether for the distribution of all films or US films only. The Notifying
         Party refers to the presence of distribution arms of US studios such as Disney, Sony,
         Universal, as well as vertically integrated European production studios and local/regional
         distributors with strong local relationships and capabilities. (291)
(253)    Third, the Notifying Party submits that Paramount and Warner have a different focus,
         with materially different slates and positioning and cinemas viewing Warner as
         competing more closely with other leading studios such as Disney and Universal than
         with Paramount. (292)
(254)    Fourth, the Notifying Party submits that market dynamics and contracting practices with
         cinema cinemas constrain the Parties’ behaviour and that the distribution market is
         volatile and hit-driven. Furthermore, the Notifying Party explains that cinemas
         multisource across competing film slates, with films booked on an individual basis,
         securing rights for international and locally distributed titles, which are booked in parallel
         and reallocated week-to-week (screens and show times) based on each titles’ actual box
         office performance. According to the Notifying Party, the breadth of a distributors’ film
         portfolio is unrelated to the booking of films. As such, cinemas also have regard to the
         availability of other films and content, and the Parties would not be able to worsen
         exhibition terms. (293)
(255)    In the Notifying Party’s view, cinemas large and small can exercise considerable buyer
         power in negotiations about, among other, rental terms, screen allocations and marketing
         commitments. Failing to secure exhibition at a given cinema loses access to the audience
         this cinema serves, with financial consequences extending beyond the theatrical window
         alone as the box office performance determines downstream revenue. (294)
(256)    Fifth, the Notifying Party submits that barriers to entry and expansion in the market for
         theatrical film distribution are low across the EEA, further mitigating any risk of
         anticompetitive effects. According to the Notifying Party, new entrants – whether studios
         or independent distributors – can readily enter through flexible arrangements, including
         single-title licenses, service deals, or territory-specific distribution agreements, with
         studios often running competitive, film-by-film processes that enable smaller players to
         secure mandates. The Notifying Party notes that local distributors routinely use territorial
         expertise to distribute major studio films, and that success depends on industry
         knowledge, negotiation skills and marketing expertise, which can be acquired through
         hiring. (295)

6.4.3.2.1.2.   The Commission’s assessment
(257)    As a preliminary remark, as discussed in Section 5.3, the Commission considers that the
         relevant competitive assessment should focus on the overall distribution of US films for

(290)   Form CO, paragraphs 767 and 771.
(291)   Form CO, paragraphs 780-782.
(292)   Form CO, paragraph 790.
(293)   Form CO, paragraphs 791-794.
(294)   Form CO, paragraphs 795-803.
(295)   Form CO, paragraphs 805-812.
                                                     59
         theatrical release to cinemas. From a cinema’s perspective, the key consideration is
         securing access to films that attract audiences, irrespective of whether they are distributed
         directly by a studio or through a licensed distributor. In both cases, distributors compete
         to secure exhibition opportunities for their films, and the films compete for the same
         cinema screens and showtimes.
(258)    The Commission considers that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
         compatibility with the internal market in the market for the distribution of US films to
         cinemas as a result of the mere combination of the Parties’ portfolios in any of the EEA
         Member States due to horizontal non-coordinated effects for the following reasons.
(259)    First, as shown in Section 6.3.1, while the Transaction gives rise to affected markets in
         most EEA countries, the Parties’ combined market share often remains below [30-40]%
         and never exceeds [30-40]%. Post-Transaction, the merged entity’s market shares will
         be comparable to Disney and Universal.
(260)    Second, in addition to Disney and Universal, the merged entity will be constrained by a
         number of other US competitors. When asked which studios have competed with or
         exerted a competitive constraint on Paramount and Warner in the distribution of films for
         theatrical release to cinemas in the last three years, most respondents to the market
         investigation chose Universal, Disney and Sony. However, respondents also chose
         Amazon MGM, Apple, A24, Lionsgate as well as local film studios and Netflix. (296)
         Similarly, respondents to the market investigation confirmed that these studios will exert
         a competitive constraint on the merged entity in the next three years. A more significant
         number of respondents chose Amazon MGM as well as Netflix than in the previous
         question, showing that the respondents anticipate these players to expand their
         distribution capabilities. (297)
(261)    Third, most responding cinemas have indicated that already today the terms (number of
         screens, number of weeks, etc. but excluding rental fees) for both Paramount and Warner
         films are not more favourable than the average terms and conditions for all US films. (298)
         As regards rental fees, most responding cinemas have indicated that they are broadly
         aligned in the market and do not differ significantly between studios (see also
         paragraph (341)) (299). Further, most responding cinemas have expressly confirmed that
         the rental fees of Paramount and Warner are not more favourable to cinemas than the
         average rates of all US films. (300) Given the above and considering that post-Transaction
         the merged entity’s market shares will be comparable to those of Disney and Universal
         in the affected markets, the merged entity will unlikely be able to alter the commercial
         terms of its films, including rental fees, to the detriment of cinemas.
(262)    Finally, the Transaction is unlikely to lead to a reduction in the Parties’ film output for
         theatrical release nor to shortening of the theatrical windows for the reasons set out above
         at Section 6.4.3.1.2.1. Consequently, the Transaction will neither reduce the essential
         inputs required for cinema operations nor restrict the commercial windows available to
         cinema operators.
(263)    For the reasons set out above, and without prejudice to the assessment set out below in
         sections 6.4.3.2.2. and 6.4.3.3.3 below, the Transaction is unlikely to have a negative
         impact on the commercial terms, including rental fees, for the distribution of the merged
         entity’s films to cinemas.

(264)       The Commission therefore considers that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts
            as to its compatibility with the internal market in the market for the distribution of US
            films to cinemas by virtue of the mere combination of the Parties’ portfolios in the EEA
            due to horizontal non-coordinated effects.

6.4.3.2.2         UIP Countries
(265)       The Commission has undertaken a further competitive assessment in the countries where
            UIP is active (‘UIP Countries’) (301) and the EEA Member States where UIP is not
            active (‘Non-UIP Countries’) (302). This is because in the UIP Countries, film portfolios
            of Paramount and Universal are already distributed together through UIP OpCos and UIP
            Shared Licensees. (303) In the UIP Countries, by virtue of UIP, there are also structural
            links between Paramount and Universal, which are not present in the Non-UIP Countries.
(266)       The Commission’s assessment of this market has therefore focused on what the
            implications of the addition of Warner’s portfolio to the merged entity would be,
            distinguishing between the countries in which the portfolios of Paramount, Warner, and
            Universal would be distributed together (UIP Countries) from those in which their
            respective portfolios would be distributed separately (Non-UIP Countries). The
            Notifying Party has also provided its views for both groups of countries separately.

6.4.3.2.2.1.      Notifying Party’ views
(267)       The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction does not raise concerns either in
            countries where Paramount films are distributed through UIP or a UIP licensee.
(268)       First, the Notifying Party argues that there is nothing preventing Warner or other studios
            from using the same third-party distributor that UIP appointed for Paramount and
            Universal, regardless of the Transaction. On this basis, contractual consolidation at the
            distribution level could take place regardless of the Transaction. (304)
(269)       Second, the Notifying Party submits that, when looking at the right market, UIP’s share
            remains below 25% in all five countries except Poland ([20-30]%), and even combining
            UIP and Warner’s shares, the total does not exceed [40-50]% in any country. However,
            Poland is the only EEA country where UIP acts as the appointed theatrical distributor for
            a third-party studio – i.e., [third-party studio]. (305)
(270)       Third, the Notifying Party submits that UIP functions as a cost-saving mechanism, not
            as a vehicle for joint commercial strategy or coordinated negotiations with cinemas. UIP
            retains full operational independence, since Paramount and Universal manage their
            respective slates independently and confidentially, and each partner sets release dates
            individually without consulting with each other. There are also operational firewalls in
            place that prevent Paramount and Universal from exchanging any commercially sensitive
            information, including about their plans to release or distribute individual films, pricing,
            and other key competitive parameters.
(271)       UIP does not negotiate jointly for Paramount and Universal vis-à-vis cinemas. UIP
            discusses these booking terms on a title-by-title basis, separately for each studio.



(301)   These include Bulgaria, Croatia, Czechia, Cyprus, Denmark, Estonia, Finland, Greece, Hungary, Iceland,
        Latvia, Lithuania, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Romania, Slovakia, Slovenia, Sweden.
(302)   These include Austria, Belgium, France, Germany, Ireland, Italy, Liechtenstein, Luxembourg, Malta,
        Netherlands, Spain.
(303)   In the UIP Non-Shared Licensee Countries, Paramount and Universal currently do not distribute through the
        same distributor.
(304)   Response to RFI 24, question 6.
(305)   Response to RFI 24, question 6.
                                                          61
(272)    Each studio also bears its own costs related to prints, digital cinema packages, dubbing,
         subtitling, marketing, and distribution-related taxes and fees, and importantly, retains its
         own revenues without pooling profits. (306)
(273)    Fourth, the Notifying Party submits that UIP does not differ from a third-party distributor
         in the freedom to negotiate with cinemas. In both cases, Paramount determines the
         commercial strategy of its films and retains final approval over key parameters, and the
         distributor – whether UIP or a third-party licensee – implements that strategy at
         Paramount’s direction. Paramount does not determine for its distributors – whether UIP
         or third-party licensees – the screen allocation, holdover decisions (307) or duration of
         theatrical runs. These are determined by distributors in their negotiations with cinemas
         in booking sessions, subject to Paramount’s feedback where relevant. (308)
(274)    Fifth, negotiations between distributors (and self-distributing studios) and cinemas take
         place on a title-by-title basis: neither of the Parties engages in block booking (whereby
         cinemas are required to license multiple films as part of a package or ‘block’ rather than
         individually) or blind bidding (whereby cinemas are required to license a film before
         having an opportunity to view it). (309)
(275)    Finally, vertical integration between cinema chains and theatrical distributors is
         pervasive in many countries. This vertical integration is precisely why Paramount’s
         distribution agreements include [details of contractual agreements with third parties]. For
         example, Cinemacity (Cineworld group), the largest cinema chain in Bulgaria owns
         Forum Film, the leading third-party distributor in Bulgaria. Similarly, in the Baltics, the
         leading cinema chain across Estonia, Lithuania and Latvia owns distributors in the
         region: Estonian Theatrical Distribution, Latvian Theatrical Distribution and Lithuania
         Theatrical Distribution. (310)

6.4.3.2.2.2.    Commission’s assessment
(276)    The Commission considers that the Transaction raises serious doubts as to its
         compatibility with the internal market in the market for the distribution of US films to
         cinemas in the UIP Countries due to horizontal non-coordinated effects as a result of the
         addition of Warner’s portfolio in the UIP Countries for the following reasons.
(277)    First, the Commission considers that market shares alone underestimate the impact of the
         Transaction at the distributor level, as they do not fully capture the bargaining power of
         distributors vis-à-vis cinemas. In particular, cinemas’ business models rely on access to
         the film slates of major US studios, as these films attract a significant proportion of
         cinema admissions. (311) The film slates of the Parties and those of other major US studios
         are differentiated versus films produced by non-US studios and are less substitutable
         from a cinema’s perspective. (312) Therefore, the film slate of each major US studio
         constitutes an important input for a cinema’s overall operations and a distributor’s
         bargaining position depends not only on its market share, but also on the competitive
         relevance of the film portfolio it manages.
(278)    Second, the merged entity’s films would be distributed with Universal and/or Disney in
         a number of UIP Countries, leading to the accumulation of a significantly large film
         portfolio in the hands of one third-party distributor, increasing said distributor’s

         portfolios of at least three out of five major US studios.

(286)    While the Commission considered the Lumiere market shares above as part of a
         robustness check, given that is a publicly available dataset used by some market
         participants, it is important to recognise the limitations of the database. Lumiere is a free
         database provided by the European Audiovisual Observatory. However, it is limited to
         the top 200 titles by cinema admissions across the EEA. (318) Although the database
         contains ‘data from what it considers to be the most reliable data sources in each
         territory’, the Observatory states that ‘there can be significant differences in the coverage
         rates among individual markets and / or years’. It also notes that it ‘is in no position to
         verify the accuracy of the data provided by the various third-party data sources. Neither
         the Observatory nor its third-party sources can warrant that the data provided is free of
         errors, omissions or other inaccuracies’. (319) By opposition, ComScore data is collected
         directly from theatres and relied on by studios as the industry standard for tracking film
         performance. (320) However, regardless of the database used, the Commission concludes
         that in a number of EEA Member States, following the combination of Paramount’s and
         Warner’s portfolios post-Transaction, the distributor likely to be used by the merged
         entity would have a market share above [40-50]%.
(287)    Further, market respondents are also concerned by the high concentration levels in their
         responses to the market investigation. For example, one cinema respondent indicated that
         ‘there are not alternatives to US Studio titles, so a higher concentration of these in the
         hands of fewer distributors improves their negotiating position’. (321)
(288)    In relation to the UIP Countries in particular, respondents flagged e.g., ‘if the current
         distributor for Paramount in the Baltics also attained Warners distribution after the
         Transaction they would be by far the dominant distributor and could use their strength
         to gain advantageous exhibition terms with cinemas that would hurt other distributors’
         and ‘it depends on the way it [the Transaction] is allowed to proceed. If it just adds to a
         company that controls [70-80]% of the exhibition, anything could happen. Referring only
         to Portugal’. (322) Another respondent indicated ‘In Portugal the market only recently
         and contingently balanced into a ~45/35 duopoly, built on the split of the major
         catalogues between two operators (Universal+Warner Bros. with Cinemundo;
         Disney+Paramount with NOS). The Transaction enables the combined
         Paramount/Warner Bros. catalogue to be consolidated with the vertically integrated
         operator that already distributes Paramount and Disney and dominates exhibition —
         reversing that balance, marginalising independent distribution and reducing choice for
         exhibitors and audiences’. (323)

        Warner data. This is likely to overstate both Paramount’s distributor’s share and the illustrative increment
        of the merged firm switching Warner’s films to the third-party distributor Paramount uses in these territories,
        as this approach covers all Paramount’s and Warner’s films showing in European cinemas in 2024 (i.e., the
        numerator is complete, from the perspective of Paramount or Warner films) but does not cover all films
        produced by other studios (and carried by said distributors), or all films carried by other distributors; the
        denominator is thus incomplete.

        accessed on 10 July 2026).
