(323)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1a Theatrical distribution value chain’, question E.2.2.
                                                             66
(289)    Fourth, several cinema operators indicated that contracts between distributors and
         cinemas/cinema operators are negotiated under a framework agreement covering the
         entire slate of films distributed by a given distributor (including films from several
         different studios). (324)
(290)    Further, most cinema operators that expressed a view confirmed that a distributor can
         drive negotiations by leveraging all films it distributes (from the same and other film
         studios). (325) For example, one respondent explained ‘an exhibitor cannot assess each
         negotiation in complete isolation from the wider commercial relationship with that
         distributor and its current or future release slate. This is particularly relevant where a
         distributor controls access to several major US studio titles, franchise films,
         family/animation films or other event releases that are important for admissions and box
         office revenues’. (326)
(291)    This is also confirmed by responding studios and distributors. The majority of responding
         studios and distributors indicated that when a distributor distributes more than one film
         studio, this strengthens their negotiation position vis-à-vis cinema operators. (327) In
         particular, one respondent explained that ‘The breadth of a multi-studio portfolio matters
         because it determines the distributor’s overall standing: a distributor that brings a steady
         flow of strong, high-potential titles across the year holds a stronger position with
         exhibitors on screen access, release dating and conditions than one carrying few or
         weaker titles. (…) More high-potential titles mean both better terms per film and greater
         overall bargaining power’. (328)
(292)    Fifth, the film industry is already characterized by a high degree of transparency; and this
         transparency would be exacerbated where UIP’s sales team, or the sales team of an UIP
         licensee, would handle Warner’s films in addition to Paramount and Universal. In that
         respect, several distributors explained that they conduct a single consolidated meeting
         with each cinema operator in which all titles across the distributor’s entire portfolio are
         discussed and negotiated simultaneously. (329) Many distributors that expressed a view
         explained that they have the same team that handles distribution of content for multiple
         studios, attending however separate meetings with cinemas for each studios’ slates /
         films. (330) That increased transparency would increase the bargaining position of UIP’s
         sales team towards cinemas, to the benefit of each of UIP’s parents.
(293)    Finally, the pre-existing structural links between Paramount and Universal in the UIP
         Countries amplify the risk of an increased bargaining power resulting from the increased
         transparency. Chinese walls cannot address these concerns as the same local UIP sales
         team or the same UIP licensee negotiate for both Paramount and Universal in each UIP
         country. (331) Adding Warner, i.e., another major US studio, to a joint portfolio of
         Paramount and Universal would increase the abovementioned risks, as the relevant sales
         teams would be aware of the commercial terms of each studio and could leverage that
         information in the negotiations with cinemas.
(294)    For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction raises serious
         doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal effects on
         the market for the distribution of US films for theatrical release in the UIP Countries.

6.4.3.2.3         Non-UIP Countries

6.4.3.2.3.1.      Notifying Party’ views
(295)       The Notifying Party submits that the merged entity will be incentivized to (i) maintain
            their existing distribution local offices in the Non-UIP EEA countries where local offices
            already exist, and (ii) shift to self-distribution in the Non-UIP EEA countries where the
            larger slate and associated local box office would justify this commercial decision.
(296)       Firstly, it would not be economically rational, absent territory-specific countervailing
            factors, to add costly layers of intermediation in territories where Warner already has its
            own distribution office, and where the merger increases the volume of titles over which
            fixed costs involved in distribution and marketing can be spread. The merged entity is
            clearly incentivized to distribute their planned larger combined slate by relying on its
            own local offices, as the costs of a local office (which are mostly fixed) ought to be even
            lower given the merged entity’s planned larger slate, enabling it to enjoy the benefits of
            self-distribution at lower cost (on a per film basis).
(297)       Secondly, the Notifying Party submits that self-distribution aligns economic incentives
            between the producer and cinemas. The incentives of producers to maximise box office
            performance are enhanced because of the importance of theatrical performance in
            subsequent windows. This incentive does not apply to third-party distributors who only
            distribute theatrically for studios. On the other hand, third-party distributors may have
            conflicting incentives, particularly where third-party distributors own a stake in local
            cinemas, as they also have an incentive to monetise their downstream exhibition business.
(298)       Thirdly, the Notifying Party submits that self-distribution also ensures that marketing and
            distribution activities are focused on maximizing the performance of the producer’s own
            titles. Where a third-party distributor acts on behalf of multiple producers, contractual
            protections to do the best for each individual film can mitigate conflicts, but they cannot
            eliminate all practical allocation issues, particularly where the distributor must prioritize
            resources across competing titles. The distributor may therefore need to act in the interest
            of one studio’s film over another studio’s film.
(299)       Finally, the Notifying Party submits that for the Non-UIP EEA countries where neither
            Paramount nor Warner currently have a local office (namely Austria and Malta), the
            merged entity will internally assess whether the benefits of self-distribution will justify
            the (fixed) costs of setting up and running a local office. As the costs of a local office
            will be spread over its larger combined slate of 30+ films per year, the Combined Entity
            will have more incentives to set up a local office compared to the pre-Transaction
            scenario. (332)

6.4.3.2.3.2.      Commission’s assessment
(300)       The Commission considers that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
            compatibility with the internal market in the market for the distribution of US films to
            cinemas in the Non-UIP Countries due to horizontal non-coordinated effects for the
            following reasons.
(301)       As a preliminary remark, Paramount has its own distribution offices in France, Ireland
            and Spain. Paramount distributes through [Licensee B] in Belgium, Germany and
            Luxembourg; through [Licensee AE] in the Netherlands; and through [Licensee AF] in
            Liechtenstein. Paramount uses third-party distributors in Austria, Italy, and Malta.
            Warner has its own distribution office in each of Belgium, France, Germany, Ireland,


(332)   Response to RFI 25, question 7.
                                                        68
         Italy, Liechtenstein, Luxembourg, Netherlands and Spain. Warner uses third-party
         distributors in Austria and Malta. (333)
(302)    First, many US studios already self-distribute in Non-UIP Countries, which means that
         post-Transaction, one distributor will not hold a significant number of film portfolios of
         US major studios, as opposed to the UIP Countries.
(303)    In the first place, in each of Belgium, France, Germany, Ireland, Italy, Liechtenstein,
         Luxembourg, Netherlands and Spain, Warner already self-distributes, and the merged
         entity is likely to rely on [local offices] for the distribution of the joint portfolio post-
         Transaction. [details on the merged entity’s incentives to self-distribute]. (334) Even
         though Warner does not have a distribution office in Austria and Malta, the Parties
         already use the same third-party distributor (i.e. [Licensee A] in Austria and [Licensee
         AG] in Malta) and there is thus no merger specificity.
(304)    In the second place, Universal and Disney have distribution offices in all the
         abovementioned countries, except for Malta and Luxembourg (Universal only). In
         addition, Sony has distribution offices in all the abovementioned countries except for
         Italy, Malta and the Netherlands.
(305)    One stakeholder raised that in Italy distribution is handled by a third party representing
         one or more other major US studios, however, as discussed below in this section, given
         that Warner already has a distribution office in Italy, the merged entity will have an
         economic incentive to self-distribute through this office. (335)
(306)    Second, it will be economically rational for the merged entity to distribute through the
         distribution offices where Warner already self-distributes, i.e., Belgium, France,
         Germany, Ireland, Italy, Liechtenstein, Luxembourg, Netherlands and Spain. The fact
         that the Parties already self-distribute in these countries demonstrates that self-
         distribution is a commercially viable and economically rational distribution model in
         these territories. It would therefore be rational for the merged entity to continue self-
         distributing through these offices, particularly considering its larger post-merger
         portfolio. As costs of running a local distribution office are mostly fixed, the Parties
         would be able to spread them across a larger portfolio of films, thereby improving the
         economics of self-distribution. Indeed, fixed costs account for more than [50-60]% of
         total costs of the Parties’ distribution operations in the Non-UIP Countries and are close
         or exceed [80-90]% in most of these countries. (336) The Parties do not expect material
         increases in local offices’ fixed costs as a result of a large portfolio and any increase in
         costs would be offset by the higher box office revenues resulting from a larger film
         slate. (337)
(307)    Finally, the patterns of box-office revenues of the Parties confirm that, the larger the local
         territory, the higher the incentives of a larger studio to consider self-distribution. Warner
         uses self-distribution in larger territories and third-party distributors in smaller territories.
         In the only small territory in which WBD self-distributes – Luxembourg – Warner does
         so through its Belgium branch rather than through an in-country local office. Given that
         the box office revenues are likely to increase given the addition of Paramount’s portfolio,
         it would be economically rational for the merged entity to distribute via Warner’s pre-
         existing offices in the Non-UIP countries.

(335)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q5- Remedies Market test’, question E.1.
(336)   Response to RFI 26, question 2.
(337)   Ibid.
                                                          69
(308)       For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction does not raise
            serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal
            non-coordinated effects on the market for the distribution of US films for theatrical
            release in the Non-UIP Countries.
(309)       Given that the merged entity is likely to self-distribute in the Non-UIP Countries, the
            Transaction will not lead to the accumulation of a significantly large film portfolio in the
            hands of one third-party distributor, increasing said distributor’s bargaining power vis-à-
            vis cinemas in the Non-UIP Countries.

6.4.3.2.4         Conclusion
(310)       For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction, due to
            horizontal non-coordinated effects:
            (a)   does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market on the
                  market for the distribution of US films to cinemas by virtue of the mere
                  combination of the Parties’ portfolios in the EEA;
            (b)   raises serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market on the market
                  for the distribution of US films for theatrical release in the UIP Countries as a result
                  of the addition of Warner’s portfolio, and
            (c)   does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a
                  result of horizontal effects on the market for the distribution of US films for
                  theatrical release in the Non-UIP Countries.

6.4.4.      The theatrical distribution value chain - Assessment of coordinated effects

6.4.4.1. Introduction
(311)       The Commission also assessed whether the Transaction would give rise to coordinated
            effects on the market for the distribution of US films for theatrical release.
(312)       Most cinemas that responded to the market investigation submitted that the Transaction
            would make it possible, easier and/or more likely for some or all film distributors to
            coordinate (i.e., align) their commercial strategies towards cinemas/cinema
            operators. (338)
(313)       First, as regards the possible terms of coordination, respondents have pointed to rental
            rates (fee splits), length of theatrical release window, release date scheduling and screen
            allocation, the latter covering aspects such as minimum screen commitments and
            minimum number of showtimes. (339)
(314)       Most cinemas submitted that the film industry is characterised by a high degree of
            transparency and observability: release dates are public, box office results are widely
            monitored, changes to release calendars are visible, and theatrical window strategies
            often become known in the market. However, the rental rates (fee splits) negotiated
            between a distributor and a cinema operator are not publicly available.
(315)       Second, as regards the sustainability of said coordination, respondents have in essence
            pointed to the following three factors. First, the Transaction will reduce the number of
            major studios from five to four, of which three would be broadly comparable players
            (Disney, Universal and the merged entity), alongside Sony. In a market with five major
            studios, a studio seeking to increase rental rates or tighten windowing requirements faces


(338)    Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, questions D.B.7.
(339)    Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, questions D.B.7.2 and
         D.B.7.3.
                                                        70
         more uncertainty as to whether competitors will follow. By contrast, in a market with
         three more symmetric participants, coordination becomes easier to sustain, and the
         incentives to maintain aligned commercial terms are correspondingly strengthened. For
         more detail, see Section 736.4.4.3.2 below.
(316)    Most cinemas also submitted that the risk of coordination is increased where the same
         local distributor distributes films of more than one major studio because shared
         distribution can make it easier to standardise or streamline commercial terms across these
         studios. (340)
(317)    Finally, most cinemas that responded to the market investigation submitted that cinema
         operators would have no or limited ability to counteract such coordination because they
         have significant fixed costs and cannot survive without stable access to high-potential
         films, which only the major studios can provide. More specifically, various respondents
         stated that they must continue to bear the costs of rent, staff, energy, maintenance,
         equipment and other operating costs regardless of admissions and that this makes it
         difficult to reject high-potential films, even if the terms for such films were to become
         less favourable. (341)

6.4.4.2. The Notifying Party’s views
(318)    The Notifying Party submits that the Transaction will not lead to coordinated effects in
         relation to the distribution of films for theatrical release for the reasons summarised
         below.
(319)    First, according to the Notifying Party, the structure of the market means that coordinated
         effects are unlikely to arise because (i) the market is supplied by a large and diverse set
         of producers and distributors, with European productions alone accounting for
         approximately 33% of EEA cinema admissions in 2024; (ii) films are heterogeneous
         products meaning that there is no common reference point or stable parameter on which
         studios could reach a mutual understanding on the terms of coordination; (iii) the
         performance of each film is specific to that title and cannot be predicted ex ante; (iv) film
         output is lumpy; (v) there is no transparency in commercial terms between distributors
         and cinemas: the terms on which distributors license films to cinemas ‒ including rental
         splits, screen allocations, holdover commitments, and marketing spend ‒ are negotiated
         bilaterally, ultimately determined on a title-by-title basis, and are always confidential
         between the contracting parties. (342)
(320)    Second, according to the Notifying Party, the Transaction will not materially increase
         symmetry in the market. A high level of asymmetry between studios will remain. Most
         notably, a key part of Disney’s strategy is to use theatrical releases to support its wider
         commercial interests in monetising its IP via both its streaming service, and via theme
         parks and consumer products. Other studios, notably Sony, differ from the Parties and
         Disney in that they do not have their own SVOD platforms (or, in the case of Universal,
         do not have such a platform in Europe). (343)
(321)    Third, according to the Notifying Party, UIP’s structure prevents its participants –
         Paramount and Universal – from engaging in any form of coordination. In essence, this
         is because (i) Paramount and Universal manage their film slates independently and
         confidentially from each other (ii) firewalls prevent Paramount and Universal from
         disclosing (or aligning on) their commercial strategies, (iii) each studio bears its own


(340)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, question D.B.7.4.
(341)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, question D.B.7.5.
(342)   Response to RFI 21, question 2, paragraphs 3-10.
(343)   Response to RFI 21, question 2, paragraphs 11-16.
                                                           71
         costs and retains its own revenues, and (iv) UIP does not negotiate jointly for Paramount
         and Universal vis-à-vis cinemas. (344)
(322)    Fourth, according to the Notifying Party, the cumulative conditions established in
         Airtours (345) that must be satisfied for coordinated effects to arise are not satisfied. These
         are that (i) the coordinating firms are able to reach a mutual understanding on the terms
         of coordination; (ii) they can monitor deviation from those terms; (iii) there is a credible
         mechanism to deter deviation; and (iv) non-coordinating market participants (not only
         rivals but also customers) are not in a position to disrupt the coordination. (346)
(323)    Fifth, beyond these general features of the market, the Notifying Party submits that
         coordination on each of the following terms can also be excluded, for the following
         reasons. In the first place, the Notifying Party submits that coordination to restrict film
         output or investment can be excluded because (i) it is not in the studio’s individual or
         collective interest, (ii) there are no obvious terms of coordination because films are highly
         heterogeneous, (iii) there is no credible mechanism to deter deviation, and (iv) a broad
         and growing set of competing studios would defeat any restriction. (347)
(324)    In the second place, the Notifying Party submits that coordination on theatrical release
         windows (348) can be excluded because this is a decision driven by film performance,
         which varies film by film, and that studios have no incentive to shorten windows, in
         particular for films that perform well in cinemas. To the contrary, Sony and Disney are
         proponents of long theatrical windows, and Universal has recently announced that it will
         significantly extend its theatrical release window in 2026 and 2027. (349)
(325)    In the third place, the Notifying Party submits that coordination on release scheduling
         can be excluded because this is a pre-existing, demand-expanding practice that the
         Transaction does not change. Overall, it notes that a number of films are released at the
         same time and films can be released simultaneously to great success. Considering only
         similar films aiming to target similar audiences, staggering their releases can be
         commercially rational independent behaviour and brings benefits to consumers, cinemas
         and studios as it avoids audience cannibalisation. (350)
(326)    In the fourth place, the Notifying Party submits that coordination of some or all of the
         larger studios to worsen the terms on which films are supplied to cinemas can also be
         excluded, because (i) there is no stable focal point on which to coordinate, given the exact
         split for each cinema on each film on each of its screens is highly commercially sensitive,
         and determined on a title by title and territory by territory; (ii) deviations could not be
         monitored, since the commercial package actually agreed between a studio and a given
         cinema for a given title is confidential and not visible to rival distributors; (iii) rival
         distributors, who account for a large and growing share of box office, would disrupt any
         coordination, by attracting reallocated screens, showtimes and marketing;
         (iv) distributors are highly differentiated, making a coordinated strategy more difficult to
         agree and enforce; (v) the countervailing buyer power of cinemas , given high cinema
         concentration across the EEA, would discipline pricing and destabilise any attempted
         coordination; (vi) the studios’ own conduct shows that distribution is not a vehicle for
         coordinating terms, since they overwhelmingly keep their distribution separate in

            sufficiently large markets (e.g. US, Canada, UK, France) despite having the option to
            consolidate it. (351)

6.4.4.3. Commission’s assessment
(327)       The Commission considers that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
            compatibility with the internal market as a result of coordinated effects between the
            merged entity and other film studios, for the reasons set out hereafter.

6.4.4.3.1         Possible terms of coordination
(328)       As noted above, respondents have pointed to possible coordination regarding release date
            scheduling, (a reduction in the) length of theatrical release window, rental rates (fee
            splits) and screen allocation, the latter covering aspects such as minimum screen
            commitments and minimum number of showtimes. (352) Further, certain respondents
            have suggested that the terms of coordination may also include (a reduction in) film
            output. (353)
(329)       First, as regards release date scheduling, the Commission notes that this is a pre-existing
            practice that the Transaction does not change. As indicated by several respondents, major
            studios closely monitor each other’s release calendars, and it is already relatively unusual
            for high-potential films to open directly against each other. (354) In any event, as set out
            at paragraph 341(343) below, coordination on this term would not be sustainable.
(330)       Second, as regards the length of the theatrical window, for the reasons set out above at
            Section 6.4.3.1.2.2, the Commission considers it unlikely that the Transaction could lead
            to a coordination to shorten the theatrical release window between major US studios, as
            suggested by some respondents, as this would run counter to the interests of Disney,
            Universal and Sony.
(331)       Third, regarding rental fees, that is the split of box office revenue between the cinema
            and the studio / third-party distributor, the Commission’s assessment is set out below, in
            the context of the sustainability of coordination.
(332)       Fourth, screen allocation is another possible term of coordination that was brought up by
            various cinemas, whereby this should be understood as encompassing (minimum) screen
            commitments, that is the number of screens on which a given film is shown, (minimum)
            number of showtimes, that is the number of times said film is shown, and screening time,
            that is the time of the day at which a film is shown. The Commission’s assessment of this
            term is set out below, in the context of the sustainability of coordination
            (Section 6.4.4.3.2).
(333)       A final factor on which some respondents consider that coordination may take place is (a
            reduction in) film output. However, for the reasons set out above at Section 6.4.3.1.2.1
            (Reduction of output), such a reduction of film output post-Transaction appears unlikely.
(334)       Having regard to the above, the Commission considers that the Transaction does not
            change the pre-existing practice as regards release date scheduling and that it is unlikely
            that coordination would arise to reduce the length of the theatrical release window or
            reduce film output. As regards a possible coordination on rental fees and screen



(351)   Response to RFI 21, question 2, paragraphs 44-52.
(352)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, questions D.B.7.2 and
        D.B.7.3.
(353)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, questions D.B.7.2 and
        D.B.7.3.
(354)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, question D.B.7.3.
                                                       73
            allocation, the Commission will analyse hereafter whether co-ordination on those two
            terms would be sustainable.

6.4.4.3.2         Sustainability of a possible coordination
(335)       The Commission’s assessment of the sustainability of coordination on rental fees and
            screen allocation, first, considers, the factors bearing on the likelihood of coordination;
            second, it follows the four remaining conditions identified in Section 8.4.1 above :
            (i) whether the coordinating firms are able to reach a mutual understanding on the terms
            of coordination; (ii) they can monitor deviation from those terms; (iii) there is a credible
            mechanism to deter deviation; and (iv) non-coordinating market participants (not only
            rivals but also customers) are not in a position to disrupt the coordination.
(336)       On the likelihood of coordination, the Commission considers that the reduction from five
            to four US major film studios as a result of the Transaction could potentially be
            considered as an element facilitating coordination on screen allocation and/or rental fees
            since the fewer the number of remaining firms in a market, the easier, in principle, it is
            to find and/or maintain a consensus, whilst at the same time reducing the alternatives
            available to cinema operators.
(337)       The Commission also considered whether contact between major US studios across
            multiple EEA jurisdictions or adjacent segments of the audiovisual value chain could
            facilitate coordination. However, the two terms identified in this Section (rental fees and
            screen allocation) are negotiated bilaterally, on a national basis, and are confidential
            between the contracting parties. Contact between the major US studios in other
            jurisdictions or segments therefore does not materially increase the risk of coordination
            on those terms.
(338)       The Commission notes the submission by most cinemas that shared distribution of more
            than one major’s films can ease the standardisation of terms. In the EEA the only such
            arrangement is UIP; as discussed in Section 7.4, the Final Commitments provide for
            Paramount’s complete exit from UIP in the EEA and interim ring-fencing measures,
            including on rental rates and booking terms. Post-Transaction the merged entity’s films
            will no longer be co-distributed with films of another US major studio through a shared
            distributor. This reinforces the conclusion that coordination would not be sustainable.
(339)       The Commission considers whether the terms of coordination can be sufficiently
            monitored. As regards the screen allocation: while commitments regarding screen
            allocation are negotiated bilaterally between a studio/ distributor and cinema, the number
            of screens and showtimes actually allocated to a given film is to a significant extent
            observable, albeit not in a centralised manner, for example by consulting the websites of
            the various cinema operators on a weekly basis. The Commission notes, however, that
            what is observable is the allocation actually implemented, rather than the minimum
            screen or showtime commitments agreed bilaterally between a studio and a cinema.
(340)       As regards the rental fees, while the approximate range of revenue splits is broadly
            known, the exact split of the rental fee negotiated between distributors and cinemas is
            not public and is commercially sensitive. (355) This applies to both rental splits agreed on
            a film-by-film basis (as is generally the case with smaller cinemas) and to pre-arranged
            rental splits agreed across one of the Parties’ entire film portfolio and for a duration that
            generally exceeds one year (as is the case with various large cinema chains). (356) This



(355)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, questions D.B.7.2 and
        D.B.7.3.
(356)   Response to RFI 20, question 2.
                                                        74
         would considerably limit the ability of other major US studios to identify deviation
         regarding any tacitly agreed rental fee split.
(341)    Therefore, considering the current functioning of this market, the broad alignment of
         rental-fee ranges (paragraph (261)) reflects common industry structure — comparable
         box-office economics and standard revenue-split conventions — not coordination; and
         an approximate known range is no focal point, since the exact split remains confidential
         and unobservable to rivals.
(342)    The Commission has considered whether any possible retaliation mechanism could make
         sustainable any potential coordinated behaviour among the major US studios. Such
         mechanisms could theoretically consist of a temporary returning to competitive
         behaviour on the terms in question. However, rental fees and screen allocation are
         negotiated bilaterally and confidentially between each studio and each cinema. A studio
         wishing to punish a deviating studio by worsening its own terms would therefore not be
         able to signal to the deviating studio that it is doing so in retaliation, nor to target the
         punishment at the deviating studio: it could only worsen its terms vis-à-vis cinemas
         generally, at a cost to itself and without any assurance that the deviating studio would
         perceive the response as punishment.
(343)    The only credible retaliation mechanism the Commission identified is that of one or
         several major US studios scheduling the release of one or more of their high-potential
         films at the same time as that of the deviating major US studio, to hurt the latter’s
         revenues. Such a mechanism would not, however, deter deviation. In releasing a high-
         potential film against another, the retaliating studio would significantly hurt its own
         revenues through audience cannibalisation, and a threat deters only if the deviating firm
         expects it to be carried out. Moreover, the mechanism operates on a different parameter
         from that on which coordination is assumed to take place and could not be targeted at the
         specific cinemas or terms in respect of which deviation occurred. This finding does not
         depend on the term on which coordination is assumed: the absence of a credible deterrent
         applies equally to any possible tacit understanding on release date scheduling, the length
         of the theatrical release window or film output.
(344)    As for countervailing abilities, as explained above at Section 6.4.3.1.2, US studios other
         than Disney, Universal and Sony exert only limited competitive pressure on the Parties
         to the Transaction, as they produce a relatively small number of high-potential films
         which are very important for cinema operators. US studios other than the US majors thus
         offer as such little challenge against the stability of any potential coordination.
(345)    The same can be said of cinema operators (as noted at paragraph (317). (357) More
         specifically, various respondents stated that they must continue to bear the costs of rent,
         staff, energy, maintenance, equipment and other operating costs regardless of admissions
         and that this makes it difficult to reject high-potential films, even if the terms for such
         films were to become less favourable. (358)
(346)    Notwithstanding the limited ability of non-coordinating market participants to disrupt
         any coordination, the absence of a credible deterrent mechanism is sufficient on its own
         to render coordination on these terms unsustainable.




(357)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, questions D.B.7. juncto
        D.B.7.5.
(358)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q1b Theatrical distribution value chain – cinemas’, question D.B.7.5.
                                                        75
6.4.4.4. Conclusion
(347)       The Commission considers that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
            compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal coordinated effects on the
            market for the distribution of US films for theatrical release.
(348)       The Commission considers coordination unlikely on release dates, shortened theatrical
            windows or film output. Release-date scheduling is demand-expanding, shorter windows
            would conflict with rivals’ commercial interests, and there is no reliable basis for
            concluding that a reduction in output would be in the combined entity’s interest.
(349)       As regards rental fees and screen allocation, any possible coordination would in any event
            not be sustainable. The Commission has identified no credible mechanism by which the
            major US studios could deter deviation from any tacitly agreed terms, which is sufficient
            on its own to render such coordination unsustainable. In addition, as regards rental fees,
            the ability of the major US studios to detect deviation from any tacitly agreed split would
            be considerably limited, given that the negotiated terms are non-public and commercially
            sensitive.

6.4.5.      The AV content value chain

6.4.5.1. Production and licensing of AV content
            The Parties are active in the production and licensing of commissioned and pre-produced
            AV content. The Parties produce and supply commissioned AV content to third parties,
            […] to SVOD services such as Netflix, Amazon Prime Video and Apple. (359) The Parties
            produce and license pre-produced AV content to third-party distributors and retail service
            providers across all EEA countries. (360) As set out in Section 6.4.2 above, the
            competitive assessment will focus on the affected markets (i.e., production and licensing
            of pre-produced US films to FTA in France, Germany and the Netherlands; and licensing
            of pre-produced US films to pay TV and SVOD in Italy).

6.4.5.1.1         The Notifying Party’s views
(350)       The Notifying Party submits that the horizontal overlap between Paramount and Warner
            on the market for the production and licensing of AV content, in particular the affected
            markets in France, Germany and the Netherlands (see Section 6.4.5.1.2.1 below) and in
            Italy (see Section 6.4.5.2.2 below), will not lead to any competition concerns at the EEA
            or national level for the following reasons.
(351)       First, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties’ combined market shares in both the
            market for the production of commissioned AV content, as well as the market for the
            licensing of pre-produced AV content are low across all overlapping EEA countries. The
            Parties’ combined shares in the production of commissioned AV content result in no
            affected markets. The Parties’ combined market shares in the licensing of pre-produced
            AV content also remain below 20% in all overlapping EEA countries. (361)
(352)       Second, the Notifying Party states that the merged entity will continue to face intense
            competition from large US studios (e.g., Disney Entertainment, Sony Pictures Television,
            Amazon MGM Studios, Lionsgate Television, or NBCUniversal Television),
            international production groups and media aggregators (e.g., Banijay, Fremantle ITV
            Studios, All3Media, BBC Studios, or The Mediapro Studio), as well as European



(359)    Form CO, paragraph 836.
(360)    Form CO, paragraph 844.
(361)    Form CO, paragraphs 819, 845.
                                                       76
            regional and national champions (e.g., StudioCanal, Pathé, Leonine Studios, RAI,
            Atresmedia, Gaumont, Mediawan, Studio TF1). (362)
(353)       Third, the Notifying Party submits that in-house production by streamers and large
            broadcasting groups also act as a further source of competitive pressure over the merged
            entity. (363)

6.4.5.1.2         Commission’s assessment
(354)       The Commission considers that, for the reasons set out below, the Transaction does not
            raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of
            horizontal non-coordinated effects in the market for the production and licensing of AV
            content at the EEA or national level. (364) The Commission will conduct its assessment
            for (i) licensing of pre-produced US films to FTA in France, Germany and the
            Netherlands, and (ii) licensing of pre-produced US films to pay TV and SVOD in Italy.

6.4.5.1.2.1.      Licensing of pre-produced US films to FTA in France, Germany and the
                  Netherlands
(355)       On the basis of 2024 individual market shares, the Parties’ licensing activities of pre-
            produced US films supplied to the FTA TV channels give rise to three affected markets,
            namely France, Germany and the Netherlands. (365) Similarly, these markets are also
            affected on the basis of 2022-2024 average market shares. (366)
(356)       First, the Commission notes that the Transaction gives rise to moderate combined market
            shares leading to affected markets only in very specific and limited market segments.
(357)       In an overall market for the licensing of pre-produced films, the Parties’ combined market
            shares in France, Germany and the Netherlands are below [10-20]% based on 2024 and
            2022-2024 market share data. (367) Considering a segmentation by exhibition window,
            within the relevant market for the licensing of pre-produced films to FTA TV channels,
            the Parties’ combined market shares would in these three countries, still be below [20-
            30]% based on 2024 and 2022-2024 market share data. (368)
(358)       In the three affected markets for the licensing of pre-produced US films to FTA TV

            data respectively (Tables 6 and 7). In the Netherlands, the Parties’ combined market share
            data would be [20-30]% and [30-40]% based on 2024 and 2022-2024 market share data

         observed market entry or expansion during the last three years. (380) One respondent

(363)    Finally, the Commission considers that while this assessment takes place based on an
         US-films based market, any assessment in these specific AV content markets should also
         be viewed in the context of specific national and European regulations and the consumer
         preferences in these relevant Member States. In this regard, most respondents to the
         market investigation, which are active in at least one of the three countries, stated that
         the presence of domestic and European films is important or very important in France,
         Germany and the Netherlands. (382) One respondent stated: ‘Local content is important
         to consumers and also due to very high AVMS investme[nt] quotas’. However, another
         respondent, identifying the role of domestic and European films as moderate, stated:
         ‘While domestic and European films impose some competitive constraint on US films,
         they are less important for the content mix than US films, which are particularly popular
         and tend to perform strongly with audiences’.
(364)    In France, French TV broadcasters are subject to strict content quota obligations,
         requiring at least 60% European works and 40% French-origin works (383), while VOD
         providers must ensure at least 30% European works in their catalogues under the
         Audiovisual Media Services Directive (384). (385) Consistent with these features, French-
         origin content accounts for a high proportion of the content aired in FTA TV, and French
         and European productions account for the most successful titles in terms of audience both
         on linear and pay TV and increasingly on SVOD platforms. (386)
(365)    In Germany, pre-produced films for FTA TV are primarily scheduled to anchor prime-
         time ‘appointment viewing’. This is also the case for public-service broadcasters such as
         ARD (Das Erste) and ZDF, who rely extensively on German-language film franchises
         and movies that consistently attract large national audiences. (387) As of 2026,

6.4.5.1.3         Conclusion
(379)       For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction will not raise
            serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal
            effects on the market for the production and licensing of AV content, and in any of its
            plausible segments, at the EEA or national level.

6.4.5.2. Wholesale supply of TV channels

6.4.5.2.1         Wholesale supply of children’s pay TV channels

6.4.5.2.1.1.      The Notifying Party’s views
(380)       The Notifying Party submits that the horizontal overlap between Paramount and Warner
            on the market for the wholesale supply of children’s pay TV channels will not lead to
            any competition concerns at the EEA or national level for the following reasons.
(381)       First, across the EEA, children’s viewing of dedicated children’s pay TV channels is in
            steady and significant decline as viewers shift at scale to other viewing alternatives,
            mainly SVOD platforms and other VOD services. Competitive pressure from
            SVOD/VOD offerings is particularly high in the children’s segment. Nowadays, only a
            very small percentage of children’s content is viewed on dedicated children’s pay TV
            channels. Most children’s content is viewed on SVOD platforms (such as Netflix,
            Disney+ or Amazon Prime Video) and on YouTube. SVOD services and YouTube carry
            a large amount of children’s content including the same or similar titles to those available
            on linear pay TV channels. Therefore, such services are in direct competition with linear
            pay TV for children’s viewership. (406)
(382)       The move in audiences away from children’s pay TV is consistent with drops in
            viewership, revenues and profitability of children’s pay TV channels. Such drops overlap
            with steep increases in the viewership of children’s content on SVOD and YouTube. (407)
(383)       This structural decline of linear children’s pay TV constrains channel suppliers and
            precludes any attempt by the merged entity to leverage its combined children’s channel
            portfolio to extract more favourable terms from European retail TV distributors/MVPDs
            post-Transaction. (408)
(384)       Second, households across the EEA can access highly substitutable children’s content on
            FTA TV channels, which often offer the exact same shows and programming as on pay
            TV. Given the broad duplication in content, pay TV and FTA TV channels compete for
            the same audiences, with FTA TV children’s channels often even outperforming pay TV
            children’s channels in viewership. As such, post-Transaction the Parties’ activities in the
            wholesale supply of children’s pay TV channels will also be constrained by a large
            number of leading suppliers of children’s FTA TV programming (including Super RTL,
            KiKA, RAI, NPO Clan, Gulli, France 5, TF1, TVP ABC, and RTP2). (409)


        the new regulation on programming and investment obligations for European works and works by
        independent producers, given the entry into force of Legislative Decree 208/2021 containing the new
        Consolidated Law on Audiovisual Media Services.
(406)   Form CO, paragraph 1003.
(407)   Form CO, paragraphs 1028-1052.
(408)   Form CO, paragraph 1012.
(409)   Form CO, paragraphs 1003, 1023-1027.
                                                       82
(385)    Third, there are multiple alternatives to the Parties’ children’s pay TV channels in the
         EEA, including as noted, children’s content available on SVOD platforms, YouTube,
         FTA TV children’s channels, as well as pay TV children’s channels from Disney, Super
         RTL, Groupe M6, De Agostini, CANAL+, YFE, AMC Networks, Pitchoun Medias,
         MWE Networks and SIC among others. The aggregation of all these alternatives will
         continue to exert significant competitive pressure over the merged entity post-
         Transaction. (410)
(386)    Fourth, wholesale suppliers of TV channels typically negotiate with retail TV
         distributors/MVPDs for a bundle of channels (or, even, a bundle of SVOD services and
         a bouquet of channels) rather than on a channel-by-channel basis, or on a genre-by-genre
         basis. Competition is typically between bundles – and it is the overall bundle that matters
         in the bargaining process, rather than a specific channel or genre. (411)
(387)    Children’s pay TV channels are not a driver of pay TV subscriptions (not least because
         subscription decisions are made by adults, not the children who view the content in
         question) and therefore are not an important component of retail TV packages. There is
         a direct correlation between the consumer demand for a particular channel or service (i.e.,
         consumer demand for children’s TV has fallen dramatically) and the MVPDs’
         willingness to pay for it and include it in its bouquet (i.e., MVPDs are less willing to pay
         for children’s TV channels). This dynamic ensures that the Transaction cannot result in
         an increased bargaining power for the Parties in the wholesale supply of children’s pay
         TV channels which would theoretically allow them to impose less favourable conditions
         on their MVPD customers post-Transaction. (412)
(388)    Fifth, MVPDs are large and sophisticated buyers with substantial countervailing buyer
         power. Importantly, the wide range of alternative sources of children’s content
         strengthens MVPDs’ bargaining power in their negotiations with TV channel suppliers.
         MVPDs can and do incorporate a wide array of children’s content (extending beyond
         linear pay TV channels) directly into their own retail packages – most obviously FTA
         TV channels, but also Disney+, YouTube, Netflix, Amazon Prime Video, and other
         streaming platforms – either as parts of bundles or as add-ons. The availability of these
         commercially viable alternatives provides MVPDs with meaningful optionality in
         negotiations with suppliers of children’s pay TV channels and has, in practice, enabled
         them to exert pricing discipline when agreeing carriage terms for children’s channels. In
         view if this, post-Transaction, the merged entity will lack the ability or incentive to raise
         prices or worsen terms to European MVPDs. (413)

6.4.5.2.1.2.   Commission’s assessment
(389)    As a cross-cutting point applicable to the genre-specific assessments below, wholesale
         negotiation occurs on a portfolio basis across genres, as is common industry practice
         unaltered by the Transaction (paragraph 405). The relevant question is therefore whether
         aggregating the Parties’ channels across genres improves the merged entity’s position
         vis-à-vis MVPDs. The Commission considers that it does not because the added channels
         are not ‘must-have’ individually or combined (paragraphs 406 and following), and
         MVPDs retain alternatives in every genre (paragraphs 396 and following).
(390)    As set out above in Section 6.3, on a narrow segmentation by genre, the combined market
         share of the Parties in the potential market for the wholesale supply of children’s TV

         channels, and its subsegment for children’s pay TV channels, is significant in several
         EEA countries, in particular:
             •    wholesale supply of children’s pay TV channels: [40-50]% in Austria, [40-50]%
                  in Belgium, [70-80]% in Bulgaria, [40-50]% in Croatia, [60-70]% in Czechia,
                  [50-60]% in Denmark, [20-30]% in Estonia, [60-70]% in Finland, [40-50]% in
                  France, [80-90]% in Germany, [40-50]% in Hungary, [70-80]% in Ireland,
                  [60-70]% in Italy, [30-40]% in Lithuania, [40-50]% in Netherlands, [70-80]% in
                  Norway, [40-50]% in Poland, [20-30]% in Portugal, [50-60]% in Romania,
                  [20-30]% in Slovakia, [30-40]% in Slovenia, and [70-80]% in Sweden (see
                  Table 12).
             •    wholesale supply of children’s TV channels (pay TV and FTA): [30-40]% in
                  Belgium, [70-80]% in Bulgaria, [30-40]% in Croatia, [20-30]% in Estonia,
                  [60-70]% in Finland, [40-50]% in Hungary, [40-50]% in Ireland, [70-80]% in
                  Italy, [30-40]% in Lithuania, [40-50]% in Netherlands, [30-40]% in Poland,
                  [20-30]% in Portugal, [50-60]% in Romania, [20-30]% in Slovakia, [30-40]% in
                  Slovenia, and [30-40]% in Spain (see Table 11).
(391)    Nonetheless, for the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the
         Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market
         as a result of horizontal non-coordinated effects in the market for the wholesale supply
         of children’s TV channels and in any of its plausible segments at the EEA or national
         level.
(392)    As mentioned in paragraph 390, the Transaction gives rise to affected markets in the
         wholesale supply of children’s channels on an overall level including both pay TV and
         FTA children’s channels, as well as in the potential pay TV subsegment. The
         Commission’s assessment in the following paragraphs focuses on the narrower pay TV
         subsegment. This is because – as shown below – the Transaction does not raise any
         concerns at the narrower pay TV (or FTA) subsegments, and therefore it equally does
         not raise concerns at the broader overall level (see also paragraphs 120-121).
(393)    First, linear children’s TV channels are on a downward trajectory across the EEA. At the
         same time, non-linear services gain from this decline with consumption of children’s
         content on SVOD platforms continuing to grow and constrain the position of linear TV
         in the consumption of children’s content. In this landscape, competitive pressure on the
         Parties’ children’s linear pay TV channels comes not only from rival TV channel
         operators, but also from non-linear SVOD services which act as a constraint.
(394)    The decline of linear TV will continue to constrain wholesale suppliers of children’s pay
         TV channels, including the merged entity, from attempting to extract more favourable
         terms from retail TV providers/MVPDs, as an attempt to worsen competitive conditions
         for the supply of linear children’s pay TV channels post-Transaction would serve to
         accelerate that trend.
(395)    The market investigation confirmed this market trend/shift, and the one-way competitive
         pressure exerted by non-linear SVOD services on linear children’s pay TV channels.
(396)    In particular, the majority of respondents stated that there has been a decline in children’s
         consumption of children’s programming on dedicated children’s pay TV channels. (414)
         In addition, the majority of respondents submitted that the children’s content
         consumption has shifted/transitioned to SVOD platforms and other OTT services
         offering children’s content. (415)


(414)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q2 Audiovisual value chain’, question D.B.6
(415)   Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q2 Audiovisual value chain’, question D.B.7.
                                                          84
(397)    In this respect a respondent noted that ‘there has been a gradual decline in children’s
         consumption of dedicated children’s pay TV channels in recent years, driven by the
         increasing use of SVOD platforms and other online video services that offer on-demand
         access to children’s content. In our view, viewing patterns among younger audiences
         have shifted towards non-linear consumption, resulting in reduced time spent on
         traditional linear children’s channels, although these channels continue to form part of
         many pay TV packages. Similarly, another respondent stated that ‘children’s
         consumption of programming on dedicated children’s pay TV channels has declined over
         time. This appears to reflect broader changes in viewing behaviour, particularly the
         increasing use of on-demand and digital services by children and families. Children’s
         audiences increasingly consume content through SVOD and OTT platforms, including
         services such as Disney+, Netflix and YouTube, which offer flexible, on-demand access
         to children’s programming. As a result, dedicated children’s pay TV channels face
         growing competition for children’s viewing time and attention. While dedicated
         children’s channels continue to play a role within the audiovisual ecosystem, they
         operate in a market where a growing share of children’s content consumption takes place
         through non-linear services’. A further respondent explained that this trend ‘should also
         be seen in the context of pay TV decline in general, as audiences move to streaming
         services and on-demand viewing. Children’s pay TV channels may be more affected than
         others as on-demand viewing is particularly popular with younger audiences’. Another
         respondent observed that ‘[c]hildren increasingly consume audiovisual content through
         on-demand services such as Disney+, Netflix and Amazon Prime Video, as well as online
         video platforms such as YouTube. These services offer flexible viewing, extensive content
         libraries and personalised user experiences that are particularly attractive to younger
         audiences. We do not consider the decline to be primarily driven by reduced demand for
         children’s content. Rather, it reflects a shift in consumption from linear children’s
         channels towards non-linear and digital services’. (416)
(398)    Furthermore, – consistent with the above-described increased viewership of young
         audiences on non-linear SVOD platforms – almost half of the wholesale suppliers of
         children’s TV channels, who responded to the market investigation, submitted that they
         have experienced a downward pressure on the licencing/carriage fees charged for their
         children’s pay TV channels, in view of the availability of children’s content on SVOD
         platforms and the corresponding reduced unwillingness of MVPDs to pay for children’s
         pay TV channels. (417) Of those market participants who have experienced a downward
         pressure, one respondent indicated that ‘[w]e have experienced this pressure on licence
         fees for children’s channels; partly that should be seen in the wider context of pay TV
         licensing becoming more challenged across all genres, but children’s pay TV channels
         may be more affected than others as on-demand viewing is particularly popular with
         younger audiences’. Another respondent explained that ‘[r]etail TV distributors/MVPDs
         increasingly take into account the presence of SVOD services when assessing the
         incremental value of linear children’s channels within their overall offering’. (418)
(399)    Second, the majority of respondents consider that Paramount’s children’s pay TV
         channels (Nickelodeon, Nick Jr., Nicktoons, TeenNick) and Warner’s children’s pay TV
         channels (Cartoon Network, Cartoonito and Boomerang) provide a similar proposition,
         for example in terms of price, content, targeted audience and viewership. (419)
(400)    However, at the same time the majority of respondents consider that the same or very
         similar children’s AV content is available on children’s pay TV channels and on SVOD

         platforms. (420) In this regard, the majority of respondents consider that non-linear SVOD
         services (such as Disney+, Netflix, and Amazon Prime), to the extent they offer
         children’s content, compete with or at least exert a competitive constraint on Paramount’s
         and Warner’s children’s pay TV channels. (421) (422)
(401)    For instance, respondents submitted that ‘[t]here is significant competition between
         traditional TV channels and platforms’, that SVOD platforms offer ‘a lot of children’s
         content competing directly with the content on Paramount’s and Warner’s children
         linear Pay channels’, as well as that ‘Paramount and Warner's children's pay-TV
         channels compete with the offerings of other major SVoD services and YouTube. All
         major SVoD services offer a wide selection of dedicated children's content, and typically
         offer dedicated children's profiles, which only allow access to age-appropriate content’.
         Another respondent explained that Disney+ constrains the Parties’ children’s pay TV
         channels as ‘it offers a highly attractive children’s proposition, with globally recognised
         brands (Disney, Pixar, Marvel, Star Wars) and a deep library of premium content. This
         gives it strong audience appeal and viewing time share, particularly among families and
         younger children. As an on‑demand SVOD service, Disney+ differs from linear channels
         (Nickelodeon, Cartoon Network, etc.), but in practice it competes for the same attention,
         engagement, and household budgets. Its ad‑free environment, binge‑watching model,
         and cross‑device availability make it particularly compelling compared to scheduled
         programming. For MVPDs, this creates a substitution effect: households may rely more
         on Disney+ and less on traditional children’s channels, reducing viewership and
         potentially weakening the bargaining position of those channels’. A further respondent
         noted that ‘Netflix exerts a significant competitive constraint on dedicated children’s TV
         channels through its extensive children’s content offering and on-demand viewing model.
         Families increasingly consume children’s content through streaming services, and
         Netflix competes directly for viewing time, engagement and subscription
         spending’. Another respondent submitted that ‘Amazon Prime Video exerts a competitive
         constraint on dedicated children’s TV channels by offering a range of children’s
         programming through an on-demand viewing model. Although children’s content is not
         the sole focus of the service, it provides families with an alternative way to access
         children’s entertainment content. As a result, Amazon Prime Video competes for
         children’s viewing time and attention and may reduce reliance on traditional linear
         children’s channels’. (423)
(402)    On the other hand, some respondents noted that certain differences exist between the
         SVOD and pay TV models. In particular, respondents pointed to the fact that SVOD
         services ‘are accessed through separate subscriptions’, do not provide ‘a continuous,
         scheduled programming stream that is available "in the background" without the need
         to actively select content’, and that availability of local language-dubbed children’s
         content may be ‘limited or more fragmented’ on SVOD platforms. (424)
(403)    Yet, despite these differences between the SVOD and pay TV models, the majority of
         respondents considers that SVOD exert a competitive constraint on children’s pay TV
         channels. (425)
(404)    Furthermore, the majority of retail TV distributors/MVPDs that responded to the market
         investigation confirmed that they carry on their retail platform both children’s pay TV

         channels and SVOD services. (426) As a result, viewers can access a wide selection of
         children’s content available both on pay TV channels and on SVOD services via the same
         retail platform. In this respect, a respondent noted that ‘[t]raditional TV channels are
         combined with SVOD services from both local and international providers in an
         aggregated retail offering from us’. Another respondent stated that ‘[w]e distribute many
         children TV channels and SVOD services. [Our] strategy is to be a one stop shop offer
         proposing as many SVOD services as possible in one single platform’. A further
         respondent indicated that ‘[o]ur platform offers both dedicated children's pay-TV
         channels and access to SVOD services. […] As a result, customers can access children's
         content through both traditional linear TV channels and subscription-based on-demand
         services on our platform’. (427)
(405)    Third, as set out above at paragraphs 111-112 wholesale suppliers of TV channels
         typically negotiate with MVPDs for a bundle of channels, including multiple TV
         channels spanning various genres (or for a bundle of SVOD/VOD services and a bouquet
         of channels). (428) Hence, competition in the wholesale supply of TV channels typically
         occurs on a portfolio basis (between bundles), rather than on a channel-by-channel or
         genre-by-genre basis. As such, it is typically the overall bundle that matters in the
         wholesale negotiations, as opposed to a specific channel or genre. The Transaction will
         not alter this approach, which is common across the industry.
(406)    Should the merged entity continue to negotiate on a portfolio basis post-Transaction, this
         would not result in any incremental bargaining power vis-à-vis MVPDs, because: (i)
         children’s pay TV channels are not decisive drivers of subscription choices and,
         therefore, have a limited relevance to driving wholesale portfolio-based
         negotiations, (429) and (ii) even if some Paramount children’s pay TV channels were to
         matter for MVPDs’ purchasing choices, (430) Warner’s children’s pay TV channels that
         will be added to the Paramount offering post-Transaction are not considered important
         or must-have channels in the EEA (431).
(407)    In what concerns children’s pay TV channels (point (i) above), when asked about the
         importance/relevance of children’s TV channels in wholesale portfolio-negotiations, the
         majority of respondents indicated, either that children’s TV channels are an important
         but not decisive factor in wholesale negotiations, or that they have no or minimal
         relevance in driving wholesale negotiations (including the price of the bundle). (432) In
         particular, a respondent noted that ‘children’s TV channels have traditionally had
         important value in portfolio-based wholesale negotiations. Children’s channels help
         ensure a balanced TV offering and are relevant for households with children. They have
         therefore historically been an important part of channel portfolios. However, in recent
         years [we have] observed a decline in the viewership and relative importance of
         children’s linear TV channels. Children’s content is increasingly consumed through on-
         demand services, streaming platforms and online video services. As a result, dedicated
         children’s TV channels still have relevance in wholesale negotiations, but their value is
         decreasing compared to other categories, in particular sports, films and premium
         entertainment content’. Similarly, another respondent indicated that ‘[t]here is an
         observable trend of children’s viewing habits shifting away from traditional linear TV
         channels towards SVOD services and online platforms such as YouTube. As a result,
         children’s TV channels have diminished in importance in commercial negotiations’. A

         further respondent stated that children’s pay TV channels ‘are generally of lower value
         than broader general entertainment or sports channels and thus have a relatively small
         proportion of the overall value being negotiated’. (433)
(408)    In what concerns Warner’s children’s pay TV channels (point (ii) above), most retail TV
         distributors/MVPDs that responded to the market investigation do not consider Warner’s
         children’s pay TV channels to be particularly important or a ‘must have’ for their retail
         offering in the EEA (e.g., in terms of audience or driver of subscriptions, branding,
         specific programming and/or any other relevant factors). (434) For instance, a respondent
         explained that ‘Warner’s children’s pay TV channels form part of a broader portfolio of
         children’s content but are not generally considered “must-have” channels. While they
         contribute to the attractiveness of the overall channel line-up, comparable children’s
         content is also available from other channels and platforms, including other pay TV
         channels and SVOD services. Accordingly, their importance should be assessed in the
         context of the overall range of children’s content available to viewers’. Another
         respondent noted that ‘[w]hile Warner’s children’s channels contribute to the overall
         attractiveness and completeness of a retail TV offering, we would not consider them to
         be “must-have” channels. Children’s content remains important for households with
         children, but consumers today have access to a wide range of alternative sources of
         children’s programming, including other children’s channels, free-to-air services, SVOD
         platforms such as Disney+ and Netflix, and OTT services such as YouTube. In addition,
         children’s viewing behaviour has increasingly shifted towards on-demand consumption.
         As a result, the absence of a specific children’s channel portfolio would not typically be
         expected to have a decisive impact on subscription decisions or the overall
         competitiveness of a retail audiovisual offering. Accordingly, while Warner’s children’s
         channels represent a valuable component of a broader content portfolio, they are not
         considered indispensable or uniquely capable of driving subscriptions’. (435)
(409)    Fourth, the majority of respondents consider that there exist barriers to entry in the
         potential subsegment for the wholesale supply of children’s pay TV channels, with the
         most considerable barrier being the access to children’s content. (436) In this regard, the
         Commission considers that the Transaction will not result in increased barriers to access
         children’s content (and thus in a deterioration of current market conditions) as the
         combined share of the Parties in the supply of children’s AV content (the input to produce
         children’s pay TV channels and sell them at wholesale level) at national level and in the
         EEA is generally low and remains below the level that would be considered as ‘affected’
         within the meaning of the Merger Regulation and the Notice on Simplified
         Treatment. (437) As a result of its limited market position in the supply of children’s AV
         content, the merged entity would not have the ability to withhold such AV content (or
         make it available at worse terms and conditions) thus raising the barriers for rival pay TV
         operators to compete on the market for the wholesale supply of children’s pay TV
         channels.
(410)    In light of the above considerations and market trends, despite the Parties’ high combined
         shares in a narrowly delineated market segment comprising solely children’s pay TV
         channels, the Commission considers that the merged entity will continue to be
         constrained by a sufficient number of alternative suppliers of children’s programming
         post-Transaction. As a result, the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
