            market for the wholesale supply of children’s pay TV channels at the national level.

6.4.5.2.1.3.     Conclusion
(411)       For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction will not raise
            serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal
            non-coordinated effects on the market for the wholesale supply of children’s pay TV
            channels at the national level.

6.4.5.2.2         Wholesale supply of pay TV channels
(412)       As set out in Section 6.3, the Parties have horizontally overlapping activities that give
            rise to affected markets in the potential market for the wholesale supply of pay TV
            channels in Ireland and Poland.

6.4.5.2.2.1.     The Notifying Party’s views
(413)       First, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties’ combined shares in the potential
            markets for the wholesale supply of pay TV channels in Ireland and Poland are modest,
            with limited increments.
(414)       In Ireland, the Parties’ combined share in 2024 was [20-30]%, with an increment of
            [5-10]% (from Paramount). Paramount’s market share has since decreased, as Paramount
            discontinued four pay TV channels as of 31 December 2025. Excluding these pay TV
            channels from the 2024 market share estimates would bring the increment down to
            [5-10]%, with a combined share at [20-30]%. (438)
(415)       In Poland, the Parties’ combined share in 2024 was [30-40]%, with a limited increment
            of [5-10]% from Paramount. The increment has since decreased, as Paramount
            discontinued seven channels as of 31 December 2025. Excluding these pay TV channels
            from the 2024 market share estimates would bring the increment down to [0-5]%, with a
            combined share at [30-40]%. (439)
(416)       Second, the Notifying Party notes that the merged entity will continue facing strong
            competition post-Transaction.
(417)       The market for linear TV channels has undergone a marked structural decline in recent
            years, driven by the rapid growth of SVOD and other non-linear services. The popularity
            of linear TV is in sustained decline as end-customers shift to other viewing non-linear
            alternatives, mainly SVOD services. In response, linear TV providers – including
            Paramount in both Ireland and Poland – are gradually discontinuing their TV channels or
            consolidating their feeds across multiple countries. In this landscape, SVOD services act
            as strong competitive constraint on pay TV channels in Ireland and Poland. Thus, any
            attempt to worsen competitive conditions for the supply of pay TV channels post-
            Transaction would only serve to accelerate that trend. (440)
(418)       In addition to SVOD, the merged entity will continue facing competitive constraints from
            a range of pay TV channel suppliers, including: Polsat, TVP, Disney, Canal+, Kino
            Polska TV, Antenna and IKO Media Group in Poland; Sky and Disney in Ireland. (441)
(419)       Furthermore, the Parties’ pay TV channels will continue to be competitively constrained
            by FTA TV channel suppliers. FTA TV channels are widely carried across retail TV
            distributor’s/MVPDs’ bundles and offer similar content to the content available on

(438)   Form CO, paragraph 976.
(439)   Form CO, paragraph 974.
(440)   Form CO, paragraphs 961, 977.
(441)   Form CO, paragraphs 979-980, 984.
                                                      89
         Warner’s and Paramount’s pay TV channels. FTA TV suppliers include: TVP, Polsat,
         Telewizja Puls, MWE Networks, Stopklatka and Wirtualna Polska in Poland; and BBC,
         Raidió Teilifís Éirann, Channel 4 and Virgin Media in Ireland. (442)
(420)    Third, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties are not close competitors. Their pay
         TV channel portfolios are broadly complementary, not competing head-to-head. While
         both Parties distribute children’s and film channels, their broader portfolios are otherwise
         distinct. For example, Warner distributes pay TV sports channels (such as Eurosport in
         Ireland and Poland, and TNT Sports in Ireland), and news channels (CNN International
         in Ireland and Poland, and TVN24 and TVN24 BiS in Poland). Paramount, on the other
         hand, does not distribute any dedicated sports or news channels in Ireland, Poland or any
         other EEA country. In factual programming, such as documentaries, Paramount does not
         operate any channels in the EEA. (443) In turn, Warner offers a wide factual portfolio that
         extends into science, technology, nature, wildlife and engineering. Regarding lifestyle
         TV channels, Warner operates Home & Garden TV, Discovery Turbo, DTX, and Food
         Network, an area where Paramount has no presence. Instead, Paramount’s channel lineup
         includes MTV that delivers youth- and pop-culture content centered around music and
         celebrity culture. By contrast, Warner offers no comparable lifestyle channels. Last,
         Paramount’s portfolio also includes Comedy Central, a dedicated comedy channel.
         Warner, in turn, does not have a comparable channel. (444)
(421)    Fourth, the Notifying Party notes that the demand side of the market is concentrated in a
         small number of large, sophisticated retail TV distributors/MVPDs, with substantial
         customer bases and developed packaging capabilities (notably, Sky, Virgin Media, Eir
         TV and Vodafone TV in Ireland; Polsat, Play, Canal+ and Vectra in Poland). These
         buyers routinely substitute channels within and across channel suppliers to optimize tiers
         and add-ons and can reconfigure packages promptly without subscriber loss in response
         to any worsening of terms. These dynamics discipline any attempt by wholesale TV
         channel suppliers to worsen commercial terms, as retail TV distributors/MVPDs can
         easily (and do) switch to alternative equivalent TV channels, adjust packaging, or remove
         channels altogether. (445)

6.4.5.2.2.2.   Commission’s assessment
(422)    The Commission considers that, for the reasons set out below, the Transaction does not
         raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of
         horizontal effects in the potential markets for the wholesale supply of pay TV channels
         in Ireland and Poland.
(423)    First, the Parties’ combined market shares in the market for the supply of pay TV
         channels in Ireland and Poland will remain moderate ([20-30]% in Ireland and [30-40]%
         in Poland) (see Table 10).
(424)    Second, the merged entity will continue to be constrained by a sufficient number of
         alternative suppliers post-Transaction.
(425)    In relation to Ireland, the majority of respondents to the market investigation indicated
         that the current level of competition in the potential market for the wholesale supply of
         pay TV channels in Ireland is high or very high. (446) Respondents identified a number
         of suppliers of pay TV channels as competitors to the Parties’ pay TV channels, including

6.4.5.2.2.3.      Conclusion
(427)       For the reasons set out above, the Commission concludes that the Transaction will not
            raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of
            horizontal non-coordinated effects on the markets for the wholesale supply of pay TV
            channels in Ireland and Poland.

6.4.5.2.3         Wholesale supply of general entertainment pay TV channels
(428)       As set out in Section 6.3., the Parties have horizontally overlapping activities that give
            rise to affected markets in the potential markets for the wholesale supply of general
            entertainment pay TV channels in Finland and Ireland.

6.4.5.2.3.1.      The Notifying Party’s views
(429)       First, the Notifying Party submits that the Parties’ combined shares in the potential
            markets for the wholesale supply of general entertainment pay TV channels in Finland
            and Ireland are moderate at [30-40]% in Finland and [30-40]% in Ireland. (452)
(430)       Second, the Notifying Party notes that the merged entity will continue facing strong
            competition post-Transaction.
(431)       Consistent with the competitive trends in the overall pay TV market, described above at
            paragraph 417, SVOD services draw viewers away from general entertainment pay TV
            channels. SVOD services in Finland and Ireland offer extensive catalogues of series,
            reality programming, documentaries, and other general entertainment content that
            directly overlaps with the content carried on the Parties’ general entertainment pay TV
            channels. Therefore, SVOD services significantly constrain suppliers of general
            entertainment pay TV channels. (453)
(432)       In addition to SVOD, the merged entity will continue facing competitive constraints from
            a range of general entertainment pay TV channel suppliers, including: Viasat World,
            Disney, Hearst Networks and SVT in Finland; Sky, BBC, Disney, NBCUniversal, Hearst
            Networks and Narrative Entertainment in Ireland. (454)

(434)    Third, the Notifying Party notes that the general entertainment pay TV channels that the

(435)    In Finland, Paramount’s offering consists of MTV, which focuses on music, pop culture,

         documentary programming.
(436)    In Ireland, while both Parties offer crime-themed channels, Paramount’s offering consists

         sitcoms, and satire. By contrast, Warner’s offering focuses on factual and documentary
         programming (Animal Planet, Discovery Channel, Discovery Science, ID, Quest), and
         lifestyle content (HGTV, Food Network, Really, DMAX, TLC).
(437)    Overall, there is no meaningful overlap in focus between the Parties’ portfolios and as
         such their combination post-Transaction could not lead to a decrease in competition
         between the Parties for this set of channels in Finland and Ireland. (456)
(438)    Fourth, similar to what is mentioned in paragraph 421 above, the demand side of the
         market is concentrated in a small number of large, sophisticated pay TV providers,
         principally: Telia, DNA, Elisa, and Allente in Finland; Eir TV, Sky, Virgin Media, and
         BT Ireland in Ireland. These buyers have substantial bargaining power and can credibly
         threaten to substitute or drop channels in response to any deterioration of terms, ensuring
         that the merged entity will be unable to leverage its combined share to extract more
         favourable wholesale conditions post-Transaction. (457)

6.4.5.2.3.2.    Commission’s assessment
(439)    The Commission considers that, for the reasons set out below, the Transaction does not

         general entertainment pay TV channels in Finland and Ireland.
(440)    First, the Parties’ combined market shares in the markets for the supply of general

(441)    Second, the merged entity will continue to be constrained by a sufficient number of

(442)    In relation to Finland, the overwhelming majority of respondents to the market

         the wholesale supply of general entertainment pay TV channels in Finland is moderate
         to very high. (458) Respondents identified a number of suppliers of general entertainment
         pay TV channels as competitors to the Parties’ general entertainment pay TV channels,
         including Viasat, Canal+, SVT1 and MTV OY. (459) Furthermore, most respondents

6.4.5.3. Retail supply of AV services
(445)       The Parties’ activities horizontally overlap with regard to their activities in SVOD.
(446)       In the EEA, Paramount only offers retail AV services through its SVOD services
            Paramount+ and SkyShowtime, and its FAST service Pluto TV. Warner is active in this
            market via its SVOD services including its HBO Max, Discovery+, and TVN/Player
            (Player.pl).

6.4.5.3.1         The Notifying Party’s views
(447)       The Notifying Party submits that the horizontal overlap between Paramount and Warner
            on the market for the retail supply of AV services will not lead to any competition
            concerns at the EEA or national level for the following reasons.
(448)       First, the Parties’ combined market shares in the supply of SVOD services are below the
            level that could indicate concerns. The Transaction gives rise to only seven affected
            markets in each of which the Parties’ combined shares remain moderate irrespective of
            the metric. On a revenue basis, in 2024, the Parties’ combined share reaches [30-40]%
            only in two Member States, Finland ([30-40]%) and Sweden ([30-40]%). The Notifying
            Party considers that revenue-based shares are a better measure of competition in SVOD
            services because they better capture customers’ willingness to pay for a certain product
            offering. Subscription-based shares also capture indirect subscriptions where the Parties’
            SVOD offering is part of third parties’ TV or telecom service bundles (e.g., Sky TV in
            Ireland) often at no extra cost. Such subscriptions are thus not a good metric to assess the
            customers’ demand for the SVOD service itself. (464)
(449)       Second, Paramount is a minor player in the supply of SVOD services in the EEA, adding
            a small increment across the seven affected markets. (465)
(450)       Third, the supply of SVOD services globally and across the EEA is dominated by Netflix
            which will remain a significant competitive constraint to all SVOD platforms. Netflix

(451)       Fourth, in addition to Netflix, the merged entity will continue to be constrained by

6.4.5.3.2         Commission’s assessment
(452)       Given that horizontally affected markets arise only at the level of SVOD services and do
            not arise in any other sub-segmentations of the retail AV market, the Commission’s
            market investigation and assessment focused on this plausible sub-segmentation.
(453)       The Commission considers that, for the reasons set out below, the Transaction does not
            raise serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of
            horizontal non-coordinated effects in the markets for the retail supply of AV and the retail
            supply of SVOD services at the EEA or national level.
(454)       First, the Parties’ combined shares are moderate, often with a limited increment from
            Paramount. On a revenue basis, in 2024, the Parties’ shares reached 30% only in two
            Member States, Finland ([30-40]%) and Sweden ([30-40]%), with a [0-5]% increment
            from Paramount in both countries. (468) On a subscription basis, in 2024, affected markets
            arise in Denmark ([20-30]% combined share, with a [5-10]% increment from
            Paramount), Finland ([30-40]% combined share with a [5-10]% increment from
            Paramount), Hungary ([30-40]% combined share, with a [10-20]% increment from
            Warner), Ireland ([20-30]% combined share with a [10-20]% increment from Warner),
            Poland ([30-40]% combined share with a [10-20]% increment from Paramount), Norway
            ([30-40]% combined share with a [10-20]% increment from Paramount) and Sweden
            ([30-40]% combined share with a [10-20]% increment from Paramount), as per Table 15
            above.
(455)       Second, Netflix is the market leader in the market for the retail supply of SVOD services
            in the EEA and in many countries where affected markets arise. Netflix is the market
            leader in Denmark, with a [30-40]% share by revenue and [20-30]% by subscriptions
            (compared to [20-30]% by revenue and [20-30]% by subscriptions for the merged entity);
            in Finland with a [40-50]% share by revenue and [30-40]% by subscriptions (compared
            to [30-40]% by revenue and [30-40]% by subscriptions for the merged entity); in Ireland,
            with a [50-60]% share by revenue and [40-50]% by subscriptions (compared to [10-20]%
            by revenue and [20-30]% by subscriptions for the merged entity), and in Poland, with a
            [50-60]% share by revenue and [40-50]% by subscriptions (compared to [20-30]% by
            revenue and [30-40]% by subscriptions for the merged entity). (469)
(456)       In Hungary and Sweden, Netflix will remain the market leader by revenue ([40-50]% and
            [30-40]%, respectively) and will be the second player by subscriptions, behind the
            merged entity (with a [20-30]% share in Hungary compared to [30-40]% for the merged

(458)    When asked to rate the relative strength of the different SVOD providers in each of the

         Netflix has the strongest offering. In each of the affected markets, the majority of

         to 5, where 5 represents the strongest offering), indicating that Netflix is widely perceived
         as offering the strongest SVOD service among the providers assessed. (472)
(459)    Third, in addition to Netflix, numerous players across the EEA as well as national players
         will continue to constrain the merged entity. The market investigation consistently
         identified, in addition to Netflix, Amazon Prime Video, Disney+ and Apple TV+ as the
         main international competitors to the Parties across all affected markets. (473)
         Respondents to the market investigation also identified other SVOD providers, including
         Crunchyroll and Rakuten Viki, as well as a number of national and regional providers,
         such as TV2 Play in Denmark (474), Ruutu+ in Finland (475), TV2 in Norway (476) and
         TV4 Play in Sweden (477), as competitive alternatives. The market investigation therefore
         confirms that the Parties operate in a competitive environment characterised by the
         presence of multiple international platforms, including specialised streaming services,
         alongside national and regional providers. Competitive pressure is reinforced by the
         characteristics of demand: subscriptions are concluded on a rolling basis without
         contractual lock-in, and consumers in the Union commonly subscribe to several SVOD
         services simultaneously. (478)
(460)    Finally, the majority of the respondents to the market investigation who expressed a view
         indicated that in the market for SVOD services, the current level of competition is high
         or very high in each of the affected markets. (479) This finding is consistent across

         considering competition to be moderate and none considering it to be low or very
         low. (480) In addition, when asked whether sufficient alternatives exist to HBO Max,
         Paramount+ and SkyShowtime, a majority of SVOD competitors indicated that such
         alternatives are available in each affected market. (481) By contrast, the results of the

6.4.5.3.3         Conclusion
(461)       For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction will not raise
            serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of horizontal
            non-coordinated effects on the market for the retail supply of AV services at the EEA or
            national level.

6.4.6.      Effects on labour markets
(462)       The Commission has examined whether the Transaction raises serious doubts as to its
            compatibility with the internal market as regards markets for the purchase of labour in
            the EEA. Article 2 of the Merger Regulation and paragraphs 61-63 of the Horizontal
            Merger Guidelines recognise that mergers creating or strengthening buyer power in
            upstream markets may significantly impede effective competition. (483)
(463)       The assessment was prompted by several third-party submissions. (484) These
            submissions raised concerns, in particular, about reduced employment opportunities,
            wage pressure, lower royalties, reduced bargaining power and employment security for
            creative and technical talent, lower production output, and potential redundancies or
            substitution of human creators.

6.4.6.1. The Notifying Party’s views
(464)       First, the Notifying Party submits that they provide limited direct employment in the
            EEA. (485) Paramount’s direct employment of specialised creative or technical workforce
            in the EEA is essentially confined to Skydance Animation Madrid (‘SAM’), a permanent
            animation facility in Spain producing animated feature films [details of contractual
            agreements with third parties]. All creative decisions are taken at Paramount’s Los
            Angeles headquarters; SAM’s EEA workforce [details of the Parties’ business
            activities]. (486) For all other EEA production activities — live-action features, television
            and visual effects — Paramount engages third-party local production companies as the
            actual employers of the relevant workforce. (487)
(465)       Warner’s principal direct-employing entity in the EEA is Warner Bros. International
            Television Production. For the majority of its local productions Warner similarly relies
            on third-party production service companies, though it may engage [details of the Parties’
            business activities] (as defined in paragraph (475)) [details of the Parties’ business
            activities]. (488)
(466)       Second, the Notifying Party submits that demand for creative and technical talent in the
            EEA is highly fragmented across numerous local production companies, broadcasters
            and international productions, and that the Transaction would not materially alter
            competitive dynamics on the relevant labour markets. (489) The Notifying Party identifies

            multiple alternative animation employers across the EEA and notes that animation
            professionals’ skills are transferable to adjacent industries including technology,
            marketing, real estate and gaming. (490)
(467)       More broadly, the Notifying Party submits that the merged entity will face competition
            for talent from US studios including Netflix, Amazon MGM, Apple, Sony, Universal and
            Disney, as well as European players including StudioCanal, Pathé, Gaumont, Nordisk
            Film, Constantin Film and SF Studios, and that creative talent is generally engaged on a
            non-exclusive, project-based basis. (491)
(468)       Third, the Notifying Party submits that the EEA workforce of each Party is subject to
            binding collective bargaining agreements and/or comprehensive statutory social and
            labour protections. SAM’s entire workforce is covered by the Convenio Colectivo de
            Oficinas y Despachos applicable in the Autonomous Community of Madrid, establishing
            binding minimum salaries, working time caps and restrictions on temporary contracts,
            with disapplication possible only on limited grounds following formal consultation with
            employee representatives. (492) Warner identifies collective bargaining arrangements in
            France, Germany, Italy, the Nordic countries and Spain, and both Parties note the
            comprehensive statutory social and labour framework applicable across EEA Member
            States. (493)
(469)       Fourth, the Notifying Party submits that the engagement and compensation of above-the-
            line talent for its productions are largely governed by US guild frameworks. Whether
            those frameworks apply depends on where and by whom the talent is engaged (that is,
            where the writing is performed or the production is shot, or whether the engaging entity
            is US-based) and not on where the resulting production is later exhibited. According to
            the Notifying Party, it follows that the subsequent distribution or licensing of a
            production in the EEA does not, of itself, change the framework that governs the talent
            engaged to make it. (494)
(470)       For productions involving EEA production activity, the Notifying Party submits that
            competition for writers and performers is shaped by language and local cultural
            knowledge, and that the ‘European works’ obligations under the Audiovisual Media
            Services Directive sustain demand for productions made with EEA-resident authors and
            workers. The Notifying Party submits that competition for certain talent operates
            differently: for directors and internationally recognised performers in particular, it takes
            place across jurisdictions and, for some engagements, globally. (495)

6.4.6.2. Commission’s assessment
(471)       The Commission considers, for the reasons below, that the Transaction does not raise
            serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as regards any labour
            market in the EEA.

6.4.6.2.1         Analytical framework
(472)       Anticompetitive effects on labour markets are more likely where: (i) purchasing markets
            are highly concentrated while sellers — here, workers — are fragmented and lack




(490)   Form CO, Annex 28, paragraphs 13-15.
(491)   Form CO, paragraphs 451, 670-671.
(492)   Form CO, Annex 28, paragraphs 24, 29-31.
(493)   Form CO, Annex 28, paragraphs 22-27, 33.
(494)   Response to RFI 31, questions 2-3.

            countervailing power; (496) (ii) workers have access to few alternative employment
            options comparable to those offered by the merging firms; (497) and (iii) effective
            collective bargaining or statutory regulation does not adequately constrain the merged
            entity’s conduct. (498)
(473)       The Commission applies the Market Definition Notice to the definition of relevant labour
            markets, treating the identification of alternative employment options as the primary
            competitive constraint on the merging firms as buyers. (499) Workers are assessed broadly
            irrespective of contractual conditions.
(474)       The Commission considers solely effects attributable to the loss of competition resulting
            from the merger. Corporate restructuring effects occurring independently of any change
            in competitive dynamics fall outside the scope of this assessment under Article 2 of the
            Merger Regulation. The concern regarding AI substitution likewise falls outside this
            framework since the potential use of AI in content production is not a consequence of the
            loss of head-to-head competition between the merging firms as employers and does not
            constitute an effect on competition in labour markets within the meaning of Article 2 of
            the Merger Regulation.

6.4.6.2.2         Market definition
(475)       The Commission considers as potential relevant labour markets the market or markets
            for the employment of specialised creative and technical personnel engaged in the
            production of audiovisual content in the EEA, with potential sub-segments for (i) above-
            the-line creative talent (i.e., senior creative personnel — typically directors, producers
            and screenwriters — whose engagement is individually negotiated and recorded
            separately from production costs on a project’s budget) and (ii) below-the-line technical
            and production crew (i.e., personnel engaged, often pursuant to collective agreements, to
            execute the physical production). The precise boundaries need not be resolved as the
            Transaction does not give rise to concerns under any plausible definition.
(476)       The Commission has considered whether differences in compensation structures between
            major studio engagements and streaming platform mandates — notably the absence in
            streaming mandates of residuals, backend participations and profit-sharing arrangements
            that are standard in theatrical and broadcast engagements — could support a narrower
            market excluding streaming platforms as comparable alternative employers for above-
            the-line talent.
(477)       The Notifying Party submits that differences in compensation structures between
            theatrical and streaming engagements reflect different risk/reward profiles rather than
            non-substitutability. For theatrical productions, fixed fees are typically supplemented by
            backend participations, box office bonuses and residuals calculated across sequential

(496)   Commission decision of 9 June 2023 in case M.10433 – Vivendi/Lagardere, paragraphs 583, 600: the loss
        of an important and close competitor on the upstream markets for the acquisition of publishing rights was
        found to increase the merged entity’s market power and correspondingly to reduce authors' bargaining power
        (‘une importante réduction du pouvoir de négociation des auteurs’).
(497)   Commission decision of 9 June 2023 in case M.10433 – Vivendi/Lagardere, paragraphs 585-594, 599.
(498)   On the constraining role of statutory regulation, the Commission refers to Directive (EU) 2019/790 of
        17 April 2019 on copyright and related rights in the Digital Single Market (OJ L 130, 17.5.2019, p. 92) and
        in particular to Article 18 and recital 73, conferring on authors and performers a right to appropriate and
        proportionate remuneration, to be implemented including through collective bargaining. On collective
        bargaining, the Commission’s Guidelines on the application of Union competition law to collective
        agreements regarding the working conditions of solo self-employed persons (OJ C 374, 30.9.2022, p. 2),
        points 33, 38-39, recognise that solo self-employed persons, including authors and performers, may face an
        imbalance in bargaining power that collective bargaining serves to redress.
(499)   Market Definition Notice, paragraph 7. Commission decision of 9 June 2023 in case M.10433 -
        Vivendi/Lagardère, paragraphs 594-595.
                                                           98
         distribution windows. For streaming-only productions, compensation typically takes the
         form of a higher all-in fixed fee, effectively buying out the backend in recognition that
         streaming content does not generate attributable per-title revenue streams. The Notifying
         Party submits that these differences do not prevent above-the-line talent from treating the
         two models as meaningful alternatives: talent routinely works across both distribution
         models on a project-by-project basis, overall and first-look agreements cover both film
         and television projects without distinguishing by distribution model, and high-profile
         talent regularly moves between theatrical and streaming engagements across multiple
         studios and platforms.
(478)    The Notifying Party further notes that the 2023 collective bargaining agreements with
         the principal US creative guilds (which apply to the vast majority of productions
         originated by the Parties, including those with EEA distribution) introduced viewership-
         based streaming participation bonuses and updated residual formulae for SVOD
         exploitation, narrowing the structural gap between the two compensation models. (500)
         The Commission notes that this characterisation is contested by the counterparties to
         those agreements. On their account, the viewership-based bonuses narrow the difference
         in writers' potential compensation between the two models but do not eliminate it. (501)
         The Commission’s assessment of the relevant market does not turn on this point.
(479)    On the basis of the available evidence, the Commission finds this distinction based on
         compensation differences insufficient to establish a separate and more concentrated
         purchasing market. No EEA-based guild, trade union or talent agency submitted evidence
         in the market investigation that streaming mandates are systematically perceived by EEA
         creative talent as non-comparable to theatrical or broadcast engagements. This
         conclusion is supported in the present case by the market investigation, in which the
         majority of non-confidential respondents confirmed that competition for talent operates
         across film studios, audiovisual content producers and streaming platforms without
         meaningful segmentation, (502) and by the Notifying Party’s submission that talent
         routinely works across both distribution models within single contractual relationships
         and that overall and first-look agreements do not distinguish by distribution model. (503).
         In any event, and as set out in Section 6.4.6.2.3, the conclusion is unaffected by whether
         streaming platforms (as employers) are included within or excluded from the relevant
         market.
(480)    The Commission has considered the geographic scope of the potential labour markets
         identified above. Consistent with the findings in Section 5.2.2, competition for certain
         categories of talent (in particular internationally recognised directors and performers, and
         screenwriters working in English-language productions) may extend beyond the EEA
         and, for some engagements, operate globally. For many other engagements, and
         particularly for local-language productions, competition for writers and performers is
         localised by language, local cultural knowledge, the residence and work-authorisation of
         the personnel concerned, and the national collective-bargaining frameworks applicable
         to local production activity, such that it takes place at national or EEA level.

(481)       This differentiated geographic scope is reinforced by the applicable regulatory and
            collective-bargaining frameworks, (504) which delineate a pool of US-engaged talent
            distinct from the EEA-engaged and EEA-resident talent relevant to the present
            assessment.
(482)       The precise geographic scope of the potential labour markets — whether EEA-wide or
            narrower, by national or linguistic area — can be left open, since the Transaction does
            not raise serious doubts on any labour market in the EEA under any plausible geographic
            delineation, for the following reasons.

6.4.6.2.3         Assessment on the merits
(483)       First, the merged entity’s combined direct employment of EEA creative and technical
            talent is limited. Paramount’s direct presence is confined to SAM, [details of contractual
            agreements with third parties] and does not constitute a significant buyer of above-the-
            line creative talent in the EEA. (505) For live-action and television production, both
            Parties [details of the Parties’ business activities]. (506) The Transaction does not alter the
            structural position of those third-party employers. [Details of the Parties’ business
            activities], the merged entity’s combined footprint as a direct employer remains limited
            relative to the overall size and fragmentation of the relevant talent pool.
(484)       Second, the EEA labour markets for audiovisual creative and technical talent are
            characterised by a large number of alternative employers, including other major US
            studios with EEA production activities, European public and commercial broadcasters
            commissioning original content, and a significant independent production sector. (507)
            The emergence of streaming platforms and independent studios has further expanded the
            employer landscape; studios entering production since 2012 including Netflix, Amazon,
            Apple, A24 and NEON confirm that no employer holds a critical position in the relevant
            talent markets. (508)
(485)       In the market investigation, the majority of respondents having expressed a view
            identified streaming platforms that commission content as competitors when acquiring
            talent for audiovisual productions, alongside other film studios and other audiovisual
            content producers. (509) Several respondents confirmed that competition for talent
            operates across all three categories without meaningful segmentation. One major US
            vertically integrated studio stated that talent and other specialised personnel work with
            all of these content producers. One European film studio and distributor confirmed that
            it competes with all three types of content producers identified in the question. A further
            European film studio and distributor noted that streaming platforms frequently outbid
            traditional film studios and independent producers for talent, indicating that streaming
            platforms represent an effective — and in some cases stronger — competing source of

        demand audiovisual media services to secure at least a 30% share of European works in their catalogues,
        which sustains demand for productions that, within the meaning of Article 1(3) of that Directive, are mainly
        made with authors and workers residing in the EEA; and national collective agreements binding the local
        third-party companies that act as employers for EEA production activity. By contrast, the US guild
        frameworks on which the parallel US labour-market claims are founded attach to talent engaged in the US
        — where the writing is performed or the production is shot, or where the engaging entity is US-based —
        and follow such talent irrespective of the territory of subsequent exhibition.

                                                           100
         demand for the same talent pool. (510) One major US film studio confirmed that its
         assessment does not differ for any specific EEA Member State, suggesting that the
         competitive dynamics identified are consistent across geographies.
(486)    One Nordic respondent noted that in the Nordic countries all production teams operate
         on a freelance basis. (511) The Commission does not treat freelance, non-exclusive
         engagement as, in itself, proof of competition between buyers, since it can coexist with
         the build-up of de facto exclusivity through long-term or ‘overall’ deals. The conclusion
         rests instead on the number and diversity of alternative buyers available to EEA talent.
(487)    The Commission considers that the market investigation results do not require a
         definitive resolution of whether streaming platforms fall within or outside the relevant
         market for the purposes of this assessment. Whether the market is defined broadly to
         include streaming commissioners or more narrowly to encompass traditional film and
         television studios only, the conclusion is unaffected: the merged entity would not
         represent a critical employer for any category of EEA creative or technical talent, given
         the number and diversity of alternative employers available under either market
         definition. The question of whether the compensation structure differences identified by
         a minority of respondents are sufficiently material to support a narrower market
         definition may warrant closer examination in future cases involving more concentrated
         employer landscapes.
(488)    Third, the relevant workforce benefits from a combination of collective bargaining
         agreements and statutory social and labour protections across EEA Member States. (512)
         Whilst these arrangements do not constitute a safe harbour, they represent a meaningful
         constraint on the merged entity’s ability to reduce wages or worsen working conditions
         unilaterally and reinforce the conclusion that no anticompetitive effects on labour
         markets arise.
(489)    Fourth, the concern raised regarding the Parties’ efficiency targets and associated
         workforce reductions falls outside the scope of this assessment. The Commission
         considers solely effects attributable to the loss of competition resulting from the merger
         under Article 2 of the Merger Regulation. Corporate restructuring effects occurring
         independently of changes in competitive dynamics are not assessed under the Merger
         Regulation. In any event, the Parties’ internal documents characterise [details of the
         parties' anticipated synergies]. (513)
(490)    Fifth, some respondents were concerned that the Transaction would depress royalties and
         similar payments to creative talent. These payments are calculated in part from the
         licence fee a studio pays to put its content on a streaming platform. Where the studio and
         the platform are part of the same group, that fee is set internally rather than negotiated,
         which can lower the payments to talent. But this arises from one firm combining content
         production with its own streaming platform — which is already the case for each Party
         today, as both Paramount and Warner produce content and run their own streaming
         platforms. The Transaction does not create or strengthen that integration, so the concern
         is not specific to the Transaction.



(510)   The Commission notes that this observation, while supporting the conclusion that streaming platforms
        compete for the same talent pool, also indicates that streaming platforms may represent a stronger source of
        competing demand than traditional studios in certain circumstances. This dynamic reinforces rather than
        undermines the finding that no concentrated purchasing market arises on the employer side.
(511)   This is consistent with the Notifying Party’s submission that above-the-line talent is generally engaged on a
        non-exclusive, project-based basis across the EEA: Response of 14 June 2026 to RFI 21, question 3(a).
(512)   Form CO, Annex 28, paragraphs 22-33.
(513)   Form CO, paragraph 1604.
                                                           101
(491)       Sixth, the Commission has also considered whether the exercise of monopsony power on
            labour markets could, in addition to potential harm in the upstream market, lead to
            downstream harm through reduced volume or quality of audiovisual content available to
            consumers. This downstream theory of harm does not arise independently of the
            upstream labour market assessment: it presupposes the prior establishment of monopsony
            power, which the Commission has not found for the reasons set out above. In any event,
            the Parties’ internal documents demonstrate a post-Transaction commitment to
            maintaining and growing content output, including third-party licensing revenues
            projected to grow from approximately [details of the Parties' projected revenues and
            commercial strategy], (514) which is inconsistent with the downstream harm scenario
            described in the third-party submissions.
(492)       Seventh, the Commission has considered whether the exercise of monopsony or
            oligopsony power on labour markets could reduce the merged entity’s incentives to invest
            in new productions or in the development of creative and technical talent, distinct from
            any effect on the volume or quality of content already discussed above. No such concern
            was raised in the market investigation. In any event, the Parties’ internal documents
            project continued growth in production volumes and third-party licensing activity over
            the 2026-31 period (see paragraph (491)(490)), and do not identify any strategy of
            reducing investment in talent development, production capacity or content
            commissioning as a source of anticipated efficiencies. The Commission therefore finds
            no evidence that the Transaction would give rise to a reduction in investment or
            innovation incentives on any labour market in the EEA.
(493)       Last, the Commission has considered whether the Transaction could render coordination
            among the remaining buyers of creative and technical talent in the EEA more likely, more
            stable or more effective, and concludes that it would not. Terms of engagement for above-
            the-line talent are individually negotiated and confidential, so the transparency required
            to arrive at and monitor a common approach to purchasing terms is absent; the applicable
            collective agreements establish only published minima, which operate as a floor rather
            than as a focal point for aligning the individually negotiated terms on which competition
            for talent takes place; there is no plausible mechanism by which departures from any
            common approach could be detected or disciplined; and the merged entity’s limited
            direct-employment footprint in the EEA, together with the number and diversity of
            alternative buyers, would leave ample scope for talent to transact outside any attempted
            coordination. The Commission therefore finds no coordinated effects on any labour
            market in the EEA resulting from the Transaction.

6.4.6.2.4         Conclusion
(494)       The Commission concludes that the Transaction does not raise serious doubts as to its
            compatibility with the internal market as regards any labour market in the EEA.

6.5.        Vertical effects

6.5.1.      Legal framework
(495)       According to the Non-Horizontal Guidelines, (515) non-coordinated effects may
            significantly impede effective competition as a result of a vertical merger if such merger
            gives rise to foreclosure. Foreclosure occurs where actual or potential competitors’ access
            to supplies or markets is hampered or eliminated as a result of the merger, thereby



(514)    Form CO, paragraphs 1644-46.
(515)    Non-Horizontal Guidelines, pages 6-25.
                                                      102
          reducing those companies’ ability and/or incentive to compete. (516) Such foreclosure
          may discourage entry or expansion of competitors or encourage their exit. (517)
(496)     The Non-Horizontal Guidelines distinguish between two forms of foreclosure. First,
          input foreclosure occurs where the merger is likely to raise the costs of downstream
          competitors by restricting their access to an important input. (518) Foreclosure may also
          take more subtle forms, such as the degradation of the quality of input supplied. In its
          assessment, the Commission may consider a series of alternative or complementary
          possible strategies. (519) Second, customer foreclosure occurs where the merger is likely
          to foreclose upstream competitors by restricting their access to a sufficient customer
          base. (520)
(497)     In assessing the likelihood of an anticompetitive foreclosure scenario, the Commission
          examines, first, whether the merged entity would have, post-merger, the ability to
          substantially foreclose access to inputs or customers; second, whether it would have the
          incentive to do so; and third, whether a foreclosure strategy would have a significant
          detrimental effect on competition. In practice, these factors are often examined together
          as they are closely intertwined. (521)
(498)     Pursuant to Article 2(2) of the Merger Regulation, a concentration which would not
          significantly impede effective competition, in the internal market or in a substantial part
          of it, shall be declared compatible with the internal market. Conversely, pursuant to
          Article 2(3) of the Merger Regulation, a concentration which would significantly impede
          effective competition, in the internal market or in a substantial part of it, shall be declared
          incompatible with the internal market. In that regard, it is sufficient for the Commission
          to establish that the Transaction is likely to lead to a significant impediment of effective
          competition.

6.5.2.    Overview of vertical links
(499)     The Transaction establishes vertical relationships between the Parties’ business activities
          in the theatrical and AV value chains:
             i.    Production and licensing of distribution rights of films for theatrical release
                   (upstream) and the distribution of films for theatrical release to cinemas
                   (intermediate);
            ii.    Production and licensing of AV content (upstream), and the wholesale supply of
                   TV channels (intermediate); and
           iii.    Production and licensing of AV content (upstream) and the retail provision of
                   SVOD services (downstream).
(500)     However, these vertical relationships do not give rise to affected markets in any EEA
          country because either the Parties’ combined share does not reach 30% on either level of
          the value chain or the markets involved fall under point 5(d)(ii)(bb) of the Notice on
          Simplified Treatment (i.e., individual and combined shares are lower than 30% on the
          upstream market and the Parties’ combined purchasing share of upstream inputs is lower
          than 30%).




(516)    Non-Horizontal Guidelines, paragraph 18.

(519)    Non-Horizontal Guidelines, paragraph 33.
(520)    Non-Horizontal Guidelines, paragraph 30.
(521)    Non-Horizontal Guidelines, paragraph 32.

(501)     For completeness, this decision discusses the vertical relationship between production
          and licensing of AV content (upstream) and retail provision of SVOD services
          (downstream) and does not discuss the other links.
(502)     This decision therefore assesses whether, post-Transaction, the merged entity could
          foreclose competing SVOD rivals downstream (Section 6.5.3), as well as whether it
          could foreclose upstream suppliers’ access to SVOD distribution (Section 6.5.4).

6.5.3.    Foreclosure of downstream SVOD rivals (input foreclosure)

6.5.3.1. The Notifying Party’s views
(503)     The Notifying Party argues that the vertical relationship between production and
          licensing of AV content (upstream) and retail provision of SVOD services (downstream)
          cannot result in foreclosure of downstream SVOD rivals for the following reasons.
(504)     First, the vertical link between the Parties’ upstream licensing of commissioned or pre-
          produced AV content and the downstream retail provision of SVOD services in the EEA
          does not give rise to any vertically affected markets. (522) The Parties’ shares across all
          plausible upstream segments (see Table 7-9 above) to retail SVOD services, computed
          on a 2022-2024 three-year average basis, are well below 20%. (523) On the 2022-2024
          period, the Parties’ combined shares are below 30% in every plausible upstream segment
          and in each individual EEA country, with the exception of Production and licensing of
          pre-produced TV content to VOD services in Lithuania, where the combined share only
          marginally exceeds 30%, at [30-40]%. (524)
(505)     Second, the Parties’ already low merchant-market shares still overstate the importance
          of their content to rival SVOD operators, as most of the Parties’ revenues are
          concentrated in vertically integrated rivals that do not depend on the Parties’ content. The
          three largest SVOD players in the EEA – Netflix, Amazon and Disney – jointly account
          for approximately [details of the composition of the Parties' revenues] of Paramount’s
          SVOD licensing revenues and approximately [details of the composition of the Parties'
          sales] of Warner’s (for the period 2022-2025). Most of the remaining customers are also
          themselves vertically integrated broadcasters and streaming groups, competing
          effectively downstream where they can rely on their own production arms: [details of the
          Parties' customers]. As such, over [details of the composition of the Parties' revenues] of
          the Parties’ EEA SVOD-licensing revenues flow from vertically integrated rivals. (525)
(506)     Third, the Parties’ content is not an important input for any leading SVOD rival. The
          Parties account for only a small proportion of the overall content spend of their largest
          SVOD customers in the EEA (Netflix, Amazon and Disney). Apple, the only other rival
          for which content spend data are available, does not license any pre-produced AV content
          from the Parties in the EEA. (526)

         and Amazon ([10-20]% / [20-30]%). Netflix holds similarly high and even much larger
         market shares in the downstream SVOD market across all EEA countries. (529)

6.5.3.2. Commission’s assessment
(510)    For the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the merged entity would
         not have the ability to foreclose downstream SVOD rivals by leveraging its position in
         the market for production and licensing of AV content. Therefore, given that the
         conditions under the Non-Horizontal Guidelines are cumulative (530), the Commission
         does not need to take a position on whether the second or third conditions are satisfied.
(511)    First, as demonstrated in Section 6.3.2.1 the Parties’ combined upstream share is below
         30% in the relevant markets for production and licensing of AV content for the period of
         2022-2024. (531)
(512)    Second, a wide range of alternative suppliers would remain available post-Transaction,
         including non-integrated suppliers (e.g., A24, Lionsgate, Sony), as well as integrated
         suppliers (e.g., Amazon, Disney, Universal) that license their content to third parties. The
         majority of respondents who expressed a view and who currently license content from
         alternative suppliers indicated that, in a hypothetical scenario where the merged entity
         increased prices of licensing out their content, they would limit licensing content from
         the Parties and license more content from alternative suppliers. (532) This suggests that
         respondents regard a number of alternative suppliers as providing them with a broad
         range of options.

(515)    Further, most market participants who expressed a view indicated that they consider it

         scenario where the merged entity withdrew their content from your streaming platform,
         this would significantly affect their business as Paramount and Warner’s content is a
         significant part of their overall offering and their content is irreplaceable. (537)

         significant source of content for most market participants. Additionally, the majority of
         market participants have also stated that, in a hypothetical scenario where the merged
         entity increased prices of licensing out their content, they would either (i) stop licensing
         content from the Parties and move to alternative suppliers or (ii) limit licensing of content
         from the Parties and license more content from alternative suppliers, which indicates that
         the Parties’ content is not a ‘must-have’ for SVOD. (538) In particular, several
         respondents explained: ‘If the merged entity were to significantly increase licensing
         prices, […] would likely seek to reduce its dependence on such content and allocate a
         greater proportion of its content investment to alternative suppliers’ and ‘we expect the
         merged entity to retain its most premium content for its own platforms, making any
         remaining available content less urgent or critical to our overall portfolio. If the prices
         for this remaining content were to increase, we would limit our acquisition from the

         would seek to mitigate the impact by gradually reducing our reliance on content from
         these providers and increasing the share of content sourced from alternative
         suppliers”. (539)
(517)    Additionally, the vast majority of market participants who expressed a view have also
         stated that the level of competition in the market for pre-produced AV content is
         moderate, high or very high. (540)
(518)    Finally, the Parties’ internal documents demonstrate that the merged entity plans to
         continue licensing content to third parties post-Transaction.

6.5.4.    Foreclosure of upstream suppliers’ access to SVOD distribution (customer foreclosure)

6.5.4.1. The Notifying Party’s views
(522)     The Notifying Party argues that the vertical relationship between production and
          licensing of AV content (upstream) and retail provision of SVOD services (downstream)
          cannot result in foreclosure of upstream suppliers’ access to SVOD distribution for the
          following reasons.
(523)     First, the Parties already source a substantial majority of their content from in-house
          production already. (546) On the 2022-2024 basis, the Parties’ combined purchasing
          shares are below 30% in every plausible upstream segment and in each individual EEA
          country, with the exception of production and licensing of pre-produced non-US films to
          VOD services in Poland, where the combined share narrowly exceeded 30%, at
          [30-40]%. (547)
(524)     Second, the Parties’ content spend does not represent a significant proportion of the
          revenues of any content supplier. A decision by the merged entity not to license a given
          title from a supplier does not affect the supplier’s ability to license titles to other buyers.
          In addition, a non-negligible share of the Parties’ current content acquisition is sourced
          from suppliers that are themselves vertically integrated downstream (e.g., Amazon,
          Disney or Canal+) and therefore have their own captive outlets for their content, and the
          ability to retaliate if the Parties attempt to engage in customer foreclosure. (548)
(525)     Third, AV content producers have a wide and growing set of alternative SVOD buyers –
          Netflix, Amazon, Disney, Apple, and regional players. The Parties account for less than
          [10-20]% of retail SVOD revenues in the EEA. (549)

6.5.4.2. Commission’s assessment
(526)     For the reasons set out below, the Commission considers that the merged entity would
          not have the ability to foreclose upstream suppliers’ access to SVOD distribution.

          Therefore, given that the conditions under the Non-Horizontal Guidelines are
          cumulative (550), the Commission does not need to take a position on whether the second
          or third conditions are satisfied.
(527)     First, the Parties are not important buyers of content from third parties. The Parties’
          combined purchasing shares are below 30% in all plausible upstream segment and in
          each individual EEA country for the period of 2022-2024. (551) (552)
(528)     Second, suppliers do not license a significant proportion of their content to the Parties for
          their SVOD platforms. Most respondents who expressed a view indicated that they
          license only 0-10% of their original content to Paramount and/or Warner for their
          streaming platform(s). (553)
(529)     Third, third-party producers selling AV content to the EEA SVOD industry, in turn, have
          access to a wide range of alternative buyers, including Netflix, Amazon, Disney, Apple
          as well as regional players (e.g., RTL, TV2, Canal+, Voyo and others).
(530)     Most market participants have stated that, if the merged entity were to hypothetically
          stop or significantly limit the licensing of market participants’ original content for its
          streaming platform, while this would affect their business, they do not license their
          original content to the Parties to a significant extent compared with other streaming
          platforms. (554) One market participant stated that, in that scenario, it ‘[…] would seek
          alternative licensing opportunities with other broadcasters, streamers or audiovisual
          platforms, but such alternatives may not always be equivalent in terms of reach,
          commercial value, windowing or strategic fit’. (555) Some market participants have also
          stated that such a hypothetical situation would not affect their business, as they can
          license their content to alternative streaming platforms. (556)

6.5.4.3. Conclusion
(531)     For the reasons described above, the Commission considers that the merged entity would
          not have the ability to foreclose downstream SVOD rivals nor foreclose upstream
          suppliers’ access to SVOD distribution as it does not have a significant degree of market
          power.

6.6.      Conglomerate effects

6.6.1.    Legal framework
(532)     The Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines acknowledge that, whereas in the majority of
          circumstances conglomerate mergers will not lead to competition concerns, in certain
          scenarios they may significantly impede effective competition. In particular, the main
          concern in the context of conglomerate mergers is that of foreclosure. (557)
(533)     According to the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, the combination of products in
          related markets may confer on the merged entity the ability and incentive to leverage a

(550)    Non-Horizontal Guidelines, paragraphs 93-118.

         services in Poland, where the combined share of [30-40]% narrowly exceeds the 30% threshold. However,
         this does not lead to a vertically affected market as Paramount has no non-captive spend in Poland, so there
         is no merger-specific increment.
(552)    RBB Economics, ‘Economic assessment of potential vertical effects in the licensing of AV content to SVOD
         services,’ 8 May 2026, page 10.

(555)    Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q2 Audiovisual value chain’, question D.F.10.
(556)    Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q2 Audiovisual value chain’, question D.F.10.
(557)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraphs18 and 93.
                                                            108
          strong market position from one market to another by means of tying or bundling or other
          exclusionary practices. In certain circumstances, these practices may lead to a reduction
          in actual or potential rivals’ ability or incentive to compete. This may reduce the
          competitive pressure on the merged entity allowing it to increase prices. (558)
(534)     In assessing the likelihood of such a scenario, the Commission examines, first, whether
          the merged firm would have the ability to foreclose its rivals, second, whether it would
          have the economic incentive to do so and, third, whether a foreclosure strategy would
          have a significant detrimental effect on competition, thus causing harm to consumers. In
          practice, these factors are often examined together as they are closely intertwined. (559)

6.6.2.    Overview of new conglomerate relationships between related markets, where the Parties
          have a market share of 30% or more
(535)     A number of conglomerate relationships between neighbouring markets can be found in
          several national markets.
(536)     However, the vast majority of such conglomerate links are pre-existing. (560) Non-
          preexisting links only arise in relation to the market for the wholesale supply of TV
          channels, among different market segments. (561)
(537)     The Commission notes that in the market for the wholesale supply of TV channels, it is
          a common market practice that negotiations between wholesale suppliers of TV channels
          and retail TV distributors/MVPDs are conducted on a portfolio basis (including multiple
          TV channels of different genres). In fact, the majority of respondents of the market
          investigation indicated that ‘wholesale negotiations are typically conducted on a
          portfolio basis’. (562) Additionally, in the market investigation, market participants raised
          concerns in relation to the non-preexisting links. (563)
(538)     Consequently, the Commission will focus its assessment on the markets which have non-
          preexisting links and for which concerns were raised:
             i.    The wholesale supply of pay TV sport channels and the neighbouring segment
                   for wholesale supply of FTA TV general entertainment channels in Czechia.
            ii.    The wholesale supply of pay TV film channels and the neighbouring segment for
                   wholesale supply of FTA TV children’s channels in Germany.
           iii.    The wholesale supply of FTA TV sport channels and the neighbouring segment
                   for wholesale supply of FTA TV children’s channels in Germany.
            iv.    The wholesale supply of FTA TV children’s channels and the neighbouring
                   segment for wholesale supply of pay TV children’s channels in Spain.

6.6.3.    Conglomerate relationship between the wholesale supply of pay TV sport channels and
          the neighbouring segment for wholesale supply of FTA TV general entertainment
          channels in Czechia

6.6.3.1. The Notifying Party’s views
(539)     The Notifying Party submits that it lacks the ability or incentive to engage in a foreclosure
          strategy for the following reasons.


(558)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 93.
(559)    Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 94.
(560)    A conglomerate link is preexisting if one of the Parties was active in the conglomerate linked market pre-
         merger. Commission decision of 22 April 2026 in case M.12076 – RTL/SKY DACH, footnote 869
         (publication pending).
(561)    Form CO, paragraph 1380 and Annex 8.3.
(562)    Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q2 Audiovisual value chain’, question C.A.C.10.
(563)    Replies to Questionnaire ‘Q2 Audiovisual value chain’, questions C.A.C.12.1, E.1.1 and E.2.
                                                           109
(540)    First, the Parties lack market power in the supply of pay TV sports channels in Czechia,
         given modest shares and strong competitors. Warner’s market share exceeds the 30%
         threshold narrowly in the segment for pay TV sport channels in Czechia ([30-40]%).
         Warner’s sports channels are not ‘must-haves’ for MVPDs in Czechia, as competitors
         have major sports events: Atresmedia has UEFA Champions League and Formula 1,
         Oneplay has the domestic football league, Canal+ has the FA Premier League, AMC
         Networks has Serie A and Česká Televize has major ice hockey and national team events.
         Post-Transaction, the Parties will continue to be constrained by players such as
         Atresmedia ([10-20]%), Canal+ ([10-20]%), Sky ([10-20]%), AMC Networks ([5-10]%),
         Foxtel ([5-10]%), and others ([5-10]%) and by a range of SVOD operators and sports-
         specific OTT services such as DAZN, Amazon Prime Video, and YouTube. (564)
(541)    Second, MVPDs’ bargaining power will remain undiminished post-Transaction, as they
         are large TV providers with substantial customer bases. (565) When they drop a channel,
         replacements may include traditional FTA TV channels, FAST channels or multi-genre
         SVOD services. (566) Additionally, MVPDs typically prefer to negotiate for a single
         bouquet of channels across genres. Therefore, segment market shares by genre for Pay
         TV channels are not indicative of market power. (567)
(542)    Third, the Parties also lack any incentive to engage in a foreclosure strategy, since
         MVPDs will react by dropping the Parties’ TV channels and by shifting to alternative
         content options from linear or non-linear competitors. Additionally, as the Parties do not
         offer pay TV channels at a retail level, they would not have a platform to which they
         could drive the dropped linear TV channels. (568)
(543)    Fourth, a potential foreclosure strategy would have no significant detrimental effect on
         competition because bundling is a common practice in the industry. Therefore, customers
         will be able to rely on alternative packaged deals from other international and local
         suppliers and could develop counterstrategies such as creating or commissioning content
         for their own use (as a number of market players have done over the last few years). (569)

6.6.3.2. Commission’s assessment
(544)    First, the Commission considers that the merged entity will not have the ability to
         foreclose competing wholesale suppliers of TV channels by leveraging its market power
         in the segment for wholesale supply of pay TV sport channels to strengthen its position
         in the neighbouring segment for wholesale supply of FTA TV general entertainment
         channels in Czechia. According to the Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, for
         foreclosure to be a concern, the merged entity must have a significant degree of market
         power in one of the markets concerned. (570) The Commission considers that the merged
         entity does not have a significant degree of market power in the market for the wholesale
         supply of pay TV sport channels in Czechia.
(545)    In the first place, the Commission notes that the Transaction gives rise to a combined
         market share of [30-40]% in the segment for wholesale supply of pay TV sport channels
         in Czechia, which only exceeds 30% by a small margin. Warner has a [30-40]% market
         share and Paramount is not active in this segment. (571)

(565)   Form CO, paragraphs 993-994.
(566)   Form CO, paragraph 1409.
(567)   Form CO, paragraph 1421.
(568)   Form CO, paragraphs 1435-1436.
(569)   Form CO, paragraphs 1437-1438.
(570)   Non-Horizontal Merger Guidelines, paragraph 99.
(571)   Section 6.3.3.
                                                          110
(546)    In the second place, the Parties are constrained by the presence of various competitors.
         In particular, Atresmedia has a market share of [10-20]% and the rights to major sports
         events such as UEFA Champions League and Formula 1; Canal+ has a market share of
         [10-20]% and the rights to major sports events such as the FA Premier League; Sky has
         a market share of [10-20]%; AMC Networks has a market share of [5-10]% and the rights
         to major sports events such as Serie A; Foxtel with a market share of [5-10]%; and others
         have the remaining market share of [5-10]% (e.g., Oneplay has the rights to the domestic
         football league and Česká Televize has the rights to major ice hockey and national team
         events). (572)
(547)    In the third place, there is competitive pressure from SVOD services. (573) According to
         the majority of respondents to the market investigation, retail TV distributors and
         MVPDs negotiate simultaneously for access to linear TV channels and non-linear SVOD
         services. (574) This indicates that a strong position in SVOD services can create
         significant competitive pressure during those negotiations. Market respondents
         (i.e., competitors and vertically integrated entertainment companies) active in Czechia
         explained the importance of acquiring both non-linear and linear services: ‘we always try
         and most of the time succeed in acquiring linear and non-linear rights since our
         subscribers want to view the content in a linear and non-linear format’ and ‘distributors
         typically seek a range of ways of delivering content to their customers, which includes
         linear and on-demand services (whether catch up or a separate SVOD
         environment)’. (575)
(548)    Additionally, several market participants indicated that SVOD is ‘a true alternative to
         TV channels’ and that they ‘consider SVOD services an alternative to tv-channels in
         general. So do the distributors, which is why there is a very systematic and structural
         change happening where the offering from distributors/MVPD is more and more based
         on SVOD services and not only on tv-channels’. (576)
(549)    In the fourth place, MVPDs seeking to acquire TV channels are able to switch between
         different wholesale suppliers of TV channels. MVPDs may replace channels with
         alternative TV channels from the same genre or different genres. (577) In fact, the majority
         of respondents of the market investigation indicated that the acquisition decision of TV
         channels is based on a multi-genre approach, rather than on the genre of a particular TV
         channel. (578) In particular, a market participant active in the Czech market indicated that
         ‘[s]topping a type of channels would not necessarily entail [the respondent] to replace
         them with channels of the exact same genre. The main objective is to build attractive,
         rich and diverse offers that reply to prospect customers and our subscribers needs and
         expectations’. (579)
(550)    In the fifth place, the Commission notes the argument of the Parties that MVPDs hold
         significant bargaining power, as they have the ability to drop a channel and replace it
         with alternatives, substantial customer basis and developed packaging capabilities. (580)
         Under this assumption, the merged entity would not have the ability to force bundles on

          MVPDs, as enforcing bundles could lead to a risk of switching to other alternatives or
          remove the channels altogether. (581)
(551)     Second, most market participants active in Czechia that have expressed concerns in the
          market investigation indicate that the Transaction may cause ‘bundling pressure’, that
          ‘content providers typically do not permit the unbundling / separation of channel
          offerings, such that distributors are required to carry the full suite of channels, even
          where these are not valued equally’ and that ‘price can go up due to bundling of content’.
          Nonetheless, responses are nuanced, as one of the participants explained that ‘this
          dynamic is evolving in light of the growth of streaming services. For example, [the
          respondent] has observed in some instances that where it has committed to carrying a
          content provider’s SVOD service, the provider may adopt a more flexible approach in
          relation to the associated linear channel offerings’. (582) The Commission considers that
          such concerns are linked to an increase in market power. However, as indicated above,
          the Commission considers that the merged entity does not have a significant degree of
          market power in the market for the wholesale supply of pay TV sport channels in
          Czechia.
(552)     Third, given that the conditions under the Non-Horizontal Guidelines are
          cumulative, (583) the Commission does not need to take a position on whether the two
          other conditions are satisfied.

6.6.3.3. Conclusion
(553)     For all the above reasons, the Commission concludes that the Transaction will not raise
          serious doubts as to its compatibility with the internal market as a result of a conglomerate
          foreclosure strategy in the market for wholesale supply of pay TV sport channels and the
          neighbouring segment for wholesale supply of FTA TV general entertainment channels
          in Czechia, considering the merged entity would not have the ability to engage in such a
          strategy.

6.6.4.    Conglomerate relationship between the wholesale supply of pay TV film channels and
          the neighbouring segment for wholesale supply of FTA TV children’s channels in
          Germany

6.6.4.1. The Notifying Party’s views
(554)     The Notifying Party submits that it lacks the ability or incentive to engage in a foreclosure
          strategy for the following reasons.
(555)     First, the Parties lack the market power in the supply of pay TV film channels in
          Germany, given Warner’s market share and strong competition. Warner’s market share
          exceeds the 30% threshold marginally in the segment for pay TV film channels in
          Germany ([30-40]%). Post-Transaction, the merged entity will face significant
          competition from alternative pay TV film channel providers, such as market leader Sky
          ([60-70]%) with five channels (i.e., Sky Cinema Highlights, Sky Cinema Family, Sky
          Cinema Action, Sky Cinema Premiere and Sky Cinema Classics), AMC ([0-5]%) with
          Kinowelt TV, and Anixe+. (584)
(556)     Second, Warner’s pay TV film channels face competition from SVOD services and
          cannot be considered must-haves for downstream distributors. SVOD operators compete
          to acquire Pay-1 window rights and the Pay-1 window most often involves SVOD

         monetized. (585)
(557)    Third, MVPDs’ bargaining power will remain undiminished post-Transaction, as they
         are large TV providers with substantial customer bases. (586) When they drop a channel,
         replacements may include traditional FTA TV channels, FAST channels or multi-genre
         SVOD services. (587) Additionally, MVPDs typically prefer to negotiate for a single
         bouquet of channels across genres. Therefore, segment market shares by genre for Pay

(558)    Fourth, the Parties also lack any incentive to engage in a foreclosure strategy, since
